☸️ Cloud Native Platform Engineering

Complete CNPE Certification Guide — From Novice to Certified Platform Engineer

Master platform engineering step-by-step using real examples with the anihpj Django project and jobpost app

🟢 Beginner Friendly ☸️ Kubernetes 🔧 Platform Engineering 🐍 Django 5.2.5 🐳 Docker 📚 36 Chapters ⏱️ ~60 Hours 🎯 5 CNPE Domains

🚀 Your Cloud Native Platform Engineering Journey

🎯 Welcome! This guide takes you from complete beginner to certified Cloud Native Platform Engineer. Every concept is explained with real examples using the anihpj/jobpost Django project. You'll learn to build, operate, and evolve Internal Developer Platforms (IDPs) that make developers 10x more productive.

Chapter 1: What is Cloud Native Platform Engineering?

Before diving into tools and configs, let's understand why Platform Engineering exists, what problems it solves, and what it means to be a Cloud Native Platform Engineer (CNPE). We'll use our anihpj/jobpost Django app to make every concept concrete.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~2 hours 📖 Concepts 🎯 Foundation

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Why "DevOps" alone isn't enough — the cognitive load problem for developers
  • ✅ What Platform Engineering is, and how it differs from DevOps, SRE, and traditional Ops
  • ✅ The evolution from bare metal → VMs → containers → K8s → Internal Developer Platforms
  • ✅ The CNCF landscape and how to navigate 1000+ cloud native tools
  • ✅ Key terminology every Platform Engineer must know
  • ✅ Your role as the bridge between Dev, Ops, and Security
  • ✅ Real-world platform engineering examples from Spotify, Netflix, and others
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🏗️ Platform Architecture & Infrastructure 15% Foundation ALL DOMAINS
💡 Understanding "Why" Matters on the CNPE Exam

The CNPE is a performance-based exam (2 hours, hands-on tasks). While Chapter 1 is conceptual, understanding why we build platforms helps you make the right design decisions. Key concepts to internalize:

  • Cognitive Load — Developers shouldn't need to know 12 different tools to deploy code. Your platform reduces this to "push and done."
  • Platform as a Product — Treat your platform like a product with real customers (developers). Measure adoption, satisfaction, and time-to-deploy.
  • Golden Paths — Paved, supported, automated workflows. The CNPE exam tests your ability to design and implement these self-service paths.

1.1 The Problem Before Platform Engineering

Imagine you're a developer on the anihpj team. You've built a Django app with a jobpost module that lists jobs. It works on your laptop. Now you need to deploy it so real users can access it. What do you need?

😰 What a Developer Needs to Deploy anihpj WITHOUT a Platform
  • 🔴 A server (or VM) — file a ticket, wait 2 weeks for IT to provision it
  • 🔴 Install Python, Django, gunicorn, nginx — hope versions match your laptop
  • 🔴 Configure a database (PostgreSQL) — more tickets for DBAs
  • 🔴 Set up networking — DNS, load balancer, firewall rules
  • 🔴 Configure HTTPS/TLS certificates — don't let them expire!
  • 🔴 Set up monitoring — otherwise you won't know if it's down at 3 AM
  • 🔴 Set up logging — so you can debug when something breaks
  • 🔴 Configure backups — because databases fail
  • 🔴 Set up CI/CD — so you don't deploy manually every time
  • 🔴 Document everything — so the next developer doesn't start from scratch
🎉 What a Developer Needs to Deploy anihpj WITH a Platform
  • ✅ Push code to GitHub — the platform does the rest
  • ✅ CI pipeline builds, tests, and packages your app automatically
  • ✅ ArgoCD deploys it to Kubernetes with zero-downtime
  • ✅ TLS certificates auto-provisioned and auto-renewed
  • ✅ Monitoring dashboards, alerts, and logs available immediately
  • ✅ Database provisioned automatically with backups configured
  • ✅ Preview environment created for every Pull Request

The Cognitive Load Problem — Visualized

Without a Platform vs With a Platform
  ❌ WITHOUT A PLATFORM — Developer Must Know 12+ Domains
  
  ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐
  │ K8s  │ │Docker│ │Helm  │ │Terra-│ │Net-  │ │Moni- │
  │ YAML │ │Image │ │Chart │ │form  │ │work  │ │toring│
  └──────┘ └──────┘ └──────┘ └──────┘ └──────┘ └──────┘
  ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐
  │Secrets│ │CI/CD │ │RBAC  │ │Ingrs │ │DNS   │ │Cost  │
  └──────┘ └──────┘ └──────┘ └──────┘ └──────┘ └──────┘

  ✅ WITH A PLATFORM — Developer Only Needs to Know:
  
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │         git push → PLATFORM DOES THE REST        │
  │  ☸️ K8s  │ 🐳 Docker │ 📦 Helm │ 🔧 Terraform   │
  │  🔐 TLS │ 📊 Monitoring │ 🔄 CI/CD │ 🛡️ RBAC   │
  │         ALL AUTOMATED BY THE PLATFORM           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
📖 Key Insight: Cognitive Load

Cognitive load is the mental effort required to complete a task. In software, it's the number of tools, concepts, and processes a developer must understand to ship code. Platform Engineering reduces cognitive load by abstracting infrastructure complexity behind simple, self-service interfaces. A developer should deploy an app in 5 minutes — not 2 weeks.

1.2 Platform Engineering Defined

Platform Engineering is the discipline of building and maintaining the internal tools, services, and workflows that enable development teams to deliver software rapidly, safely, and efficiently — without needing deep infrastructure expertise.

Platform Engineering — The Elevator Pitch

  Platform Engineering = 
  
  ┌─────────────────┐   ┌─────────────────┐   ┌─────────────────┐
  │  Infrastructure │ + │   Developer     │ + │   Automation    │
  │  as Code (IaC)  │   │  Experience (DX)│   │  & GitOps      │
  └─────────────────┘   └─────────────────┘   └─────────────────┘
  
  = Building the INTERNAL DEVELOPER PLATFORM (IDP)
    that makes deploying anihpj as easy as pushing code

What Platform Engineering IS vs What It ISN'T

✅ Platform Engineering IS:

🏗️Building self-service tools that devs love
📐Designing golden paths for common workflows
👥Treating the platform as a product with real users
📊Measuring developer experience and adoption
🔄Automating repetitive operational tasks
🛡️Embedding security by default in every path
🔌Building platform APIs for self-service

❌ Platform Engineering ISN'T:

🚫Just "DevOps renamed" with fancier tools
🚫Only managing Kubernetes clusters
🚫A single team doing all the ops work
🚫Building without developer feedback
🚫Just writing YAML files all day
🚫Throwing tools at developers and walking away
🚫Centralized ticket-based infrastructure requests

1.3 The Evolution to Platform Engineering

Platform Engineering didn't appear overnight. It's the latest stage in a 20+ year evolution of how we build and operate software. Understanding this journey helps you see why the CNPE role exists.

2000-2010
🖥️
SysAdmin Era

Manual SSH, bash scripts, physical servers. "File a ticket, wait 2 weeks." No automation.

2010-2015
⚙️
DevOps Era

CI/CD pipelines, config management (Puppet, Ansible), "You build it, you run it." Better but still complex.

2015-2020
📊
SRE Era

Error budgets, SLOs/SLIs, incident management, reliability engineering. Data-driven operations.

2020-2024
🏗️
Platform Engineering

IDPs, golden paths, self-service, Backstage, developer portals. YOU ARE HERE.

2025+
🤖
AI-Augmented Platforms

AI-assisted operations, self-healing platforms, intelligent developer portals. THE FUTURE.

💡 CNPE Career Insight

Platform Engineering is one of the fastest-growing roles in tech. Gartner predicts 80% of large organizations will have a platform engineering team by 2026. The CNPE certification positions you at the forefront of this transformation.

1.4 The CNCF Landscape — Your Toolbox

The Cloud Native Computing Foundation (CNCF) hosts over 180 projects that form the ecosystem you'll work with as a Platform Engineer. Understanding this landscape is essential — it's your toolbox.

CNCF Ecosystem — The Platform Engineer's Toolbox

                         ┌─────────────────┐
                         │    Backstage     │
                         │   Dev Portal     │
                         └────────┬────────┘
                ┌─────────┐       │       ┌──────────┐
                │ ArgoCD  │       │       │Crossplane│
                │ GitOps  │       │       │   IaC    │
                └────┬────┘       │       └────┬─────┘
                     │     ┌──────┴──────┐     │
                     └────▶│  ☸️ K8s     │◀────┘
                  ┌───────▶│  Platform   │◀────────┐
                  │        └──────┬──────┘         │
              ┌───┴────┐         │          ┌──────┴─────┐
              │  Helm  │         │          │  Kyverno   │
              │Package │         │          │  Policies  │
              └────────┘         │          └────────────┘
                      ┌──────────┴──────────┐
                      │                     │
                 ┌────┴─────┐         ┌─────┴────┐
                 │Prometheus│         │OpenTele- │
                 │ Metrics  │         │metry     │
                 └──────────┘         └──────────┘

The CNCF landscape is organized into categories. As a Platform Engineer, you'll focus on:

CategoryKey ProjectsCNPE Relevance
OrchestrationKubernetes🏗️ Domain 1 — Core of your platform
GitOps / CDArgoCD, Flux🔄 Domain 2 (25%) — Critical exam topic
Platform APIsCrossplane, Backstage🔌 Domain 3 (25%) — Build your IDP
ObservabilityPrometheus, Grafana, Loki, Tempo, OpenTelemetry📊 Domain 4 (20%) — See everything
SecurityKyverno, OPA, cert-manager, Falco🔐 Domain 5 (15%) — Lock it down
Service MeshIstio, Linkerd🏗️ Advanced networking
Package MgmtHelm, Kustomize🔌 Platform packaging

1.5 Key Terminology for Platform Engineers

Before diving deeper, let's establish the vocabulary you'll use every day as a Platform Engineer.

TermDefinitionExample (anihpj)
IDPInternal Developer Platform — the self-service layer devs interact withBackstage portal where devs click "Deploy Django App"
Golden PathA sanctioned, supported, automated workflow for common tasks"Add a database" → Crossplane provisions RDS, creates Secret, configures backup
GitOpsGit is the single source of truth; an agent pulls desired state and applies itArgoCD watches anihpj Git repo → syncs to K8s cluster
IaCInfrastructure as Code — define infra in version-controlled filesTofu/Terraform creates EKS cluster, VPC, IAM roles
Shift-LeftMoving security/testing earlier in the development lifecycleScan Docker images for CVEs BEFORE they reach production
Self-ServiceDevelopers can provision resources without filing ticketsDev runs kubectl apply -f postgres-claim.yaml — DB created automatically
DXDeveloper Experience — how easy and pleasant your platform is to useMeasured via surveys, time-to-deploy, adoption rate
Cognitive LoadMental burden of complex infrastructure on developersReduced from 12 tools to "git push"

1.6 Your Role as a Cloud Native Platform Engineer

As a CNPE, you wear multiple hats. You're not just a "Kubernetes admin" — you're the bridge between development, operations, and security.

The Platform Engineer's Responsibilities — BUILD | OPERATE | MEASURE

                    PLATFORM ENGINEER
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                        │
  │  ┌───────────────┐  ┌───────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐│
  │  │   🏗️ BUILD    │  │   🔧 OPERATE  │  │  📊 MEASURE ││
  │  │               │  │               │  │             ││
  │  │ • K8s clusters│  │ • Monitoring  │  │ • DORA      ││
  │  │ • CI/CD pipes │  │ • Alerting    │  │   metrics   ││
  │  │ • IaC modules │  │ • Upgrades    │  │ • Adoption  ││
  │  │ • Dev portal  │  │ • Backup/Rest │  │ • Dev NPS   ││
  │  │ • Templates   │  │ • Security    │  │ • MTTR      ││
  │  └───────────────┘  └───────────────┘  └─────────────┘│
  │                                                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │           👥 ENABLE DEVELOPERS                   │  │
  │  │  Self-service, documentation, support, training  │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🏗️
Platform Architect

Design the Internal Developer Platform. Choose tools (ArgoCD, Crossplane, Backstage). Define golden paths and platform APIs. Make architectural decisions that scale.

🔧
Platform Builder

Implement automation and self-service. Write Infrastructure as Code. Build CI/CD pipelines. Create Helm charts and Kustomize overlays. Automate everything.

📊
Platform Operator

Maintain reliability of the platform itself. Set SLOs. Monitor with Prometheus/Grafana. Respond to incidents. Manage upgrades. Backup etcd. Tune performance.

👥
Platform Evangelist

Drive adoption of the platform. Gather developer feedback. Measure satisfaction (NPS). Document golden paths. Train teams. The platform is a PRODUCT — you're the product manager.

📖 The 4 Hats of a CNPE

On Monday you might be architecting a new golden path. On Tuesday, building a Crossplane composition. On Wednesday, troubleshooting a production incident. On Thursday, presenting the platform roadmap to developers. Versatility is your superpower.

The Cloud Native Platform Stack — What You Build as a CNPE

5 Layers — From Infrastructure to Developer Experience
  LAYER 5: DEVELOPER EXPERIENCE (Devs only touch this!)

1.7 Real-World Platform Engineering Examples

Platform Engineering isn't theoretical — companies of all sizes are adopting it. Here's how the leaders do it:

🎵
Spotify

Created Backstage (now CNCF incubating) to solve developer confusion across 150+ microservices. Their internal developer portal became the industry standard.

📺
Netflix

Built platform tooling (Spinnaker, Chaos Monkey) that enables developers to deploy thousands of times per day across global infrastructure.

🏠
Airbnb

Adopted platform engineering to reduce cognitive load. Their IDP lets developers deploy services with a single command, reducing time-to-production by 80%.

🚀
Your Team (anihpj)

Even a small team benefits! With an IDP, deploying jobpost takes minutes instead of days. Every PR gets a preview environment automatically.

💡 Platform Engineering for a 5-Person Startup — The anihpj Mini-Platform Journey

You don't need a 50-person platform team. Here's how a small team can adopt platform engineering with the anihpj project:

PhaseWhat You BuildTime InvestmentBenefit
Week 1Docker Compose for local dev2 hoursEveryone runs the same environment
Week 2GitHub Actions CI pipeline3 hoursAutomated tests on every push
Week 3K8s cluster (EKS/GKE) + ArgoCD4 hoursGitOps deployment to staging

Result: In ~20 hours of work, you've gone from "SSH into a server and run Django manually" to "git push → automated deploy → monitored in production." That's Platform Engineering, even at startup scale.

📝 Chapter 1 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What is the PRIMARY goal of Platform Engineering? Choose the BEST answer.
Show Answer
Answer

Reducing cognitive load on developers by providing self-service tools, golden paths, and an Internal Developer Platform (IDP) that abstracts infrastructure complexity.

Explanation

While Platform Engineering involves automation, K8s management, and CI/CD, the primary goal is reducing cognitive load. Developers should deploy code without needing to understand 12 different infrastructure tools. The IDP makes the right thing the easy thing.

Q2
How does Platform Engineering differ from DevOps? Explain in 2-3 sentences.
Show Answer
Answer

DevOps is a cultural philosophy ("you build it, you run it") that broke down silos between dev and ops. Platform Engineering is the practical implementation — building internal tools, golden paths, and self-service platforms that make DevOps principles scalable across large organizations. DevOps is the why; Platform Engineering is the how.

Explanation

Think of it as: DevOps says "developers should own their infrastructure." Platform Engineering says "we'll build the platform so developers CAN own their infrastructure without being infrastructure experts."

Q3
What is an Internal Developer Platform (IDP)? List 3 key components.
Show Answer
Answer

An IDP is the set of tools, services, and workflows that enables developers to deploy, run, and manage applications without deep infrastructure knowledge. Key components:

1. Developer Portal (Backstage) — unified UI for all tools and services

2. GitOps Engine (ArgoCD) — automated deployments from Git

3. Platform APIs (Crossplane) — self-service provisioning of databases, buckets, etc.

Explanation

The IDP is what your developers interact with. It's the "product" you build as a Platform Engineer. A good IDP makes deploying anihpj as easy as git push.

Q4
What is a "Golden Path"? Why are golden paths important for Platform Engineering?
Show Answer
Answer

A Golden Path is a sanctioned, supported, and automated workflow for a common development task (e.g., "deploy a new microservice"). Golden paths are important because they:

• Make the right thing easy — developers follow the supported path naturally

• Reduce cognitive load — the path handles infra complexity automatically

• Embed best practices — security, monitoring, and compliance are built into the path

Q5
What role does the CNCF (Cloud Native Computing Foundation) play in Platform Engineering?
Show Answer
Answer

The CNCF hosts and governs the open-source projects that form the cloud native ecosystem — including Kubernetes, ArgoCD, Crossplane, Backstage, Prometheus, Kyverno, Helm, and 180+ others. As a CNPE, you'll use CNCF projects as the building blocks of your platform.

Explanation

The CNCF is to cloud native what the Linux Foundation is to open source — the neutral home where projects grow. Understanding the CNCF landscape helps you choose the right tools for your platform.

Q6
A developer on the anihpj team says: "I just want to deploy my code. I don't want to learn Kubernetes." How would you, as a Platform Engineer, address this?
Show Answer
Answer

This is EXACTLY the problem Platform Engineering solves. I would:

1. Provide a Golden Path: "Push to the main branch → ArgoCD auto-deploys to staging."

2. Create a Self-Service Template in Backstage: "Click 'Deploy Django App' → fill in name → done."

3. Ensure the platform handles everything else: K8s manifests, TLS, monitoring, logging, backups.

The developer never needs to write a Kubernetes YAML file.

Q7
What are the 5 official CNPE exam domains and their weights?
Show Answer
Answer
DomainWeight
🏗️ Platform Architecture & Infrastructure15%
🔄 GitOps & Continuous Delivery25%
🔌 Platform APIs & Self-Service Capabilities25%
📊 Observability & Operations20%
🔐 Security & Policy Enforcement15%
Explanation

GitOps/CD and Platform APIs/Self-Service are the heaviest domains at 25% each — together they make up half the exam. Focus your study here first.

Q8
Explain the concept of "Platform as a Product." Why is this mindset important?
Show Answer
Answer

"Platform as a Product" means treating your Internal Developer Platform like a commercial product with real customers (developers). This means:

• Discover: Talk to developers, understand their pain points

• Build: Create an MVP that solves the biggest pain point

• Measure: Track adoption, NPS, time-to-deploy, ticket reduction

• Iterate: Improve based on feedback, not assumptions

Without this mindset, platform teams build things developers don't want or need.

Q9
What does the term "cognitive load" mean in the context of Platform Engineering? Give an example from the anihpj project.
Show Answer
Answer

Cognitive load is the mental effort a developer must expend to understand all the tools, processes, and concepts needed to deploy and run an application.

anihpj example: Without a platform, deploying jobpost requires knowing: Docker, Kubernetes YAML, Helm, Terraform, networking (Ingress/DNS), TLS certificates, secrets management, monitoring setup, logging, CI/CD pipelines, RBAC, and cost management — 12+ domains. With a platform, it's "git push."

Q10
List the evolution stages from traditional operations to modern Platform Engineering, in order. What was the key innovation at each stage?
Show Answer
Answer

1. SysAdmin (2000-2010) — Manual operations, physical servers, ticket-driven

2. DevOps (2010-2015) — CI/CD, "you build it you run it," breaking silos

3. SRE (2015-2020) — Error budgets, SLOs, data-driven reliability

4. Platform Engineering (2020+) — IDP, golden paths, self-service, reducing cognitive load

5. AI-Augmented Platforms (2025+) — AI-assisted operations, self-healing platforms

Explanation

Each stage built on the previous one. Platform Engineering combines DevOps culture, SRE reliability practices, and adds the product mindset to create truly self-service developer experiences.

Chapter 2: Kubernetes Foundation for Platform Engineers

Kubernetes is the universal control plane upon which every modern platform is built. As a CNPE, you don't just use K8s — you build your platform ON it. This chapter gives you the K8s foundation you need to design, build, and operate cloud native platforms.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~2 hours ☸️ Core K8s 🏗️ Domain 1

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Why Kubernetes is the undisputed baseline for all cloud native platforms
  • ✅ Control Plane vs Worker Node architecture — from a Platform Engineer's perspective
  • ✅ Core K8s objects: Pods, Deployments, Services, ConfigMaps, Secrets, Namespaces, CRDs
  • ✅ K8s networking: ClusterIP, NodePort, LoadBalancer, Ingress, NetworkPolicy, CNI
  • ✅ K8s storage: PVs, PVCs, StorageClasses, StatefulSets
  • ✅ K8s security fundamentals: RBAC, Pod Security Standards, NetworkPolicies
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🏗️ Platform Architecture & Infrastructure 15%
💡 K8s Architecture — What the CNPE Exam Tests

The exam won't ask you to recite K8s architecture — it tests your ability to use K8s as a platform control plane. Key focal areas:

  • Reconciliation Loop — Understand Observe→Diff→Act. This is the pattern ALL platform tools follow (ArgoCD, Crossplane, Kyverno).
  • API Server as Single Entry Point — Every platform tool (Backstage, ArgoCD) interacts through the API server. Know how auth works.
  • Namespaces as Boundaries — Use them for multi-tenancy. But remember: namespaces DON'T provide network isolation by default!
  • CRDs Extend Everything — ArgoCD Applications, Crossplane Compositions, Kyverno Policies — ALL are CRDs. Understand the pattern.

2.1 Why Kubernetes is the Platform Baseline

Kubernetes has become the "operating system of the cloud" — a standard, portable API that runs the same way on AWS EKS, GCP GKE, Azure AKS, or on-premises. Every major CNCF tool is designed to run on K8s. Your platform IS a set of K8s-native tools.

Kubernetes as the Platform Foundation

                    YOUR PLATFORM (IDP)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Backstage (Dev Portal) | ArgoCD (GitOps)            │
  │  Crossplane (IaC) | Kyverno (Policy)                 │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Prometheus | Grafana | Loki | Tempo | OpenTelemetry │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Istio/Linkerd (Service Mesh) | cert-manager (TLS)   │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │              ☸️ KUBERNETES API                        │
  │         (The Universal Control Plane)                │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  AWS EKS | GCP GKE | Azure AKS | On-Prem K8s        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
📖 Platform Insight

Kubernetes provides the declarative API that makes Platform Engineering possible. You describe what you want (3 replicas of anihpj), and K8s continuously ensures that's reality. Every platform tool — ArgoCD, Crossplane, Kyverno — builds on this same declarative pattern.

2.2 Kubernetes Architecture (Platform Engineer's View)

Kubernetes has two main parts: the Control Plane (the brain) and Worker Nodes (the muscles). Let's understand each from a Platform Engineer's perspective.

Kubernetes Architecture — Complete View

                    KUBERNETES CLUSTER
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │              🧠 CONTROL PLANE                        │
  │  ┌────────────┐  ┌────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌────────┐
  │  │ API Server │  │  etcd  │  │ Scheduler│  │Ctrl Mgr│
  │  │ 🛡️ Gateway│  │ 💾 DB  │  │ 📍 Place │  │ 🔄 Loop│
  │  └────────────┘  └────────┘  └──────────┘  └────────┘
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                        │
  ┌─────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┐
  │              💪 WORKER NODES                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────┐  ┌──────────────────┐        │
  │  │  Node 1          │  │  Node 2          │        │
  │  │  ┌────────────┐  │  │  ┌────────────┐  │        │
  │  │  │📦 anihpj   │  │  │  │📦 anihpj   │  │        │
  │  │  │📦 jobpost  │  │  │  │📦 jobpost  │  │        │
  │  │  │📦 redis    │  │  │  │📦 postgres │  │        │
  │  │  └────────────┘  │  │  └────────────┘  │        │
  │  └──────────────────┘  └──────────────────┘        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Control Plane Components

Kubernetes Control Plane — Deep Dive for Platform Engineers

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                  kube-apiserver                          │
  │  🛡️ THE GATEKEEPER — All communication goes through it  │
  │  • REST API — kubectl, kubelet, controllers all talk    │
  │    to the API server (NOT to etcd directly!)            │
  │  • Authentication: Who are you? (certs, tokens, OIDC)   │
  │  • Authorization: What can you do? (RBAC)               │
  │  • Admission Control: Is this allowed? (Kyverno, OPA)   │
  │  • Audit Logging: Records every API call                │
  │  💡 Platform Tip: The API server IS your platform API   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
              │
  ┌───────────┼───────────┐
  ▼           ▼           ▼
  ┌──────────┐ ┌────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐
  │  etcd   │ │ Scheduler  │ │ Controller   │
  │ 💾 BRAIN│ │📍 PLACEMENT│ │  Manager     │
  │          │ │            │ │ 🔄 RECONCILE │
  │•All state│ │•Watches for│ │              │
  │•Key-Value│ │ unscheduled│ │•Deployment   │
  │•Raft     │ │ pods       │ │ Controller   │
  │ consensus│ │•Filters by:│ │•ReplicaSet   │
  │•ALWAYS   │ │ resources, │ │ Controller   │
  │ BACKUP!  │ │ affinity,  │ │•Node         │
  │          │ │ taints     │ │ Controller   │
  │          │ │•pod→node   │ │•+20 more     │
  └──────────┘ └────────────┘ └──────────────┘
🛡️
kube-apiserver

The front door. ALL communication goes through it. REST API. Authentication, authorization, admission control. kubectl, kubelet, controllers — everyone talks to the API server, NOT to etcd directly.

💾
etcd

Distributed key-value store. The cluster's single source of truth. All cluster state lives here. Uses Raft consensus. ALWAYS BACKUP ETCD. If etcd is lost without backup, the cluster state is gone.

📍
kube-scheduler

Watches for unscheduled pods. Decides WHICH node to place each pod on. Considers: resource requests, affinity rules, taints/tolerations. If scheduler is down, NEW pods stay Pending — existing pods keep running.

🔄
kube-controller-manager

Runs controller loops (observe → diff → act). Deployment controller ensures replica count. Node controller monitors node health. 20+ controllers run here. The "reconciliation loop" is the heart of K8s.

Worker Node Components

🤖
kubelet

Agent on every node. Receives Pod specs from API server. Ensures containers are running via container runtime. Reports node/pod status back to API server. If kubelet stops, node shows NotReady.

🌐
kube-proxy

Network proxy on every node. Maintains iptables/IPVS rules. Enables Service abstraction — routes traffic to correct pod. Without kube-proxy, Services don't work.

The Reconciliation Loop — K8s Magic

The reconciliation loop is Kubernetes' secret sauce. Controllers continuously watch the current state, compare it to the desired state, and take action to converge them:

👀OBSERVE
→
📐DIFF
→
🔧ACT
→
🔄REPEAT

2.3 Core Kubernetes Objects for Platform Engineers

These are the building blocks you'll use to define your platform and the applications running on it.

CNPE Tools Placement — What Runs Where on Your K8s Platform

  CONTROL PLANE NODE:                WORKER NODES:
  ┌──────────────────────┐          ┌──────────────────────┐
  │ 🛡️ API Server        │          │ 📦 anihpj pods       │
  │ 💾 etcd              │          │ 📦 jobpost pods      │
  │ 📍 Scheduler         │          │ 📦 redis pods        │
  │ 🔄 Controller Mgr    │          │ 📦 postgres pods     │
  │                      │          │                      │
  │ 🛠️ PLATFORM TOOLS:   │          │ 🛠️ PLATFORM TOOLS:   │
  │ ┌──────────────────┐ │          │ ┌──────────────────┐ │
  │ │ ArgoCD Server    │ │          │ │ ArgoCD App Ctrlr │ │
  │ │ Backstage        │ │          │ │ Crossplane Prov. │ │
  │ │ Kyverno Ctrlr    │ │          │ │ Prometheus Node  │ │
  │ │ cert-manager     │ │          │ │   Exporter       │ │
  │ │ Prometheus Svr   │ │          │ │ Grafana Agent    │ │
  │ │ Grafana          │ │          │ │ Loki Promtail    │ │
  │ └──────────────────┘ │          │ │ Istio Sidecars   │ │
  └──────────────────────┘          │ └──────────────────┘ │
                                    └──────────────────────┘

  💡 Platform Insight: Most CNCF tools run as K8s workloads
     themselves — "drinking your own champagne."

Pods & Deployments

Pod: Smallest deployable unit. One or more containers sharing network/storage. Deployment: Manages ReplicaSets, enables rolling updates and rollbacks.

📖 Platform Pattern

Almost NEVER create bare Pods in production. Always use Deployments (or StatefulSets for databases). Deployments give you self-healing, scaling, and zero-downtime updates.

Services & Ingress

Service: Stable network endpoint for a set of pods (pods are ephemeral — IPs change). Ingress: HTTP/HTTPS routing to Services based on host/path.

ConfigMaps & Secrets

ConfigMap: Non-sensitive config (DB host, log level). Secret: Sensitive data (passwords, API keys). Both decouple config from container images — change config without rebuilding.

Namespaces & CRDs

Namespace: Logical partition for multi-tenancy. Custom Resource Definition (CRD): Extend the K8s API with your own resource types. This is how ArgoCD, Crossplane, and Kyverno work — they add CRDs that Kubernetes understands natively.

2.4 Kubernetes Networking Deep Dive

Kubernetes networking follows a simple rule: every Pod gets its own IP, and Pods can communicate with each other across nodes without NAT.

ClusterIP & NodePort

TypeAccessUse Case
ClusterIPInternal only (within cluster)Service-to-service communication
NodePortExternal via NodeIP:PortDevelopment, debugging

LoadBalancer & Ingress

TypeAccessUse Case
LoadBalancerCloud LB → ServiceProduction external access
IngressHTTP routing (host/path)TLS termination, path-based routing

NetworkPolicy & CNI

NetworkPolicy: Firewall rules for pods (default deny, then allow). CNI (Container Network Interface): Plugins that implement pod networking — Calico, Cilium, Flannel. Choose based on your networking requirements.

⚠️ Platform Security

By default, ALL pods can communicate with ALL other pods across namespaces. Always implement NetworkPolicies to segment your platform.

2.5 Kubernetes Storage for Platform Engineers

Storage in K8s is managed through a clean abstraction:

📋PVC
→
📦StorageClass
→
💾PV
→
☁️Cloud Disk

PersistentVolume (PV): Cluster-wide storage resource. PersistentVolumeClaim (PVC): User request for storage. StorageClass: Dynamic provisioning (EBS, Azure Disk). StatefulSet: For stateful apps (databases) needing stable identity + persistent storage.

2.6 Kubernetes Security Fundamentals

🔑
RBAC

Role-Based Access Control. Roles (namespace) + ClusterRoles (cluster-wide) + RoleBindings. Grant least privilege.

🛡️
Pod Security Standards

Privileged, Baseline, Restricted. Enforced via namespace labels. Restricted is best practice.

🌐
NetworkPolicies

Micro-segmentation. Default deny all inbound, then explicitly allow required traffic.

🤖
ServiceAccounts

Identity for pods. Every pod has a ServiceAccount. Control what pods can access via RBAC.

📝 Chapter 2 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
Which component is the single source of truth for all cluster state in Kubernetes? Why is backing it up critical?
Show Answer
Answer

etcd. It stores ALL cluster state — pods, deployments, secrets, RBAC, everything. If etcd is lost without backup, the entire cluster state is unrecoverable.

Explanation

Only the API server communicates with etcd. All other components go through the API server. On the CNPE exam, you may be asked to backup/restore etcd or diagnose etcd issues.

Q2
What is the reconciliation loop? A Deployment specifies 3 replicas. You delete one pod. What happens?
Show Answer
Answer

The ReplicaSet controller detects the discrepancy (2 running vs 3 desired) and creates a new pod. This is the reconciliation loop: Observe → Diff → Act. The controller continuously watches and enforces desired state.

Q3
Name the 4 required fields in every Kubernetes YAML manifest.
Show Answer
Answer
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: anihpj
spec:
  containers:
  - name: app
    image: anihpj:v1

The 4 fields are: apiVersion, kind, metadata, spec.

Q4
What is the difference between a Service of type ClusterIP, NodePort, and LoadBalancer?
Show Answer
Answer

ClusterIP: Internal-only, accessible within cluster. NodePort: Opens a port (30000-32767) on every node. LoadBalancer: Provisions a cloud load balancer that routes external traffic to the service.

Q5
What is a CRD (Custom Resource Definition) and why is it critical for Platform Engineering?
Show Answer
Answer

A CRD extends the Kubernetes API with your own custom resource types. It's critical because every platform tool (ArgoCD, Crossplane, Kyverno, cert-manager) uses CRDs. CRDs allow you to build platform-specific APIs on top of K8s.

Q6
By default, can a pod in namespace "dev" communicate with a pod in namespace "prod"?
Show Answer
Answer

YES. Namespaces provide organizational isolation, NOT network isolation. To enforce network isolation, you must use NetworkPolicies. Cross-namespace communication is via service-name.namespace.svc.cluster.local.

Q7
What is a StorageClass in Kubernetes? How does it enable dynamic provisioning?
Show Answer
Answer

A StorageClass defines a type of storage (e.g., "fast SSD" or "standard HDD") with a provisioner (AWS EBS, GCP PD). When a PVC references a StorageClass, K8s dynamically provisions a PV — no manual PV creation needed.

Q8
What happens if the kube-scheduler goes down? Do existing pods stop running?
Show Answer
Answer

NO — existing pods keep running. Only newly created pods stay in Pending state because there's no scheduler to assign them to a node. The scheduler only handles pod placement; it doesn't manage running pods.

Q9
How does a Service discover which Pods to route traffic to? What happens if labels don't match?
Show Answer
Answer

Services use label selectors to find pods. A Service with selector: app=anihpj routes traffic only to pods with label app=anihpj. If labels don't match, the Service has no endpoints — traffic goes nowhere. Always verify with kubectl get endpoints.

Q10
Why can't other components (scheduler, controller-manager, kubelet) communicate directly with etcd?
Show Answer
Answer

Only the kube-apiserver communicates with etcd. This architecture provides: (1) Security — all access goes through authentication/authorization, (2) Consistency — the API server handles validation and optimistic concurrency, (3) Abstraction — components don't need to know etcd's protocol.

🔑 Chapter 2 Key Takeaways

🎛️Control PlaneAPI server, etcd, scheduler, controller-manager — the brain of K8s
⚙️Worker Nodeskubelet, kube-proxy, container runtime — where pods actually run
🫛Pod = Smallest UnitOne or more containers sharing network/storage namespace
🔄Reconciliation LoopObserve → Diff → Act — the core K8s pattern
📦Namespace IsolationOrganizational, not network. Use NetworkPolicy for isolation.
🔌Service DiscoveryLabel selectors + DNS. Always verify with kubectl get endpoints.

Chapter 3: Containers, Images, and Registries

Containers are the fundamental packaging format for everything that runs on your platform. As a CNPE, you need to understand how to build production-grade images, manage registries, and harden containers for security. We'll use the anihpj Django project throughout.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 🐳 Docker 🏗️ Domain 1

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ The container model: images, layers, registries, and runtimes
  • ✅ How to build production-grade Docker images using multi-stage builds
  • ✅ Container registries: Docker Hub, ECR, GCR, ACR, Harbor
  • ✅ Docker Compose for local development — and when to transition to K8s
  • ✅ 12 container security best practices every Platform Engineer must enforce
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🏗️ Platform Architecture 15% 🔐 Security 15%
💡 Containers — CNPE Exam Focus

Container fundamentals are tested indirectly throughout the exam. You'll need to:

  • Read and write Dockerfiles — Multi-stage builds, layer caching, USER directive, HEALTHCHECK
  • Debug container issues — ImagePullBackOff, CrashLoopBackOff, ErrImagePull are the most common exam scenarios
  • Push/pull from registries — Know how to authenticate to ACR/ECR/GCR and reference images in K8s manifests
  • Security context — runAsNonRoot, readOnlyRootFilesystem, allowPrivilegeEscalation — these appear in policy questions

3.1 Container Fundamentals

Think of a container like a shipping container 🚢 — standardized, stackable, works on any ship/truck/train. In software, a container packages your application with ALL its dependencies, so it runs identically on your laptop, in CI, and in production.

Containers vs Virtual Machines

📦 Containers

✅Share host OS kernel
✅~50 MB disk per image
✅Seconds to start
✅100s per host

💻 Virtual Machines

❌Full OS per VM
❌~10-40 GB disk per VM
❌Minutes to start
❌~10 per host

OCI Standards & Runtimes

The Open Container Initiative (OCI) defines standards for container images and runtimes. Common runtimes: containerd (default in modern K8s), CRI-O (lightweight), gVisor/Kata (sandboxed for security).

Image Layers & Caching

Docker images are built in layers — each instruction in a Dockerfile creates a new layer. Layers are cached: if a layer hasn't changed, Docker reuses it. This is why you COPY requirements.txt BEFORE copying source code — dependencies change less often than code, so they're cached.

3.2 Building Production-Grade Docker Images

Multi-Stage Builds

Multi-stage builds separate the build environment from the runtime environment, resulting in drastically smaller, more secure images.

Dockerfile — anihpj multi-stage build 
# Stage 1: BUILD
FROM python:3.12-slim AS builder
WORKDIR /app
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt
COPY . .
RUN python manage.py collectstatic --noinput

# Stage 2: RUNTIME (smaller, no build tools)
FROM python:3.12-slim
WORKDIR /app
COPY --from=builder /usr/local/lib/python3.12 /usr/local/
COPY --from=builder /app .
USER 1000:1000
EXPOSE 8000
CMD ["gunicorn", "anihpj.wsgi:application", \
     "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000", "--workers", "4"]
💡 Why Multi-Stage?

Stage 1 might be ~1.2 GB with gcc and build tools. Stage 2 is only ~200 MB — 6x smaller. Smaller images = faster pulls, smaller attack surface, less storage cost.

Distroless Images

Distroless images contain ONLY your application and its runtime dependencies — no shell, no package manager, no utilities. This makes them extremely secure (attackers can't get a shell) but harder to debug.

Image Tagging Strategy

Never use :latest in production. Use: Semantic versioning (anihpj:v1.2.3), Git SHA (anihpj:abc1234), or both (anihpj:v1.2.3-abc1234). This gives you reproducibility and traceability.

3.3 Container Registries Deep Dive

A container registry is where you store and distribute container images. Think of it as "PyPI for containers."

RegistryBest ForKey Feature
Docker HubPublic images, small teamsRate limited (100 pulls/6h free)
Amazon ECRAWS workloadsIAM integration, vulnerability scanning
GCP Artifact RegistryGCP workloadsVulnerability scanning, regional
Azure ACRAzure workloadsIntegrated with AKS, geo-replication
HarborEnterprise self-hostedVulnerability scanning, RBAC, replication
GitHub Container RegistryGitHub-native teamsTight GitHub Actions integration

Container Image Lifecycle

  ① Developer pushes code ──▶ ② CI builds image ──▶ ③ Scan (Trivy)
      │                              │                    │
      │                              │           ┌────────┴────────┐
      │                              │        Found?              Clean?
      │                              │           │                  │
      │                              │           ▼                  ▼
      │                              │    ④ Fix & rebuild    ⑤ Sign (cosign)
      │                              │           │                  │
      │                              │           └────────┬─────────┘
      │                              │                    ▼
      │                              │          ⑥ Push to Registry
      │                              │                    │
      │                              │                    ▼
      │                              │          ⑦ ArgoCD syncs to K8s
      └──────────────────────────────┘

3.4 Docker Compose for Local Development

Docker Compose is still valuable for local development. It lets you define multi-container apps (Django + PostgreSQL + Redis) in a single YAML file. Here's the Compose file for anihpj:

version: '3.8'
services:
  web:
    build: .
    ports: ["8000:8000"]
    volumes: [".:/app"]  # Hot reload!
    depends_on: [db, redis]
    environment:
      - DATABASE_URL=postgres://anihpj:secret@db:5432/anihpj
  db:
    image: postgres:16
    volumes: ["pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data"]
    environment:
      - POSTGRES_USER=anihpj
      - POSTGRES_PASSWORD=secret
  redis:
    image: redis:7-alpine
volumes:
  pgdata:
📖 When to Graduate from Compose to K8s

Compose is perfect for local dev. Use K8s when you need: auto-scaling, self-healing, rolling updates, multi-node deployment, GitOps, or production-grade networking. Most teams use Compose locally and K8s for staging/production.

3.5 Container Security Best Practices

#PracticeWhy It Matters
1Use specific tags (no :latest)Prevents unexpected image changes breaking production
2Run as non-root USERLimits blast radius if container is compromised
3Read-only root filesystemPrevents attackers from writing malicious files
4Drop ALL capabilitiesLeast privilege — containers rarely need CAP_SYS_ADMIN
5Use distroless base imagesNo shell = much harder for attackers to exploit
6Scan images (Trivy/Grype)Catch CVEs before they reach production
7Sign images (cosign)Verify image integrity — prevent supply chain attacks
8Generate SBOM (Syft)Know exactly what's in your images (compliance)
9Set resource limitsPrevent one container from starving others
10Use Secrets, not env varsDon't leak credentials in plain text

📝 Chapter 3 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What is the purpose of a multi-stage Docker build? Give a concrete benefit for the anihpj project.
Show Answer
Answer

Multi-stage builds separate the build environment from the runtime environment. Stage 1 compiles, installs dependencies, and collects static files. Stage 2 copies ONLY the artifacts needed to run. For anihpj, this reduces image size from ~1.2 GB to ~200 MB — 6x smaller. Smaller images pull faster and have a smaller attack surface.

Q2
Why should you never use the :latest tag in production?
Show Answer
Answer

:latest is a mutable tag — it can point to a different image tomorrow than it does today. This breaks reproducibility and can cause unexpected deployments. Use semantic versioning (v1.2.3) or Git SHA (abc1234) for immutable, traceable deployments.

Q3
What is a distroless image? What is its main security advantage and its main operational challenge?
Show Answer
Answer

A distroless image contains only the application and its runtime — no shell, no package manager, no utilities. Advantage: Attackers can't get a shell if they compromise the container. Challenge: You can't docker exec into the container to debug — you need external observability (logs, metrics, traces).

Q4
What is the purpose of a .dockerignore file? List 3 things you should exclude for anihpj.
Show Answer
Answer

.dockerignore excludes files from the Docker build context, making builds faster and images smaller. Exclude: __pycache__/, .git/, env/ (virtual environment), db.sqlite3, *.pyc, .env (secrets!).

Q5
What is the difference between Docker Hub, ECR, and Harbor? When would you choose each?
Show Answer
Answer

Docker Hub: Public, free tier with rate limits. Best for open-source or small teams. ECR/GCR/ACR: Cloud-native registries tightly integrated with their respective clouds. Best for teams on that cloud. Harbor: Self-hosted enterprise registry with vulnerability scanning, RBAC, and replication. Best for enterprises needing full control.

Q6
What does the USER 1000:1000 directive do in a Dockerfile? Why is it important?
Show Answer
Answer

It runs the container as a non-root user (user ID 1000) instead of root (UID 0). If an attacker compromises the container, they have limited privileges — they can't install packages or modify system files. This is a critical security practice required by Pod Security Standards.

Q7
What is SBOM? Why is it important for Platform Engineers?
Show Answer
Answer

SBOM (Software Bill of Materials) is a detailed list of every component, library, and dependency in your container image. It's important for: (1) Vulnerability management — know if you're affected by a CVE, (2) Compliance — regulatory requirements, (3) Supply chain security — know exactly what's running. Generate with Syft, attach with cosign.

Q8
When should you use Docker Compose vs Kubernetes for the anihpj project?
Show Answer
Answer

Docker Compose: Local development with hot reload, single machine, simple setup. Kubernetes: Staging/production, multi-node, auto-scaling, self-healing, rolling updates, GitOps, TLS automation, monitoring integration. Most teams use Compose locally and K8s for deployed environments.

Q9
Explain image layer caching. How should you order COPY instructions in a Dockerfile for optimal caching?
Show Answer
Answer

Docker caches each layer. If a layer hasn't changed, it's reused. Order COPY instructions from least frequently changed to most frequently changed: (1) COPY requirements.txt first (rarely changes), (2) RUN pip install (cached if requirements unchanged), (3) COPY source code last (changes most often). This way, dependency installation is cached across builds — only the final COPY runs.

Q10
What is cosign? How does it help prevent supply chain attacks?
Show Answer
Answer

cosign (part of Sigstore) signs container images to prove their origin and integrity. It creates a cryptographic signature that can be verified before deployment. This prevents supply chain attacks where an attacker replaces a legitimate image with a malicious one. Combined with Kyverno admission control, you can require all images to be signed before they run.

🔑 Chapter 3 Key Takeaways

🐳Dockerfile StructureFROM → WORKDIR → COPY → RUN → EXPOSE → USER → CMD
📦Multi-Stage BuildsBuilder stage compiles, runtime stage runs — 60% smaller images
🔒Run as Non-RootUSER 1000 — limits container breakout damage
📋.dockerignoreExclude .git, __pycache__, .env — faster builds, no secret leaks
🏷️Image TagsNever use :latest in production — use semantic versioning
🛡️SBOM + CosignKnow your dependencies, sign your images — supply chain security

Chapter 4: Platform as a Product — Mindset & Design Principles

Building a platform is not just about installing tools — it's about designing an experience that makes developers productive. This chapter teaches you the product mindset that separates successful platform teams from those whose platforms sit unused. We'll design golden paths for the anihpj project.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~2 hours 📐 Design 🔌 Domain 3 (25%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ How to treat your platform as a PRODUCT with real customers (developers)
  • ✅ The Discover → Build → Measure → Iterate lifecycle
  • ✅ How to define your Minimum Viable Platform (MVP)
  • ✅ Golden Paths: designing paved roads for common developer workflows
  • ✅ Self-service infrastructure: from tickets to APIs
  • ✅ Platform APIs and abstraction layers
  • ✅ Key metrics: DORA, adoption, NPS, time-to-deploy
  • ✅ Platform team topologies and Conway's Law
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🔌 Platform APIs & Self-Service 25% 🏗️ Platform Architecture 15%
💡 Platform Design IS on the CNPE Exam

Domain 3 (25%) directly tests your ability to design self-service workflows, platform APIs, and golden paths. You need to understand not just HOW to implement them technically, but WHY certain design choices lead to better developer experiences. Key concepts to internalize:

  • Platform as a Product — Developers are customers. Build what they NEED, not what you think is cool.
  • Golden Paths vs Cow Paths — Paved, supported paths vs. ad-hoc solutions. The exam rewards the paved road approach.
  • Self-Service — The goal is zero-touch. Developers should provision databases without filing a ticket.

4.1 Treating Your Platform as a Product

Your developers are your CUSTOMERS. If they don't use your platform, it doesn't matter how well-engineered it is. This product mindset is the single biggest differentiator between successful platform teams and failed ones.

Platform as a Product — The Continuous Lifecycle

    🔍 DISCOVER         🏗️ BUILD           📊 MEASURE
  ┌─────────────┐   ┌─────────────┐   ┌─────────────┐
  │ Talk to devs│──▶│ MVP platform│──▶│ Track DORA  │
  │ Survey pain  │   │ Golden paths│   │ metrics     │
  │ Watch shadow │   │ Self-service│   │ Dev NPS     │
  │ IT patterns  │   │ Templates   │   │ Adoption %  │
  └─────────────┘   └─────────────┘   └──────┬──────┘
                                             │
                    ◀────────────────────────┘
                    🔄 ITERATE & IMPROVE
👂
Listen First

Survey developers. Watch how they work. What's their biggest pain point? What takes the most time? Build THAT first.

📊
Measure Everything

If you can't measure it, you can't improve it. Track DORA metrics, adoption rate, NPS, time to 10th deploy.

🔄
Iterate Fast

Ship the MVP in weeks, not months. Get feedback. Improve. The platform is never "done" — it's always evolving.

🎯
Default to Yes

Make the right thing the easy thing. Golden paths should be so smooth that devs naturally follow them.

4.2 Minimum Viable Platform (MVP)

Don't build everything at once. Start with the one thing developers struggle with most, and deliver it well. Here's what an MVP platform looks like:

Your First Platform MVP — Start Here

          YOUR FIRST PLATFORM MVP
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                             │
  │  ┌───────────┐  ┌───────────┐  ┌──────────┐│
  │  │ ☸️ K8s    │  │ 🔄 CI/CD  │  │ 📊 Monitor││
  │  │ Cluster   │  │ Pipeline  │  │ + Logs   ││
  │  └───────────┘  └───────────┘  └──────────┘│
  │                                             │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │      📋 Service Catalog / Templates   │  │
  │  │  "kubectl apply -f template.yaml"     │  │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                             │
  │  This is ALL you need to start.            │
  │  Build more ONLY when developers ask.      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────┘
💡 MVP Rule of Thumb

If a developer can push code → automated deploy → see it running with monitoring, you have a platform. Everything else (Backstage, Crossplane, service mesh) is an enhancement you add when the need is proven.

4.3 Golden Paths — Paved Roads for Developers

A Golden Path is a sanctioned, supported, and automated workflow for a common development task. It's the "paved road" that developers follow because it's easier than building their own path (a "cow path").

Golden Paths vs Cow Paths — Why Paved Roads Matter

  🥇 GOLDEN PATH (Paved Road):          🐄 COW PATH (DIY):
  ┌──────────────────────────┐          ┌──────────────────────────┐
  │ Supported & documented   │          │ Ad-hoc, tribal knowledge │
  │ Automated end-to-end     │          │ Manual steps, error-prone│
  │ Security built-in        │          │ Security afterthought    │
  │ Monitoring pre-configured│          │ Monitoring if remembered │
  │ Updatable via Git        │          │ "Bob set it up in 2019"  │
  │ ⏱️ 5 min to deploy       │          │ ⏱️ 2 weeks to figure out  │
  └──────────────────────────┘          └──────────────────────────┘

  💡 Your job as a CNPE: Pave the golden paths so smooth
     that developers NEVER feel the need to create cow paths.

Golden Paths for anihpj — 3 Self-Service Workflows

  🥇 GOLDEN PATH 1: "Deploy a Django Microservice"
  Dev clicks "Create Django Service" in Backstage →
  ✅ GitHub repo (Cookiecutter scaffold)
  ✅ Multi-stage Dockerfile  ✅ CI/CD pipeline
  ✅ ArgoCD auto-sync  ✅ K8s namespace + RBAC
  ✅ Grafana dashboard  ✅ TechDocs
  ⏱️ Time to deploy: < 5 MINUTES

  🥈 GOLDEN PATH 2: "Add a Database"
  Dev: kubectl apply -f postgres-claim.yaml →
  ✅ Cloud RDS/Cloud SQL provisioned (Crossplane)
  ✅ K8s Secret with connection string
  ✅ Automated backups  ✅ Monitoring & alerts

  🥉 GOLDEN PATH 3: "Preview Environment per PR"
  Dev opens PR →
  ✅ Temporary namespace (pr-123-anihpj)
  ✅ Full app deployed (ArgoCD ApplicationSet)
  ✅ Unique URL: pr-123.anihpj.dev
  ✅ Auto-deleted when PR merges

4.4 Self-Service Infrastructure

The ultimate goal: developers provision whatever they need without filing a ticket. This requires platform APIs that abstract away cloud complexity.

Self-Service Infrastructure Flow

  Dev Request          Platform API           Cloud Provision
  ┌──────────┐        ┌──────────────┐        ┌──────────────┐
  │ kubectl  │───────▶│ Crossplane   │───────▶│ ☁️ AWS RDS   │
  │ apply -f │        │ Composition  │        │ ☁️ GCP SQL   │
  │ db.yaml  │        │              │        │ ☁️ Azure DB  │
  └──────────┘        └──────┬───────┘        └──────┬───────┘
                             │                       │
                             ▼                       ▼
                      ┌──────────────┐        ┌──────────────┐
                      │ K8s Secret   │        │ ✅ DB Ready  │
                      │ (connection) │◀───────│ ✅ Backups   │
                      └──────┬───────┘        │ ✅ Monitored │
                             │                └──────────────┘
                             ▼
                      ┌──────────────┐
                      │ anihpj Pod   │
                      │ mounts Secret│
                      │ → Connected! │
                      └──────────────┘
  ⏱️ Total time: ~3 minutes | Zero tickets | Fully automated
🚫
Without Self-Service

Dev files ticket → Ops reviews (2 days) → Ops provisions manually → Dev waits → 2-week cycle.

✅
With Self-Service

Dev runs kubectl apply -f db-claim.yaml → Crossplane provisions RDS → Secret created → Dev uses it in 5 minutes.

4.5 Platform APIs and Abstractions

The platform API layer hides K8s complexity behind simple interfaces. Developers don't need to know about Pods, Deployments, or Services — they just need "Deploy my app" and "Give me a database."

  Dev Interface (Backstage/CLI)
         │
         ▼
  Platform API (Crossplane XRD / CRD)
         │
         ▼
  Cloud Resources (RDS, S3, GKE, AKS)

4.6 Measuring Platform Success

You can't improve what you don't measure. DORA metrics tell you how well your teams deliver software. Platform metrics tell you how well your platform enables them.

DORA Metrics — The 4 Key DevOps Metrics with Performance Levels

                DORA METRICS
  Metric               Elite      High     Medium    Low
  ──────────────────── ────────── ──────── ──────── ──────
  Deployment Freq      On-demand  Daily    Weekly   Monthly
  Lead Time (commit→   < 1 hr    1d-1wk   1wk-1mo  1-6mo
    production)
  MTTR (recovery)      < 1 hr    < 1 day  < 1 wk   > 1 wk
  Change Fail Rate     0-5%      5-10%    10-15%   15%+

  💡 Target ELITE for your platform teams
MetricWhat It MeasuresTarget
Deployment FrequencyHow often you deployDaily or on-demand
Lead Time for ChangesTime from commit to production< 1 hour
MTTRMean Time to Recover from failure< 1 hour
Change Fail Rate% of deploys causing failure< 5%
Platform Adoption% of teams using platform> 80%
Developer NPSWould devs recommend the platform?> 50
Ticket ReductionOps tickets before vs after platform> 70% reduction

"You can't improve what you don't measure." — Peter Drucker. Track DORA metrics for delivery performance and platform-specific metrics for adoption. If a metric isn't moving in the right direction, your platform isn't working.

💡 Beyond DORA — Platform-Specific KPIs

📊 Time to Onboard: New dev → first deploy (target <1h). 📊 Self-Service Rate: % of resources provisioned without tickets. 📊 Platform Adoption: % of teams actively using the platform. 📊 Time to 10th Deploy: How fast devs become proficient. 📊 Cost per Deployment: Track efficiency gains over time.

4.7 Platform Team Topologies

How you organize your platform team matters. The Team Topologies model (by Matthew Skelton and Manuel Pais) defines four fundamental team types:

🏗️
Platform Team

Builds and runs the IDP. Your role as a CNPE. Enables stream-aligned teams to deliver work autonomously.

🚀
Stream-Aligned Team

Aligned to a business domain (e.g., "jobpost team"). These are your CUSTOMERS. They use your platform.

🤝
Enabling Team

Helps stream-aligned teams overcome obstacles. Coaches, not does. Temporary engagement.

🔧
Complicated-Subsystem

Deep specialists (e.g., ML infrastructure). Handles parts too complex for generalist teams.

📖 Conway's Law

"Organizations design systems that mirror their communication structure." If your platform team is isolated from developers, the platform will reflect that isolation. Embed platform engineers with dev teams to build what's actually needed.

📝 Chapter 4 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What does "Platform as a Product" mean? Why is this mindset critical?
Show Answer
Answer

It means treating your Internal Developer Platform like a commercial product with real customers (developers). You apply product management principles: discover needs, build MVP, measure adoption, iterate based on feedback. Without this mindset, platform teams build things developers don't want.

Q2
What is a Golden Path? How does it differ from a "cow path"?
Show Answer
Answer

A Golden Path (or paved road) is a sanctioned, supported, automated workflow. A cow path is an ad-hoc solution developers build themselves because no golden path exists. Golden paths make the right thing easy; cow paths indicate platform gaps.

Q3
What are the 4 DORA metrics? Why do they matter for Platform Engineering?
Show Answer
Answer

Deployment Frequency, Lead Time for Changes, MTTR, Change Fail Rate. They measure software delivery performance. A good platform should improve all four DORA metrics for the teams using it — faster deploys, quicker recovery, fewer failures.

Q4
What is the minimum set of components for a Platform MVP?
Show Answer
Answer

K8s cluster (orchestration) + CI/CD pipeline (automated builds) + monitoring + service catalog/templates. That's it. Don't add Backstage, Crossplane, or service mesh until there's proven need.

Q5
Explain Conway's Law and its relevance to platform team structure.
Show Answer
Answer

Conway's Law: "Organizations design systems that mirror their communication structure." If your platform team works in isolation from developers, the platform will reflect that gap. Platform engineers should be embedded with or work closely alongside dev teams.

Q6
A developer says: "I need a PostgreSQL database for anihpj. How do I get one?" In a self-service platform, what happens?
Show Answer
Answer

The developer runs kubectl apply -f postgres-claim.yaml (or clicks in Backstage). Crossplane provisions a cloud database, creates a K8s Secret with the connection string, configures backups, and sets up monitoring. No ticket. No waiting. 5 minutes.

Q7
What is the difference between "thin wrapper" and "thick" platform approaches?
Show Answer
Answer

Thin wrapper: The platform is a light abstraction over existing tools — devs can still access K8s directly. Thick platform: The platform is the ONLY interface — devs never see K8s. CNPE favors thin-to-medium — don't hide too much, or devs lose understanding and debugging ability.

Q8
What is Developer NPS? How do you measure it?
Show Answer
Answer

Net Promoter Score for your platform. Ask developers: "How likely are you to recommend our platform to a colleague?" (0-10). Promoters (9-10) minus Detractors (0-6) = NPS. Track over time to see if platform improvements are working.

Q9
Name the 4 fundamental team types in Team Topologies and their roles.
Show Answer
Answer

1. Stream-Aligned — aligned to a business domain (your customers). 2. Platform — builds and runs the IDP (you!). 3. Enabling — helps teams overcome obstacles. 4. Complicated-Subsystem — deep specialists for complex areas.

Q10
You're building the anihpj platform. Developers say deployments take 2 weeks. What MVP components would you build first?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Containerize anihpj (Docker), 2. Set up CI pipeline (GitHub Actions), 3. Deploy to K8s with ArgoCD (GitOps), 4. Add monitoring (Prometheus + Grafana). This reduces deploy time from 2 weeks to ~5 minutes. That's your MVP.

🔑 Chapter 4 Key Takeaways

🎯Platform as ProductTreat platform like a commercial product — developers are customers
🛤️Golden PathsPaved, supported workflows. Devs choose: golden path (easy) or DIY (hard).
📏TVP (Thinnest Viable)Start with the minimum that solves real pain — expand based on feedback
📊DORA + NPSMeasure deploy frequency + developer satisfaction — prove platform value
🏢Team TopologiesPlatform team as enabling team — not ops team, not gatekeepers
🔧Self-ServiceDevs provision DBs, namespaces, TLS via portal/CLI — no tickets!

Chapter 5: Developer Portals with Backstage

Backstage is Spotify's open-source developer portal (now CNCF Incubating) that has become the industry standard for Internal Developer Platforms. It provides a unified UI for all your tools, services, docs, and workflows — the single pane of glass for your platform.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 🎵 Backstage 🔌 Domain 3 (25%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ What Backstage is and why Spotify created it
  • ✅ How to set up Backstage for the anihpj platform
  • ✅ Software Templates: scaffolding new services automatically
  • ✅ Essential Backstage plugins: K8s, ArgoCD, Prometheus, TechDocs
  • ✅ Alternatives: Port, Cortex, Roadie, and when to use each
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🔌 Platform APIs & Self-Service 25%
💡 Backstage — CNPE Exam Focus

Backstage is central to the Platform APIs & Self-Service domain (25%). Expect:

  • Software Catalog — Know catalog-info.yaml structure: kind, type, lifecycle, owner fields
  • Software Templates — Understand how templates scaffold repos, CI, and K8s manifests automatically
  • Plugin architecture — Know which plugins to use: Kubernetes, ArgoCD, Prometheus, TechDocs
  • Self-service workflow — Developer clicks "Create DB" → Backstage → Crossplane → Cloud DB → Secret in K8s

5.1 What is Backstage?

Backstage is a developer portal — a unified web UI where developers can find all their services, documentation, and tools in one place. Spotify built it because they had 150+ microservices and developers were drowning in tool sprawl.

Backstage — The Developer Portal Architecture

                    BACKSTAGE
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │              🔍 SEARCH EVERYTHING                    │
  ├───────────┬───────────┬───────────┬──────────────────┤
  │ Software  │ Software  │ TechDocs  │     Plugins      │
  │ Catalog   │ Templates │           │                  │
  │           │           │           │                  │
  │ • anihpj  │ • Django   │ • API docs│ • K8s            │
  │ • jobpost │   Service  │ • Runbooks│ • ArgoCD         │
  │ • redis   │ • React    │ • Guides  │ • PagerDuty      │
  │ • postgres│   Frontend │           │ • Prometheus     │
  └───────────┴───────────┴───────────┴──────────────────┘
📖 Why Backstage Matters for CNPE

Backstage is the user interface of your platform. Without it, developers interact with your platform through kubectl and YAML. With it, they have a beautiful, searchable portal that reduces cognitive load and makes the platform feel like a polished product.

5.2 Setting Up Backstage for anihpj

Backstage runs as a Node.js application that you deploy on your platform. You configure it via YAML files and extend it with plugins.

📋
Software Catalog

Register all services (anihpj, jobpost) as Components via catalog-info.yaml files. Each service gets a page showing its owner, docs, CI status, and K8s health.

🔌
Kubernetes Plugin

Shows pod status, deployments, and services directly in Backstage. Developers can see if their pods are running without using kubectl.

🔄
ArgoCD Plugin

Displays GitOps sync status for each application. Green = synced, yellow = out of sync, red = error.

📖
TechDocs

Documentation-as-code. Write docs in Markdown alongside your code. Backstage renders them beautifully.

5.3 Software Templates — Scaffolding Services

Backstage Software Templates let developers scaffold new services with best practices baked in. Instead of copying an old project and deleting stuff, they click "Create" and get a production-ready scaffold.

  Dev clicks: "Create Django Microservice"
  →
  Template automatically creates:
  ✅ GitHub repo (Cookiecutter scaffold)
  ✅ CI/CD pipeline (GitHub Actions)
  ✅ Dockerfile (multi-stage, optimized)
  ✅ K8s manifests (deployment, service, ingress)
  ✅ ArgoCD Application (auto-sync to dev)
  ✅ Monitoring dashboard
  ✅ TechDocs skeleton
  ⏱️ Ready in < 2 minutes

📝 Chapter 5 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What problem did Backstage solve at Spotify? Why did they open-source it?
Show Answer
Answer

Spotify had 150+ microservices and developers couldn't find information — "What does this service do? Who owns it? Is it healthy?" Backstage became the single pane of glass. They open-sourced it because every company faces the same tool sprawl problem.

Q2
What are the 4 core capabilities of Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Software Catalog — register and discover all services. 2. Software Templates — scaffold new projects. 3. TechDocs — documentation-as-code. 4. Plugins — extensible architecture for K8s, ArgoCD, Prometheus, etc.

Q3
How would you register the anihpj Django app in Backstage's Software Catalog?
Show Answer
Answer

Add a catalog-info.yaml to the anihpj repo with: kind: Component, type: service, lifecycle: production, owner: team-platform. Backstage auto-discovers it and creates a service page showing owner, docs, CI status, and K8s health.

Q4
What is the difference between Backstage (self-hosted) and Port (SaaS)?
Show Answer
Answer

Backstage: Open-source, self-hosted on your K8s cluster. Full control, 150+ plugins, but requires maintenance. Port: SaaS, no infrastructure to manage, faster setup, but less customizable and you pay per user.

Q5
A developer wants to create a new microservice for anihpj. How does Backstage Software Templates help?
Show Answer
Answer

The developer clicks "Create" in Backstage, selects the "Django Microservice" template, fills in a few fields, and Backstage automatically creates: GitHub repo, Dockerfile, CI/CD pipeline, K8s manifests, ArgoCD Application, and monitoring dashboard — all with best practices baked in.

Q6
Which Backstage plugin would you use to show anihpj's pod status in the portal?
Show Answer
Answer

The Kubernetes plugin. It displays pods, deployments, services, and ingress directly on the Backstage service page. Developers can see if their pods are running, check logs, and view deployment history — all from Backstage, without kubectl.

Q7
What is TechDocs in Backstage? Why is it important for platform adoption?
Show Answer
Answer

TechDocs is Backstage's documentation-as-code system. You write docs in Markdown alongside your code, and Backstage renders them. It's critical for platform adoption because if it's not documented, it doesn't exist. Every golden path needs clear documentation.

Q8
When would you choose Roadie over self-hosting Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

Roadie is a hosted Backstage service. Choose it when: (1) Your team is small and can't maintain Backstage infrastructure, (2) You want Backstage features without the ops burden, (3) You need faster time-to-value. Self-host when you need full control or have specific compliance requirements.

Q9
How does the Software Catalog help reduce cognitive load for developers?
Show Answer
Answer

Instead of asking "Who owns this service? Where's the repo? Is it healthy? Where are the docs?", developers search in Backstage and get ALL that information on one page. It centralizes service metadata that would otherwise be scattered across Slack, wikis, and tribal knowledge.

Q10
What is a Backstage plugin? Name 3 essential plugins for a CNPE.
Show Answer
Answer

A plugin extends Backstage with new functionality (like a VS Code extension). Essential CNPE plugins: 1. Kubernetes — pod/service status. 2. ArgoCD — GitOps sync status. 3. Prometheus — embedded Grafana dashboards. These give developers a complete view of their service health.
