Chapter 6: Platform Abstractions with Crossplane

Crossplane turns Kubernetes into a universal control plane for managing anything — cloud databases, buckets, VMs, even SaaS services — all through kubectl. It's the key to building self-service platform APIs. We'll use it to let anihpj developers provision their own PostgreSQL databases.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~2 hours ☁️ Crossplane 🔌 Domain 3 (25%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ What Crossplane is and how it enables self-service infrastructure
  • ✅ Providers, Managed Resources, and ProviderConfigs
  • ✅ Composite Resources (XR): building your own platform APIs
  • ✅ How to integrate Crossplane into the anihpj platform
  • ✅ Crossplane vs Terraform/Tofu — when to use which
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🔌 Platform APIs & Self-Service 25% 🏗️ Platform Architecture 15%
💡 Crossplane — CNPE Exam Focus

Crossplane is tested in the Platform APIs & Self-Service domain (25%). Key exam topics:

  • Composite Resources (XR) — Your platform API. Define what devs can request (e.g., "PostgreSQL")
  • Composition — The template that turns an XR into actual cloud resources (AKS, DB, bucket)
  • Claims (XRC) — Developer-facing request. A dev creates a Claim; Crossplane provisions real infra
  • Providers — Crossplane's "drivers" for cloud APIs (Azure, AWS, GCP). Install via Helm.

6.1 What is Crossplane?

Crossplane is a Kubernetes-native control plane that lets you manage external infrastructure (cloud databases, buckets, networks) using kubectl and Kubernetes-style YAML. It extends K8s with CRDs for cloud resources — so creating an RDS database feels just like creating a Pod.

Crossplane — Kubernetes-Native Infrastructure as Code

  Developer says:              Platform provides:
  ┌──────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────┐
  │ apiVersion: db.  │        │                  │
  │   platform.io/v1 │        │  Crossplane      │
  │ kind: PostgreSQL │        │  ┌────────────┐  │
  │ spec:            │───────▶│  │Provider AWS│  │
  │   size: small    │        │  │┌──────────┐│  │
  │   version: "15"  │        │  ││RDS       ││  │
  └──────────────────┘        │  ││Instance  ││  │
                              │  │└──────────┘│  │
  Result:                    │  └────────────┘  │
  ✅ RDS created             │                  │
  ✅ Connection Secret       │  Cloud:          │
  ✅ Backups configured      │  ┌────────────┐  │
                              │  │ ☁️ AWS RDS │  │
                              │  └────────────┘  │
                              └──────────────────┘

6.2 Crossplane Providers & Managed Resources

A Provider connects Crossplane to a cloud API (AWS, GCP, Azure). Managed Resources are the individual cloud resources (RDS instance, S3 bucket). ProviderConfig holds the authentication credentials.

ProviderCloudKey Managed Resources
provider-awsAWSRDS, S3, EKS, IAM, VPC, Lambda
provider-gcpGCPCloud SQL, GCS, GKE, IAM, Pub/Sub
provider-azureAzureAzure DB, Blob Storage, AKS, AD
provider-helmK8s (any)Helm Releases (deploy charts)
provider-kubernetesK8s (any)Deployments, Services, CRDs
📖 Key Insight

Managed Resources are 1:1 mappings to cloud resources — too low-level for developers. That's where Composite Resources (XR) come in: you define a simple API that bundles multiple managed resources together.

6.3 Composite Resources — Building Platform APIs

Composite Resource Definitions (XRDs) let you define your own platform APIs. Instead of "create an RDS instance + security group + subnet group," developers just say "give me a PostgreSQL database of size small."

  XRD: "PostgreSQL" (your platform API)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ apiVersion: database.platform.io/v1 │
  │ kind: PostgreSQL                     │
  │ spec:                                │
  │   size: small | medium | large      │
  │   version: "15" | "16"              │
  │   encrypted: true | false           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┘
              │
              ▼
  Composition maps to:
  ✅ RDS Instance (cloud resource)
  ✅ Security Group (network)
  ✅ Subnet Group (VPC placement)
  ✅ K8s Secret (connection string)
  ✅ Backup Configuration

6.4 Crossplane in the anihpj Platform

🗄️
Database API

Devs create a PostgreSQL claim → Crossplane provisions RDS in AWS, Cloud SQL in GCP, or Azure DB — same API, different clouds.

🪣
Storage API

Devs create a Bucket claim → S3, GCS, or Azure Blob. Connection details auto-injected as K8s Secrets.

6.5 Crossplane vs Terraform — When to Use Which

AspectCrossplaneTerraform/Tofu
ModelK8s-native, continuous reconciliationState file, plan/apply cycle
Self-ServiceBuilt-in (kubectl apply)Requires CI/CD pipeline
Drift DetectionContinuous (controllers watch)Only during plan
EcosystemGrowing (50+ providers)Massive (3000+ providers)
Best ForDeveloper self-service resourcesFoundational infrastructure (VPC, EKS)
💡 Use Both!

Use Tofu/Terraform for foundational infra (VPC, EKS cluster, IAM). Use Crossplane for developer-facing self-service resources (databases, buckets, queues). They complement each other perfectly.

📝 Chapter 6 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What is Crossplane? How does it differ from Terraform?
Show Answer
Answer

Crossplane is a K8s-native control plane that uses CRDs and continuous reconciliation (controllers watch resources). Terraform uses a state file with plan/apply cycles. Crossplane enables self-service via kubectl; Terraform typically runs in CI/CD pipelines.

Q2
What is the difference between a Managed Resource and a Composite Resource in Crossplane?
Show Answer
Answer

Managed Resource: 1:1 mapping to a cloud resource (e.g., an RDS instance). Too low-level for devs. Composite Resource (XR): A custom API that bundles multiple managed resources (RDS + security group + backup + secret) into a simple dev-friendly interface.

Q3
A developer on anihpj wants a PostgreSQL database. How does Crossplane enable self-service?
Show Answer
Answer

The developer runs kubectl apply -f postgres-claim.yaml (or clicks in Backstage). Crossplane's PostgreSQL XRD triggers the Composition which creates the RDS instance (or Cloud SQL on GCP), security group, connection Secret, and backup config — all automatically.

Q4
What is a Composition in Crossplane? What problem does it solve?
Show Answer
Answer

A Composition defines HOW a Composite Resource (XR) maps to one or more Managed Resources. It solves the problem of exposing simple dev-facing APIs that hide cloud complexity. Devs ask for "PostgreSQL, size small" — the Composition handles RDS, security groups, and backups.

Q5
What is a Provider in Crossplane? Name 3 cloud providers Crossplane supports.
Show Answer
Answer

A Provider connects Crossplane to a cloud API. Crossplane supports: AWS (provider-aws), GCP (provider-gcp), Azure (provider-azure), plus 50+ community providers for Datadog, GitHub, Confluent, etc.

Q6
How does Crossplane handle drift detection when someone manually changes a cloud resource?
Show Answer
Answer

Crossplane controllers continuously reconcile. If someone manually changes an RDS instance's configuration, the Crossplane controller detects the drift and reverts it to match the desired state defined in the Managed Resource YAML. This is the same reconciliation loop K8s uses for Pods.

Q7
What is the relationship between XRD (Composite Resource Definition), Composition, and Claim (XRC)?
Show Answer
Answer

XRD defines the API schema (what fields exist). Composition defines how the API maps to cloud resources. Claim (XRC) is what the developer creates (a lightweight request). XRD + Composition = Platform API. Claim = Developer-facing self-service interface.

Q8
When should you use Crossplane vs Terraform/Tofu for infrastructure?
Show Answer
Answer

Tofu/Terraform: Foundational infrastructure (VPC, EKS cluster, IAM roles). Crossplane: Developer self-service resources (databases, buckets, queues). Use both — Terraform for the platform's foundation, Crossplane for the developer-facing self-service layer.

Q9
How does Crossplane integrate with GitOps tools like ArgoCD?
Show Answer
Answer

Since Crossplane resources are just K8s CRDs stored as YAML, you commit them to Git. ArgoCD watches the Git repo and syncs any changes — updating XRD schemas, Compositions, or even developer Claims. Everything is Git-managed and auditable.

Q10
A developer creates a PostgreSQL Claim via Crossplane. Where does the connection string go?
Show Answer
Answer

The Composition creates a Kubernetes Secret with the connection string (host, port, user, password, database name). The anihpj pod mounts this Secret as environment variables. The developer never sees or handles the raw credentials — the platform manages them securely.

Chapter 7: CI/CD Fundamentals for Platform Engineers

CI/CD is the engine that powers your platform's software delivery. As a CNPE, you don't just use CI/CD — you build the pipelines that let developers go from code to production in minutes. We'll build a complete CI pipeline for the anihpj Django project.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~2 hours 🔄 CI/CD 🔄 Domain 2 (25%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ CI vs CD vs Continuous Deployment — the differences that matter
  • ✅ How to choose the right CI/CD tool for your platform
  • ✅ Building a complete CI pipeline for anihpj with GitHub Actions
  • ✅ Container image building strategies in CI (Docker, Kaniko, Buildpacks)
  • ✅ Testing strategies: unit, integration, e2e, and infrastructure tests
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🔄 GitOps & Continuous Delivery 25%
💡 CI/CD — CNPE Exam Focus

CI/CD is central to GitOps & Continuous Delivery (25%) and overlaps with Architecture (15%). Focus on:

  • Pipeline Design — Know how to design CI that builds, tests, scans, and pushes images to a registry
  • GitOps Integration — CI does NOT deploy. CI pushes images; ArgoCD deploys. Understand this separation.
  • Image Tagging Strategy — Never :latest. Use git SHA or semver. CI must update the GitOps repo with the new tag.
  • Pipeline as Code — GitHub Actions, GitLab CI — know YAML syntax for common CI tasks (build, test, push)

7.1 What is CI/CD?

Continuous Integration (CI): Merge code frequently, run automated tests on every push. Continuous Delivery (CD): Every change is deployable (but deployment is manual). Continuous Deployment: Every change goes to production automatically.

The CI/CD Pipeline — End to End

  👨‍💻 Push Code    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐
  ─────────────▶  │  BUILD   │───▶│   TEST   │───▶│  SCAN    │
                  │ pip inst │    │ pytest   │    │ Trivy    │
                  │ collect- │    │ lint     │    │ Semgrep  │
                  │ static   │    │ coverage │    │          │
                  └──────────┘    └──────────┘    └──────────┘
                                                         │
                                    ┌────────────────────┘
                                    ▼
  👥 Users Access    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐
  ◀─────────────    │  DEPLOY  │◀───│  PROMOTE │
                    │ ArgoCD   │    │ Staging  │
                    │ sync     │    │ tests    │
                    └──────────┘    └──────────┘

  ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────────┐    ┌──────────────┐
  │ GitHub   │───▶│ GitHub Actions│───▶│  ArgoCD      │
  │ GitLab   │    │ OR GitLab CI  │    │  GitOps Sync │
  │ Bitbucket│    │ OR Tekton    │    │              │
  └──────────┘    └──────────────┘    └──────────────┘
                                               │
                    ┌──────────────────────────┘
                    ▼
  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐
  │ ☸️ DEV   │─▶│ ☸️ STAGE │─▶│ ☸️ PROD  │
  │ Auto on  │  │ Auto on  │  │ Manual   │
  │ push     │  │ PR merge │  │ promote  │
  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘
  🎯 Domain 2 (25%): This GitOps workflow IS the exam

7.3 Building a CI Pipeline for anihpj

Here's a complete GitHub Actions workflow for the anihpj Django project — checkout, setup Python, install dependencies, run tests, lint, build Docker image, and push to registry.

.github/workflows/ci.yml
name: CI Pipeline
on: [push, pull_request]
jobs:
  test:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-python@v5
        with: {python-version: '3.12'}
      - run: pip install -r requirements.txt
      - run: pytest --cov
      - run: ruff check .
  build-and-push:
    needs: test
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: docker/build-push-action@v5
        with: {push: true, tags: anihpj:${{ github.sha }}}

7.4 Container Image Building in CI

ToolHow It WorksBest For
Docker BuildKitFaster builds, better caching, parallel stagesStandard CI runners
KanikoBuilds images in K8s without Docker daemonK8s-native CI (no Docker socket)
BuildpacksAuto-detects language, no Dockerfile neededSimple apps, quick start

7.5 Testing Strategies for Platform Components

The Testing Pyramid for Platform Engineering

            ╱     E2E Tests      ╲
           ╱  (Full user flows)   ╲      ← Fewest, slowest, most realistic
          ╱─────────────────────────╲
         ╱    Integration Tests      ╲
        ╱  (Component interactions)   ╲    ← Medium count & speed
       ╱─────────────────────────────────╲
      ╱         Unit Tests                ╲
     ╱    (Individual functions/classes)   ╲  ← Most, fastest, most isolated
    ╱─────────────────────────────────────────╲

  For anihpj platform:
  Unit: pytest for Django models/views
  Integration: Test API + PostgreSQL + Redis together
  E2E: Playwright/Cypress — "Can user create job posting?"
  Infra: Terratest — "Does Crossplane create RDS?"
🧪
Unit Tests

Test individual functions in isolation. Fast, reliable. pytest for Django models and views.

🔗
Integration Tests

Test component interactions. Does anihpj correctly connect to PostgreSQL and Redis?

🌐
E2E Tests

Test full user workflows. "Can a user create a job posting and see it listed?" Playwright/Cypress.

🏗️
Infra Tests

Test IaC modules. Does the Crossplane Composition actually create an RDS instance? Terratest.

📝 Chapter 7 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What is the difference between Continuous Delivery and Continuous Deployment?
Show Answer
Answer

Continuous Delivery: Every change is deployable, but deployment to production is a manual decision. Continuous Deployment: Every change that passes tests is automatically deployed to production — no human gate.

Q2
Why would a Platform Engineer choose Tekton over GitHub Actions?
Show Answer
Answer

Tekton is Kubernetes-native — pipelines run as pods in your K8s cluster. Choose Tekton when you need: custom pipeline logic, tight K8s integration, multi-cloud portability, or when you want your CI system managed via GitOps like everything else.

Q3
What is a multi-stage CI pipeline? What stages would anihpj need?
Show Answer
Answer

A pipeline with sequential stages, each depending on the previous passing. anihpj's stages: Checkout → Setup Python → Install Dependencies → Run Tests → Lint → Build Docker Image → Scan Image → Push to Registry. Each stage only runs if the previous one succeeds.

Q4
Why might you use Kaniko instead of Docker to build images in CI?
Show Answer
Answer

Kaniko builds container images without needing a Docker daemon. This is critical in K8s environments where mounting the Docker socket is a security risk. Kaniko runs as a pod and pushes directly to the registry.

Q5
What caching strategies can speed up CI builds for a Django project like anihpj?
Show Answer
Answer

1. pip cache: Cache installed Python packages. 2. Docker layer cache: Use registry caching or buildkit cache mounts. 3. Test cache: Only run tests for changed code (pytest-testmon). 4. Static files cache: Skip collectstatic if no static files changed.

Q6
What is a matrix build? Give an example for anihpj.
Show Answer
Answer

A matrix build runs the same tests against multiple configurations in parallel. For anihpj: test against Python 3.10, 3.11, 3.12 × Django 4.2, 5.0, 5.1 = 9 parallel test jobs. This ensures compatibility across versions without slowing down CI.

Q7
What is the difference between unit, integration, and end-to-end tests?
Show Answer
Answer

Unit: Tests single functions (e.g., test the JobPost model's slug generation). Integration: Tests component interactions (e.g., JobPost API + PostgreSQL). E2E: Tests full user flows (e.g., user logs in → creates job → sees it listed). Each level increases in scope and execution time.

Q8
How should you tag container images built in CI? Why?
Show Answer
Answer

Tag with Git SHA (anihpj:abc1234) for immutability and traceability. Also tag with semantic version (anihpj:v1.2.3) for readability. Optionally tag branch builds (anihpj:main-abc1234). Never use :latest in production.

Q9
What happens if the CI pipeline fails at the "build Docker image" stage?
Show Answer
Answer

The pipeline stops (fail-fast). No image is pushed to the registry. ArgoCD doesn't see a new image, so nothing gets deployed. The developer gets a notification (GitHub status check, Slack alert) and fixes the issue. The current production version keeps running unaffected.

Q10
You're setting up CI for anihpj. After tests pass, what should happen before the image is pushed?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Lint the code (ruff/pylint). 2. Scan for secrets (gitleaks). 3. Check dependency vulnerabilities (safety/pip-audit). 4. Build the Docker image. 5. Scan the image for CVEs (Trivy). Only if ALL pass: tag and push to registry. This is shift-left security in practice.

Chapter 8: GitOps with ArgoCD

GitOps is the practice of using Git as the single source of truth for both application code AND infrastructure configuration. ArgoCD is the industry-standard GitOps tool that continuously syncs your cluster to match your Git repository. This is 25% of the CNPE exam — master it.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~2 hours 🔄 GitOps 🔄 Domain 2 (25%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ GitOps principles: Git as truth, declarative config, automated reconciliation
  • ✅ ArgoCD architecture and how it syncs desired state to your cluster
  • ✅ Deploying anihpj with ArgoCD using the Base + Overlays pattern
  • ✅ ArgoCD ApplicationSets for multi-environment deployments
  • ✅ Argo Rollouts for Blue/Green and Canary deployments
  • ✅ ArgoCD vs FluxCD — choosing the right GitOps tool
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🔄 GitOps & Continuous Delivery 25%
💡 GitOps with ArgoCD — CNPE Exam Focus

This is the MOST heavily tested CNPE domain (25%). You must know:

  • ArgoCD Application YAML — source, destination, syncPolicy fields — write from memory!
  • Sync Policies — Manual vs Automated. Auto-prune. Self-heal. Know when to use each.
  • Apps-of-Apps Pattern — Parent app manages child apps via Git. This is the platform team pattern.
  • Drift Detection — ArgoCD compares Git (desired) with cluster (actual). OutOfSync = manual kubectl edit.

8.1 What is GitOps?

GitOps flips the traditional deployment model: instead of CI pushing changes to the cluster, a GitOps agent like ArgoCD pulls the desired state from Git and applies it.

GitOps — The Pull-Based Model

  Developer changes       GitOps Agent watches
  ┌──────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────┐
  │ Git Repository   │    │ ArgoCD / Flux    │
  │ ┌──────────────┐ │    │ ┌──────────────┐ │
  │ │deployment.yaml│ │───▶│ │ Sync desired │ │
  │ │ replicas: 5   │ │Pull│ │ state to     │ │
  │ │ image: v2.0   │ │    │ │ cluster      │ │
  │ └──────────────┘ │    │ └──────┬───────┘ │
  └──────────────────┘    └───────┼────────┘
                                  │
                                  ▼
                          ┌──────────────────┐
                          │ ☸️ K8s Cluster   │
                          │ ┌──────┐┌──────┐│
                          │ │Pod v2││Pod v2││
                          │ └──────┘└──────┘│
                          └──────────────────┘
  💡 PULL is more secure than PUSH — cluster
     credentials never leave the cluster.

8.2 ArgoCD Deep Dive

ArgoCD has 3 core components: API Server (handles gRPC/REST), Repo Server (clones Git repos), and Application Controller (compares desired vs actual state and triggers sync).

ArgoCD Architecture

                       ARGOCD
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │             🖥️ Web UI / CLI / API              │
  ├───────────────┬───────────────┬─────────────────┤
  │ API Server    │ Repo Server   │ Application     │
  │ gRPC/REST     │ Clones Git    │ Controller      │
  │ requests      │ repos, caches │ Compare→Sync    │
  └───────┬───────┴───────┬───────┴────────┬────────┘
          │               │                │
          ▼               ▼                ▼
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │              ☸️ K8s Cluster                      │
  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐        │
  │  │ anihpj   │ │ jobpost  │ │ redis    │        │
  │  └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

8.3 Deploying anihpj with ArgoCD

Use the Base + Overlays pattern: base/ holds common config, overlays/ holds environment-specific patches.

GitOps Repo Structure for anihpj

  gitops-repo/
  └── apps/anihpj/
      ├── base/                 # Common config
      │   ├── deployment.yaml
      │   ├── service.yaml
      │   └── kustomization.yaml
      └── overlays/             # Env-specific
          ├── dev/              → replicas: 1
          ├── staging/          → replicas: 2
          └── prod/             → replicas: 3

8.4 ArgoCD ApplicationSets — Deploy at Scale

ApplicationSets use generators to create ArgoCD Applications from templates — instead of creating 15 separate Application YAMLs for 3 apps across 5 environments, you define one ApplicationSet that generates them all.

GeneratorInputExample Use Case
ListStatic list of itemsDeploy anihpj to dev, staging, prod
GitDirectories in a Git repoAuto-discover apps in apps/ folder
ClusterRegistered K8s clustersDeploy to all clusters in a fleet
Pull RequestOpen PRs on GitHub/GitLabPreview env per PR (pr-123-anihpj)
MatrixCartesian product of 2 generatorsAll apps × all environments

8.5 Argo Rollouts — Advanced Deployment Strategies

Argo Rollouts extends K8s Deployments with advanced strategies beyond the built-in rolling update.

🔵🟢
Blue/Green

Two complete environments. Traffic switches instantly via Service selector change. Rollback is instant (switch back). Cost: double resources during cutover.

🐤
Canary

Gradual traffic shift (10%→25%→50%→100%). Each step has a Prometheus analysis window. Auto-rollback if metrics degrade. Safer but slower than Blue/Green.

📖 Argo Rollouts vs K8s Deployments

K8s Deployments only support rolling updates. Argo Rollouts adds a Rollout CRD that replaces the standard Deployment and gives you Blue/Green and Canary capabilities with built-in metric analysis and automated rollback.

8.6 ArgoCD vs FluxCD

FeatureArgoCDFluxCD
UIRich web UI + CLICLI-focused, basic UI
Multi-tenancyBuilt-in projectsNamespace-based
Sync StrategyAuto-sync or manualAuto-sync by default
CNPE Pick✅ Best for mostGood for CLI-heavy teams

ArgoCD Sync Strategies

  STRATEGY           BEHAVIOR                  WHEN TO USE
  ────────────────── ───────────────────────── ───────────
  Manual Sync        Click "Sync" in UI        Production
  Auto-Sync          Auto on Git change        Dev/Staging
  Auto-Sync + Prune  Auto + delete removed     PR previews
  Auto-Sync +        Auto + revert manual      Strict envs
  Self-Heal          changes automatically

📝 Chapter 8 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What is GitOps? How does it differ from traditional push-based deployment?
Show Answer
Answer

GitOps uses Git as the single source of truth. An agent (ArgoCD) pulls desired state from Git and syncs it to the cluster. Traditional CI/CD pushes changes via kubectl or helm. Pull is more secure — cluster credentials never leave the cluster.

Q2
What are the three core components of ArgoCD and their roles?
Show Answer
Answer

API Server: Handles gRPC/REST requests (UI, CLI). Repo Server: Clones Git repos, generates manifests. Application Controller: Compares desired state (Git) vs actual state (cluster), triggers sync when they differ.

Q3
What is the Base + Overlays pattern in ArgoCD? Why use it?
Show Answer
Answer

Base/ holds common configuration (deployment, service). Overlays/ hold environment-specific patches (dev=1 replica, prod=3 replicas). This avoids duplicating YAML across environments and ensures consistency — you change the base once, all environments inherit it.

Q4
What happens if someone manually deletes a pod that ArgoCD manages?
Show Answer
Answer

If Self-Heal is enabled, ArgoCD detects the drift and recreates the pod immediately. If self-heal is disabled, ArgoCD shows the app as OutOfSync (yellow) but doesn't automatically fix it — you must manually sync. Prod usually uses manual sync; dev uses auto-sync + self-heal.

Q5
What is an ArgoCD ApplicationSet? Name 3 generator types.
Show Answer
Answer

An ApplicationSet template-generates ArgoCD Applications. Generators: List (static list of clusters/envs), Git (directories in a repo), Cluster (auto-discover registered clusters), Pull Request (creates preview env per PR), Matrix (combine generators).

Q6
What is the difference between Blue/Green and Canary deployments in Argo Rollouts?
Show Answer
Answer

Blue/Green: Two complete environments. Traffic switches from Blue (v1) to Green (v2) instantly. Fast rollback: switch back. Canary: Gradual %-based traffic shift (10% → 25% → 50% → 100%). Each step checks Prometheus metrics. Auto-rollback on error spikes.

Q7
When would you choose ArgoCD over FluxCD?
Show Answer
Answer

Choose ArgoCD when you need: a rich web UI, built-in multi-tenancy (projects), ApplicationSets for scaled deployments, or your teams prefer visual GitOps management. Choose Flux when you prefer CLI-heavy workflows or need tighter OCI/Helm integration.

Q8
What does "prune" mean in ArgoCD sync options?
Show Answer
Answer

Prune deletes cluster resources that exist in the cluster but NOT in Git. Without prune, removing a YAML file from Git doesn't delete the resource from K8s. With prune, ArgoCD cleans up orphaned resources automatically.

Q9
How does the PR generator in ArgoCD ApplicationSets work?
Show Answer
Answer

The PR generator watches for open Pull Requests on a Git repo. For each PR, it creates a temporary ArgoCD Application that deploys the PR's code to a preview namespace (e.g., pr-123-anihpj). When the PR is merged or closed, the preview environment is automatically deleted.

Q10
An anihpj deployment shows "OutOfSync" in ArgoCD. What could cause this?
Show Answer
Answer

Possible causes: (1) Someone manually changed a resource via kubectl, (2) A new image was pushed but the Git manifest still references the old tag, (3) The Git repo is unreachable, (4) A sync failed due to validation errors. Check ArgoCD's diff view to see exactly what diverged.

Chapter 9: Progressive Delivery & Release Engineering

Deploying software isn't just "push and pray." Modern platforms use progressive delivery — gradually shifting traffic to new versions while monitoring for errors, with automatic rollback if something goes wrong. This is where platform engineering directly prevents production incidents.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 🚀 Delivery 🔄 Domain 2 (25%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ 6 deployment strategies: Rolling, Recreate, Blue/Green, Canary, A/B, Shadow
  • ✅ How to implement Canary deployments with Argo Rollouts and Prometheus analysis
  • ✅ Feature flags and how they enable safer releases
  • ✅ Release gates, approval workflows, and automated promotion
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🔄 GitOps & Continuous Delivery 25%
💡 Progressive Delivery — CNPE Exam Focus

Progressive delivery is a core GitOps competency (25% of exam). The exam expects you to:

  • Choose the right strategy — Canary for risk reduction, Blue/Green for instant rollback, Rolling for simple stateless apps
  • Configure Argo Rollouts — Know the YAML structure: canary steps, analysis templates, auto-rollback thresholds
  • Understand analysis — Prometheus queries that gate each canary step. If error rate > 5%, auto-rollback.
  • Feature flags vs deployments — Deployment puts code in production; feature flags make it visible. Know the difference.

9.1 Deployment Strategies Deep Dive

Not all deployments are created equal. Here's how the 6 strategies compare:

Deployment Strategies — Visual Comparison

  🔄 ROLLING UPDATE (Default K8s)
    v1 v1 v1 → v2 v1 v1 → v2 v2 v1 → v2 v2 v2
    Gradual replacement, zero downtime

  🔵🟢 BLUE/GREEN (Instant Switch)
    ┌─────────┐     ┌─────────┐
    │ 🔵 BLUE │────▶│ 🟢 GREEN│  Traffic switches at once
    │ v1 v1 v1│     │ v2 v2 v2│  100% v1 → 100% v2
    └─────────┘     └─────────┘  ✅ Instant rollback

  🐤 CANARY (Gradual Traffic Shift)
    v1: 90% ████████████  v1: 0%
    v2: 10% ██       →    v2: 100% ████████████
    Each step: watch metrics → healthy? → increase v2%
    ❌ If error rate spikes → AUTO-ROLLBACK
StrategyDowntimeRollbackResource CostBest For
Rolling UpdateZeroMediumNormalDefault for most apps
RecreateYesSlowLowBatch jobs, migrations
Blue/GreenZeroInstant2x during cutoverCritical apps
CanaryZeroInstant (auto)NormalHigh-risk changes
A/B TestingZeroMediumNormalUser experiments
Shadow/MirrorZeroN/A2xProduction traffic testing

Rolling Update & Recreate

Rolling Update: K8s default strategy — replaces pods one by one with zero downtime. The Deployment controller creates new pods and terminates old ones gradually. Recreate: Stops ALL existing pods first, then starts new ones. Causes downtime but is the simplest strategy — use only for non-critical batch jobs or database migrations where concurrent old+new would cause issues.

Blue/Green Deployments

Two complete, identical environments — Blue (current v1) and Green (new v2). Traffic switches instantly via Service selector change from Blue to Green. Advantage: Instant rollback — just switch the Service selector back to Blue. Disadvantage: Double resource usage during cutover (both environments run at full capacity).

Canary, A/B Testing & Shadow

Canary: Gradual percentage-based traffic shift with automated metric analysis between each step. A/B Testing: Route based on user attributes (headers, cookies, geography) — different from canary which is purely percentage-based. Shadow/Mirror: Duplicate production traffic to the new version, but discard its responses. The new version processes real traffic silently — perfect for risk-free production testing before going live.

9.2 Implementing Canary Deployments

Canary Deployment Pipeline for anihpj

  ① BUILD & TEST        ② DEPLOY CANARY      ③ ANALYZE
  ┌────────────────┐   ┌────────────────┐   ┌────────────────┐
  │ GitHub Actions │   │ Argo Rollouts  │   │ Prometheus     │
  │ • pytest       │──▶│ • v2: 10%      │──▶│ • Error rate   │
  │ • build image  │   │ • v1: 90%      │   │ • Latency p99  │
  └────────────────┘   └───────┬────────┘   └───────┬────────┘
                               │                    │
                    ┌──────────┴────────────────────┘
                    │  Metrics OK? → v2: 33% → 66% → 100%
                    │  Metrics BAD? → AUTO-ROLLBACK to v1
                    │  ⏱️ Each step: 5 min analyze window

9.3 Feature Flags for Platform Engineers

Feature flags are runtime toggles that decouple deployment from release. Deploy code with the feature hidden, then enable it for specific users. OpenFeature is the CNCF standard for feature flagging.

Feature Flags Architecture — Decouple Deploy from Release

  DEPLOY (Technical)          RELEASE (Business)
  ┌──────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────┐
  │ Code pushed to   │        │ Feature enabled   │
  │ production       │   ≠    │ for users         │
  │ (v2 running but  │        │ (v2 visible to    │
  │  feature hidden) │        │  everyone)        │
  └──────────────────┘        └──────────────────┘
           │                           ▲
           ▼                           │
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │        FEATURE FLAG SYSTEM           │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Feature: "jobpost-export"      │  │
  │  │ Enabled: 10% (beta users)      │  │
  │  │ → Gradual rollout 10→50→100%  │  │
  │  │ → Kill switch: disable if 💥  │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┘

  💡 Platform Pattern: Feature flags + canary deploys
     = maximum safety with maximum velocity
💡 Platform Pattern

Use feature flags for: dark launches (deploy disabled), canary releases (enable for 10%), beta programs (enable for beta users), and kill switches (disable broken features instantly without redeploying).

9.4 Release Gates and Approval Workflows

🛑
Manual Approval Gates

ArgoCD can require manual approval before syncing to production. Used for regulated industries or high-risk changes.

✅
Automated Gates

Tests pass → metrics green → security scan clean → deploy. No human needed. This is the goal for most platforms.

Release Pipeline — Dev to Production with Gates

  git push ──▶ ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐
               │ ☸️ DEV   │───▶│ ☸️ STAGE │───▶│ ☸️ PROD  │
               │ Auto     │    │ Auto on  │    │ Manual   │
               │ on push  │    │ PR merge │    │ or auto  │
               └──────────┘    └──────────┘    └──────────┘
                     │               │               │
                     ▼               ▼               ▼
               🟢 Gate:        🟡 Gate:        🔴 Gate:
               • Tests pass    • Integration   • Security scan
               • Lint clean    • tests pass     • clean
               • Build OK      • Perf OK        • Approval
                               • Canary OK      • Metrics OK

📝 Chapter 9 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What is the difference between a Rolling Update and a Canary deployment?
Show Answer
Answer

Rolling Update: Replaces pods one by one with no traffic control — K8s default. Canary: Explicitly controls traffic percentages (10%→25%→50%→100%) with metric analysis between each step. Canary auto-rolls back if metrics degrade; rolling update doesn't.

Q2
Why does Blue/Green deployment require double the resources?
Show Answer
Answer

During cutover, BOTH the Blue environment (v1) and Green environment (v2) run simultaneously with full replicas. If anihpj runs 3 replicas normally, Blue/Green needs 6 replicas during the switch. This is the cost of instant rollback capability.

Q3
How does Argo Rollouts automatically roll back a Canary deployment?
Show Answer
Answer

Each canary step runs a Prometheus analysis. If the analysis detects: error rate > threshold, latency p99 > threshold, or any defined metric violation — Argo Rollouts automatically reverts all traffic back to the stable version (v1). No human intervention needed.

Q4
What is the difference between feature flags and deployment strategies like canary?
Show Answer
Answer

Canary/Blue-Green: Controls which VERSION of code receives traffic (v1 vs v2). Feature Flags: Controls which FEATURES are enabled within the same code version. You can deploy code with Feature X disabled, then enable it via flag — without redeploying.

Q5
What is a "dark launch"? How do feature flags enable it?
Show Answer
Answer

A dark launch deploys new code to production but keeps it hidden from users via a feature flag. The platform team (or internal testers) can enable it for themselves to test in production. If it breaks, disable the flag — instant rollback without redeploying.

Q6
What is a release gate? Give 3 examples of automated gates.
Show Answer
Answer

A release gate is a checkpoint that must pass before deployment proceeds. Examples: 1. Tests pass (CI green), 2. Security scan clean (no critical CVEs), 3. Metrics healthy (error rate < 1%, latency p99 < 200ms). Gates prevent bad code from reaching production.

Q7
How does A/B testing differ from canary deployment?
Show Answer
Answer

Canary: Traffic split by percentage (10% get v2). A/B Testing: Traffic split by user attributes (beta users get v2, regular users get v1). Canary is about risk reduction; A/B testing is about measuring user behavior differences between versions.

Q8
What is a "kill switch" in the context of feature flags?
Show Answer
Answer

A kill switch is a feature flag that can instantly disable a broken feature in production — without redeploying. If the jobpost export feature starts crashing, flip the flag to "off" and the feature disappears for all users. Fix the bug, test, then re-enable. Zero-downtime mitigation.

Q9
What's the difference between automated and manual approval for production deploys? When use each?
Show Answer
Answer

Automated: Metric-based gates pass → deploy automatically. Use for most services. Manual: Human clicks "approve" in ArgoCD. Use for: financial transactions, healthcare data, or when regulatory compliance requires human sign-off.

Q10
You're deploying anihpj v2 via canary. 10% traffic shows doubled error rate. What happens?
Show Answer
Answer

Argo Rollouts' Prometheus analysis detects the error spike → the canary step FAILS → Argo Rollouts automatically reverts ALL traffic back to v1 (100% v1, 0% v2). The v2 pods are scaled down. On-call is alerted. The deployment is aborted. Developers investigate the v2 issue.

Chapter 10: Infrastructure as Code with OpenTofu/Terraform

Infrastructure as Code (IaC) is the practice of defining infrastructure in version-controlled files instead of clicking in cloud consoles. OpenTofu (the open-source fork of Terraform) lets you provision everything — K8s clusters, databases, networking — with a single command. We'll provision the anihpj platform from scratch.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~2 hours 🔧 IaC 🔌 Domain 3 (25%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ IaC principles: declarative vs imperative, state files, drift detection
  • ✅ OpenTofu basics: HCL syntax, providers, modules, workspaces
  • ✅ Provisioning an EKS/GKE/AKS cluster with OpenTofu
  • ✅ Bootstrapping platform components (ArgoCD, Prometheus, Kyverno)
  • ✅ IaC best practices: remote state, locking, plan review, module structure
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🔌 Platform APIs & Self-Service 25%

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — IaC

The CNPE exam tests practical IaC knowledge: you may be asked to provision a cluster, manage state, or fix a broken state file. Know the difference between plan and apply, how state locking prevents corruption, and why remote state is critical. Declarative vs imperative is a favorite exam topic — be able to explain why declarative IaC is essential for platform engineering. Also expect questions about Tofu/Terraform + Crossplane integration.

10.1 IaC Fundamentals

Infrastructure as Code means treating your infrastructure like software: version-controlled, reviewed, tested, and automated. No more clicking in cloud consoles (click-ops) or SSH-ing into servers and running commands. Your entire platform — from VPC to K8s cluster to monitoring stack — is defined in .tf files checked into Git.

The IaC Workflow — The Platform Engineer's Loop

  ① WRITE                    ② PLAN                    ③ APPLY
  ┌──────────────────┐      ┌──────────────────┐      ┌──────────────────┐
  │ main.tf          │      │ tofu plan        │      │ tofu apply       │
  │ variables.tf     │─────▶│                  │─────▶│                  │
  │ outputs.tf       │      │ Plan: 5 to add,  │      │ ☁️ Resources     │
  │ terraform.tfvars │      │  2 to change,    │      │   created!       │
  └──────────────────┘      │  1 to destroy    │      │                  │
                            └──────────────────┘      └──────────────────┘
                                   │                          │
                                   │     ┌────────────────────┘
                                   │     │
                                   ▼     ▼
  ④ STATE MANAGEMENT                               ⑤ GIT COMMIT
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐      ┌──────────────────┐
  │ Remote State (S3/GCS/Azure Blob)     │      │ git add .        │
  │ • State locking (DynamoDB)           │─────▶│ git commit -m    │
  │ • Versioning enabled                 │      │   "Add EKS"      │
  │ • Encryption at rest                 │      │ git push         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┘      └──────────────────┘

  💡 NEVER commit .tfstate to Git! Always use remote state.
  💡 State contains ALL your secrets in plain text — encrypt it!

Declarative vs Imperative IaC

AspectDeclarative (Tofu/Terraform)Imperative (ClickOps/Scripts)
What you defineDESIRED state (what you want)HOW to get there (steps)
Exampleresource "aws_eks_cluster" "main" { name = "prod" }aws eks create-cluster --name prod ...
Idempotency✅ Built-in — run 100 times, same result❌ Must check if-already-exists manually
Drift Detection✅ `plan` shows any drift from desired state❌ No built-in drift detection
Peer Review✅ Git PR review like any code❌ Hard to review console clicks
💡 CNPE Mindset

Everything you build as a Platform Engineer should be declarative and version-controlled. If your EKS cluster dies tomorrow, you should be able to recreate it with ONE command: tofu apply. No manual steps. No tribal knowledge. Just Git and code.

10.2 OpenTofu (Open-Source Terraform)

OpenTofu uses HCL (HashiCorp Configuration Language), a declarative language designed for infrastructure. You define resources (EKS cluster, VPC, RDS database), run tofu plan to preview changes, and tofu apply to create them. State is stored remotely (S3, GCS, Azure Blob) with locking via DynamoDB to prevent concurrent modifications.

OpenTofu Core Concepts — At a Glance

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    OPEN TOFU ECOSYSTEM                          │
  │                                                                │
  │  ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐   │
  │  │ PROVIDER │   │ RESOURCE │   │  MODULE  │   │  STATE   │   │
  │  │          │   │          │   │          │   │          │   │
  │  │ AWS ████ │   │ VPC      │   │ Reusable │   │ S3/Dynamo│   │
  │  │ GCP ████ │   │ EKS      │   │ building │   │ GCS      │   │
  │  │ Azure ██ │   │ RDS      │   │ blocks   │   │ Azure    │   │
  │  │ K8s  ███ │   │ IAM      │   │          │   │ Blob     │   │
  │  │ Helm ██  │   │ S3       │   │ DRY      │   │ Locking  │   │
  │  │ 3000+... │   │ ...      │   │ principle│   │ Version  │   │
  │  └──────────┘   └──────────┘   └──────────┘   └──────────┘   │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Minimal OpenTofu Example — EKS Cluster

# main.tf — Define the AWS provider
provider "aws" {
  region = "us-east-1"
}

# VPC for the cluster
module "vpc" {
  source  = "terraform-aws-modules/vpc/aws"
  version = "5.0.0"
  name    = "anihpj-vpc"
  cidr    = "10.0.0.0/16"
  azs     = ["us-east-1a", "us-east-1b", "us-east-1c"]
}

# EKS Cluster
module "eks" {
  source  = "terraform-aws-modules/eks/aws"
  version = "20.0.0"
  cluster_name    = "anihpj-prod"
  cluster_version = "1.29"
  vpc_id          = module.vpc.vpc_id
  subnet_ids      = module.vpc.private_subnets
}

# Output the kubeconfig
output "cluster_endpoint" {
  value = module.eks.cluster_endpoint
}

Key Commands Every CNPE Must Know

CommandWhat It DoesWhen to Use
tofu initDownloads providers, initializes backendFirst time, or after changing providers
tofu planShows what WILL change (dry run)Before EVERY apply — review changes!
tofu applyCreates/updates infrastructureAfter reviewing the plan
tofu destroyTears down ALL resources⚠️ Be VERY careful — irreversible!
tofu fmtFormats HCL code consistentlyBefore committing to Git
tofu validateChecks syntax without connectingIn CI pipeline, after linting

10.3 Provisioning a K8s Cluster with OpenTofu

Provisioning a production K8s cluster involves defining VPC networking, IAM roles, the cluster control plane, and worker node groups — all in code. One tofu apply creates everything. The same pattern works across AWS (EKS), GCP (GKE), and Azure (AKS).

AWS EKS — The Full Stack

What tofu apply creates for EKS
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     AWS CLOUD (us-east-1)                     │
  │                                                               │
  │  ┌─────────────────── VPC (10.0.0.0/16) ───────────────────┐ │
  │  │                                                           │ │
  │  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐               │ │
  │  │  │ Public   │  │ Public   │  │ Public   │  ← NAT GW     │ │
  │  │  │ Subnet A │  │ Subnet B │  │ Subnet C │               │ │
  │  │  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘               │ │
  │  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐               │ │
  │  │  │ Private  │  │ Private  │  │ Private  │               │ │
  │  │  │ Subnet A │  │ Subnet B │  │ Subnet C │               │ │
  │  │  │          │  │          │  │          │               │ │
  │  │  │ ┌──────┐ │  │ ┌──────┐ │  │ ┌──────┐ │               │ │
  │  │  │ │Node  │ │  │ │Node  │ │  │ │Node  │ │ ← Worker     │ │
  │  │  │ │Group │ │  │ │Group │ │  │ │Group │ │   Nodes      │ │
  │  │  │ └──────┘ │  │ └──────┘ │  │ └──────┘ │               │ │

  │  │                                                           │ │
  │  │         ┌────────────────────────────┐                   │ │
  │  │         │   EKS Control Plane        │  ← Managed by AWS│ │
  │  │         │   (runs in AWS account)    │                   │ │
  │  │         └────────────────────────────┘                   │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Cloud Comparison — EKS vs GKE vs AKS

ResourceAWS (EKS)GCP (GKE)Azure (AKS)
Clusteraws_eks_clustergoogle_container_clusterazurerm_kubernetes_cluster
Node Poolaws_eks_node_groupgoogle_container_node_poolazurerm_kubernetes_cluster_node_pool
