Outputoutput "kubeconfig" { value = module.eks.kubeconfig }
📖 Key Concept: The kubeconfig Output

After tofu apply creates the cluster, Tofu outputs the kubeconfig — the authentication file that lets kubectl talk to your cluster. You can pipe this directly to subsequent Tofu resources (like the Helm provider) or save it for manual use. Never commit kubeconfig to Git — it contains credentials!

💡 CNPE Pattern

Use Tofu/Terraform for foundational infrastructure (VPC, EKS, IAM) and Crossplane for developer self-service resources (databases, buckets). They complement each other perfectly — Tofu builds the platform, Crossplane lets developers use it.

10.4 Provisioning Platform Components — "One Command to Rule Them All"

After the cluster exists, use the Helm provider within Tofu to install: ArgoCD (GitOps), Prometheus + Grafana (monitoring), Kyverno (policy), cert-manager (TLS). One tofu apply bootstraps your entire platform from bare cloud to a working IDP.

Platform Bootstrap — One `tofu apply` Creates Everything

  $ tofu apply    ← Single command that creates EVERYTHING

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  STEP 1: INFRASTRUCTURE (2-3 minutes)                      │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ✅ VPC + Subnets + NAT Gateway + Internet Gateway     │  │
  │  │ ✅ EKS Cluster (control plane across 3 AZs)          │  │
  │  │ ✅ Node Groups (3 worker nodes, t3.large)            │  │
  │  │ ✅ IAM Roles (cluster role + node role + pod role)   │  │
  │  │ ✅ Security Groups + KMS keys for encryption         │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  STEP 2: PLATFORM SERVICES (3-5 minutes)                   │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ✅ ArgoCD (GitOps engine — syncs everything after)   │  │
  │  │ ✅ cert-manager (automatic TLS via Let's Encrypt)     │  │
  │  │ ✅ Prometheus + Grafana (monitoring & dashboards)    │  │
  │  │ ✅ Kyverno (policy engine — governance from day 0)   │  │
  │  │ ✅ Crossplane (developer self-service APIs)          │  │
  │  │ ✅ External Secrets Operator (secure secret sync)    │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  STEP 3: APPLICATION DELIVERY (1 minute)                   │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ✅ ArgoCD App-of-Apps (bootstraps all team apps)     │  │
  │  │ ✅ anihpj deployed via GitOps automatically          │  │
  │  │ ✅ jobpost API live at https://anihpj.example.com    │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ⏱️ TOTAL TIME: ~8 minutes from empty cloud → fully operational IDP
  🎯 This is what Domain 3 (25%) is about — platform automation!

Helm Provider Example — Installing ArgoCD via Tofu

# After EKS module creates the cluster, install platform tools
provider "helm" {
  kubernetes {
    host                   = module.eks.cluster_endpoint
    cluster_ca_certificate = base64decode(module.eks.cluster_ca_certificate)
    exec {
      api_version = "client.authentication.k8s.io/v1beta1"
      args        = ["eks", "get-token", "--cluster-name", module.eks.cluster_name]
      command     = "aws"
    }
  }
}

resource "helm_release" "argocd" {
  name       = "argocd"
  repository = "https://argoproj.github.io/argo-helm"
  chart      = "argo-cd"
  version    = "6.7.0"
  namespace  = "argocd"
  create_namespace = true

  set {
    name  = "server.service.type"
    value = "LoadBalancer"
  }
}

resource "helm_release" "prometheus" {
  name       = "kube-prometheus-stack"
  repository = "https://prometheus-community.github.io/helm-charts"
  chart      = "kube-prometheus-stack"
  namespace  = "monitoring"
  create_namespace = true
}
💡 CNPE Best Practice

Use Tofu/Terraform for foundational infrastructure (VPC, EKS, IAM roles) and Crossplane for developer self-service resources (databases, buckets, queues). Tofu creates the platform; Crossplane lets developers use it. They complement each other — not compete.

What Else Should You Bootstrap?

ComponentPurposeHelm ChartPriority
ArgoCDGitOps — sync everything else from Gitargo/argo-cd🔴 Critical
cert-managerAuto TLS via Let's Encryptjetstack/cert-manager🔴 Critical
Prometheus StackMetrics + Dashboards + Alertsprometheus-community/kube-prometheus-stack🟡 High
KyvernoPolicy engine — governance from day 0kyverno/kyverno🟡 High
CrossplaneDev self-service APIs (RDS, S3, etc.)crossplane/crossplane🟡 High
External Secrets OperatorSync secrets from cloud → K8sexternal-secrets/external-secrets🟡 High
BackstageDeveloper portal / service catalogbackstage/backstage🟢 Medium
Ingress ControllerHTTP routing into the clusteringress-nginx/ingress-nginx🔴 Critical
📖 Bootstrap Order Matters!

Install in this order: (1) cert-manager (needed for TLS), (2) Ingress Controller (needed for routing), (3) ArgoCD (installs everything else via GitOps), (4) Prometheus + Kyverno + Crossplane + ESO via ArgoCD App-of-Apps. After step 3, everything is GitOps-managed — you never run helm install manually again.

10.5 IaC Best Practices & Advanced Patterns

#PracticeWhy It Matters
1Remote stateNever store .tfstate locally — use S3/GCS with versioning and encryption
2State lockingPrevent two people/CI pipelines running `apply` simultaneously (DynamoDB)
3Plan before applyAlways review what will change BEFORE it changes — save plans as artifacts
4Modules (DRY)Don't copy-paste VPC config. Write a module once, reuse across environments
5State securityState contains secrets! Encrypt at rest, restrict IAM access, never commit to Git
6WorkspacesIsolate environments (dev/staging/prod) — separate state per workspace
7Policy as CodeUse Sentinel/OPA to enforce compliance — "no S3 buckets can be public"
8GitOps for IaCRun `tofu apply` from CI, not laptops. Git is the source of truth for infra too

Module Structure — The Canonical Layout

anihpj-infra/
├── main.tf              # Root module — calls child modules
├── variables.tf          # Input variables (with types & defaults)
├── outputs.tf            # Output values (cluster endpoint, etc.)
├── terraform.tfvars      # Variable values (NEVER commit secrets here!)
├── versions.tf           # Provider & Tofu version constraints
├── modules/
│   ├── vpc/              # Reusable VPC module
│   │   ├── main.tf
│   │   ├── variables.tf
│   │   └── outputs.tf
│   ├── eks/              # Reusable EKS module
│   │   ├── main.tf
│   │   ├── variables.tf
│   │   └── outputs.tf
│   └── platform-tools/   # ArgoCD, Prometheus, Kyverno
│       ├── main.tf
│       ├── variables.tf
│       └── outputs.tf
└── environments/
    ├── dev/
    │   └── terraform.tfvars   # dev-specific values
    ├── staging/
    │   └── terraform.tfvars   # staging-specific values
    └── prod/
        └── terraform.tfvars   # prod-specific values

Workspaces vs Directory-Based Environments

AspectTofu WorkspacesDirectory-Based (Recommended)
How it worksSame code, separate state per workspaceSeparate directories with their own .tf files
ProsSingle codebase, quick switchingFull isolation, different configs per env
ConsSame backend, same creds, less isolationSome code duplication (mitigated by modules)
Best forSmall projects, quick prototypingProduction platforms (CNPE-recommended)
⚠️ Critical — State File Security

Tofu/Terraform state files contain ALL your infrastructure secrets in plain text — database passwords, API keys, TLS private keys. Anyone who can read your S3 bucket can read your secrets. Always: (1) Enable server-side encryption on the state bucket, (2) Restrict IAM access to platform engineers only, (3) Use sensitive = true on outputs that expose secrets, (4) Never, EVER commit state files to Git.

📝 Chapter 10 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What is Infrastructure as Code? Why is it essential for Platform Engineering?
Show Answer
Answer

IaC defines infrastructure in version-controlled files instead of manual console clicks. It's essential because platforms need repeatability, auditability, and disaster recovery — if your EKS cluster is destroyed, you recreate it with one command.

Q2
What is the difference between OpenTofu and Terraform?
Show Answer
Answer

OpenTofu is the open-source fork of Terraform, created after HashiCorp changed Terraform's license to BSL. They're functionally equivalent. OpenTofu is governed by the Linux Foundation, uses the same HCL syntax, and supports the same providers.

Q3
Why should Terraform/Tofu state be stored remotely? What problems does local state cause?
Show Answer
Answer

Local state: (1) Can't be shared between team members, (2) Gets lost if your laptop dies, (3) No locking — two people can apply simultaneously and corrupt state. Remote state (S3/GCS with DynamoDB locking) solves all three.

Q4
What happens if two people run `tofu apply` at the same time without state locking?
Show Answer
Answer

State corruption. Both processes read the same state, both apply changes, and the second one to write overwrites the first one's changes. Resources may be duplicated, orphaned, or left in an inconsistent state. Always use state locking.

Q5
How do you provision platform components like ArgoCD using Tofu after creating the K8s cluster?
Show Answer
Answer

Use the Helm provider in Tofu. After the EKS cluster is created (and kubeconfig is available), define helm_release resources for ArgoCD, Prometheus, Kyverno, and cert-manager. They're installed as Helm charts managed by Tofu — one `apply` bootstraps everything.

Q6
What is the difference between `tofu plan` and `tofu apply`?
Show Answer
Answer

plan: Shows what WILL change (add/modify/destroy) without actually changing anything — a dry run. apply: Executes the plan and makes real changes to infrastructure. Always review the plan before applying in production.

Q7
Why should you use Tofu/Terraform for foundational infra and Crossplane for dev self-service?
Show Answer
Answer

Tofu excels at foundational infra (VPC, EKS, IAM) with its massive provider ecosystem and plan/apply workflow. Crossplane excels at developer self-service with continuous reconciliation via K8s controllers and kubectl-native APIs. Use both: Tofu for the platform foundation, Crossplane for the dev-facing layer.

Q8
What is a Tofu/Terraform module? Why use them?
Show Answer
Answer

A module is a reusable, composable IaC component. Instead of copying 100 lines of VPC config for each environment, you define a vpc module once and reuse it. Modules enforce the DRY principle, reduce errors, and make infrastructure consistent across environments.

Q9
Tofu state files contain secrets. How do you protect them?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Remote state with encryption (S3 SSE, GCS encryption). 2. Restrict access to the state bucket (IAM policies). 3. Never commit state to Git. 4. Use sensitive outputs to prevent secrets from appearing in plan output. 5. Consider Terraform Cloud/Enterprise for managed state.

Q10
Your EKS cluster is accidentally deleted. You have Tofu state in S3. What do you do?
Show Answer
Answer

Run tofu apply. Tofu reads the state, sees that the cluster resource exists in state but not in reality, and recreates it. The entire cluster — VPC, subnets, IAM, EKS, node groups — is rebuilt from code. Disaster recovery in one command. This is why IaC matters.

Chapter 11: Configuration Management with Helm

Helm is the package manager for Kubernetes — it lets you define, install, and upgrade complex K8s applications as reusable charts. As a CNPE, you'll package your platform components and developer applications as Helm charts for consistent, repeatable deployments.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 📦 Helm 🔌 Domain 3 (25%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Helm fundamentals: Charts, Releases, Repositories, Values
  • ✅ Creating a complete Helm chart for the anihpj application
  • ✅ Helm chart testing, linting, and CI integration
  • ✅ Helm vs Kustomize — when to use each (or both together)
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🔌 Platform APIs & Self-Service 25%

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — Helm

Expect questions about chart structure, values overriding, and Helm vs Kustomize tradeoffs. You may be asked to debug a failing Helm release, roll back to a previous version, or package a chart for an OCI registry. Know the values precedence chain: chart defaults → parent values → user values file → --set flags (highest priority).

11.1 Helm Fundamentals

Helm has 3 core concepts: Chart (the package — templates + default values), Release (an installed instance of a chart), and Repository (where charts are stored, now using OCI registries).

Helm — The K8s Package Manager

  📦 HELM CHART                    ⚙️ VALUES
  ┌──────────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────┐
  │ anihpj-chart/        │        │ values.yaml      │
  │ ├── Chart.yaml       │   +    │ replicas: 3       │
  │ ├── values.yaml      │        │ image.tag: v2.1   │
  │ ├── templates/       │        │ ingress.enabled:  │
  │ │   ├── deployment   │        │   true            │
  │ │   ├── service      │        └──────────────────┘
  │ │   ├── ingress      │              │
  │ │   ├── configmap    │              ▼
  │ │   └── _helpers.tpl │        ┌──────────────────┐
  │ └── charts/          │        │  RENDERED YAML   │
  └──────────────────────┘        │  ↓               │
                                  │  kubectl apply    │
  ┌──────────────────────┐        │  ↓               │
  │  📡 HELM REPO        │        │  ☸️ CLUSTER      │
  │  (OCI Registry)      │        └──────────────────┘
  └──────────────────────┘

Helm vs Plain YAML — When to Use Each

ScenarioPlain YAMLHelm Chart
Simple, static app (1 env)✅ Quick and easyOverkill — unnecessary complexity
Multi-environment (dev/staging/prod)Copy-paste YAML × 3, drift risk✅ One chart, different values per env
Distributing to other teamsRaw YAML, no versioning✅ Packaged chart with version, deps
CI/CD integrationkubectl apply -f .helm upgrade --install with rollback
Complex logic (if/else, loops)Not possible — static YAML✅ Go templates with conditionals
📖 Platform Engineer's Rule of Thumb

If you need to deploy the SAME application to MORE THAN ONE environment (dev, staging, prod), use Helm. If it's a one-off config (like a NetworkPolicy that's the same everywhere), plain YAML + kubectl apply is fine. But for platform components that teams install — always package as Helm charts.

11.2 Creating a Helm Chart for anihpj

A complete Helm chart packages your application as a reusable, configurable unit. Here's the full structure for anihpj:

Complete anihpj Helm Chart Structure

  anihpj-chart/
  ├── Chart.yaml              # Chart metadata
  │   ├── apiVersion: v2
  │   ├── name: anihpj
  │   ├── version: 1.0.0
  │   ├── appVersion: "2.1.0"
  │   └── description: "Django job board platform"
  │
  ├── values.yaml             # Default configuration
  │   ├── replicaCount: 3
  │   ├── image:
  │   │   ├── repository: registry.io/anihpj
  │   │   └── tag: "v2.1.0"
  │   ├── service:
  │   │   └── port: 8000
  │   ├── ingress:
  │   │   ├── enabled: true
  │   │   └── host: anihpj.example.com
  │   └── resources:
  │       ├── limits: {cpu: 500m, memory: 512Mi}
  │       └── requests: {cpu: 250m, memory: 256Mi}
  │
  ├── templates/
  │   ├── deployment.yaml     # Kubernetes Deployment
  │   ├── service.yaml        # ClusterIP Service
  │   ├── ingress.yaml        # Ingress + TLS
  │   ├── configmap.yaml      # Django settings
  │   ├── hpa.yaml            # HorizontalPodAutoscaler
  │   ├── serviceaccount.yaml # Pod identity
  │   ├── _helpers.tpl        # Reusable template functions
  │   └── NOTES.txt           # Post-install instructions
  │
  └── charts/                 # Sub-chart dependencies
      └── (postgresql, redis, etc.)

Deployment Template Example

# templates/deployment.yaml
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: {{ include "anihpj.fullname" . }}
  labels:
    {{- include "anihpj.labels" . | nindent 4 }}
spec:
  replicas: {{ .Values.replicaCount }}
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      {{- include "anihpj.selectorLabels" . | nindent 6 }}
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        {{- include "anihpj.selectorLabels" . | nindent 8 }}
    spec:
      serviceAccountName: {{ include "anihpj.serviceAccountName" . }}
      containers:
        - name: {{ .Chart.Name }}
          image: "{{ .Values.image.repository }}:{{ .Values.image.tag }}"
          ports:
            - containerPort: {{ .Values.service.port }}
          envFrom:
            - configMapRef:
                name: {{ include "anihpj.fullname" . }}-config
            - secretRef:
                name: {{ include "anihpj.fullname" . }}-db-credentials
          resources:
            {{- toYaml .Values.resources | nindent 12 }}
📖 Platform Pattern — Golden Paths

Package your platform as Helm charts. Developers don't write YAML from scratch — they run helm install my-app platform-chart --set db=true. Golden paths become Helm commands with pre-configured values. This is how you make platform engineering self-service.

11.3 Helm Chart Testing and CI Integration

Test your charts at every level before they reach production:

🔍
helm lint

Static analysis — checks YAML syntax, missing required fields, template errors. Runs in seconds. First line of defense.

📄
helm template

Renders templates locally — see exactly what YAML your chart produces without installing anything. Great for debugging.

🧪
helm unittest

Unit tests for templates — test that specific values produce expected YAML output. Like pytest for your Helm charts.

☸️
Chart Testing (ct)

End-to-end: lint → install → run test pods → uninstall. Tests in a real (temporary) K8s cluster. The gold standard.

Helm Chart CI Pipeline (GitHub Actions)

# .github/workflows/helm-ci.yml
name: Helm Chart CI
on:
  pull_request:
    paths:
      - 'charts/anihpj/**'

jobs:
  lint-test:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
        with:
          fetch-depth: 0  # Required for ct to detect changes

      - name: Setup Helm
        uses: azure/setup-helm@v4

      - name: Setup Kubernetes (kind)
        uses: helm/kind-action@v1

      - name: Run chart-testing (lint)
        uses: helm/chart-testing-action@v2
        with:
          command: lint

      - name: Run chart-testing (install + test)
        uses: helm/chart-testing-action@v2
        with:
          command: install
        # This installs the chart in a kind cluster,
        # runs helm test pods, then uninstalls

      - name: Push chart to OCI registry
        if: github.ref == 'refs/heads/main'
        run: |
          helm package charts/anihpj
          helm push anihpj-*.tgz oci://registry.io/charts

OCI Registries — The Modern Way to Store Charts

Traditionally, Helm charts were stored in chart repositories (a simple HTTP server with an index.yaml). Today, OCI registries (Docker Hub, ECR, GCR, ACR) support Helm charts natively — the same registry that stores your container images also stores your charts. One registry, one auth, simpler ops.

OperationTraditional Chart RepoOCI Registry (Modern)
Add repohelm repo add myrepo https://...N/A — use registry login
Push chartUpload .tgz + update index.yamlhelm push chart.tgz oci://registry.io/charts
Installhelm install myapp myrepo/charthelm install myapp oci://registry.io/charts/chart
AuthSeparate auth mechanismSame as Docker — `helm registry login`

11.4 Helm vs Kustomize — Choosing the Right Tool (or Both!)

AspectHelmKustomize
ApproachTemplate-based (Go templates)Template-free (overlay patches)
ComplexityHigher — templates + functionsLower — pure YAML patches
ReusabilityCharts + repos + dependenciesOverlays + bases + components
VersioningSemantic versioning built-inGit versioning (no built-in version)
Rollbackhelm rollback — one commandManual — revert Git commit, re-apply
Built into kubectl❌ Separate CLI install✅ kubectl apply -k
Best ForPackaging & distributing appsEnvironment-specific config
TogetherHelm for packaging + Kustomize for overlays = perfect combo

The "Helm + Kustomize" Pattern — Best of Both Worlds

The CNPE-recommended pattern: use Helm to package your application (with sensible defaults), then use Kustomize overlays to apply environment-specific patches on top of the rendered Helm output.

# Directory structure: Helm + Kustomize together
anihpj-deploy/
├── base/                          # Kustomize base
│   ├── kustomization.yaml         # References the Helm chart
│   │   # helmCharts:
│   │   #   - name: anihpj
│   │   #     repo: oci://registry.io/charts
│   │   #     version: 1.0.0
│   │   #     releaseName: anihpj
│   │   #     valuesFile: values.yaml
│   └── values.yaml                # Base values (applies to all envs)
│
├── overlays/
│   ├── dev/
│   │   ├── kustomization.yaml     # Patches for dev
│   │   └── dev-values.yaml        # dev-specific: replicas: 1, debug: true
│   ├── staging/
│   │   ├── kustomization.yaml     # Patches for staging
│   │   └── staging-values.yaml    # staging-specific: replicas: 2
│   └── prod/
│       ├── kustomization.yaml     # Patches for prod
│       └── prod-values.yaml       # prod-specific: replicas: 5, resources+

# Deploy to prod:
kubectl apply -k overlays/prod/
📖 Real-World CNPE Pattern

Helm does the heavy lifting: "Here's how to deploy anihpj — it needs a Deployment, Service, Ingress, ConfigMap, and HPA." Kustomize handles the differences: "In dev, use 1 replica and no TLS. In prod, use 5 replicas, bigger resources, and a different domain." Together they give you parameterized packaging + environment-specific customization — exactly what a platform team needs.

📝 Chapter 11 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What is a Helm Chart? What are its 4 key components?
Show Answer
Answer

A Helm Chart is a package of pre-configured K8s resources. 4 components: Chart.yaml (metadata), values.yaml (default config), templates/ (Go-templated YAML files), charts/ (sub-chart dependencies).

Q2
What is the difference between a Helm Chart and a Helm Release?
Show Answer
Answer

Chart: The package/template (like a Docker image). Release: An installed instance of a chart in a cluster (like a Docker container). You can install the same chart multiple times as different releases with different values.

Q3
What is `_helpers.tpl` in a Helm chart? Why is it used?
Show Answer
Answer

_helpers.tpl contains reusable template functions — like defining a common name or label selector that's used across deployment.yaml, service.yaml, etc. It prevents duplicating template logic across files.

Q4
How does Helm handle upgrades? What happens if an upgrade fails?
Show Answer
Answer

Helm upgrades are incremental — only changed resources are updated. If an upgrade fails, Helm automatically rolls back to the previous successful release. You can also manually roll back: helm rollback my-release 3 (where 3 is the revision number).

Q5
When should you use Helm vs Kustomize? Can you use both?
Show Answer
Answer

Helm: Packaging and distributing complex apps (ArgoCD, Prometheus). Kustomize: Environment-specific configuration overlays (dev/staging/prod patches). YES, use both: Helm for packaging, Kustomize for overlays on top of rendered Helm output.

Q6
What are OCI-based Helm registries? How do they differ from traditional chart repos?
Show Answer
Answer

OCI registries (like Docker Hub, ECR, GCR) now support Helm charts natively. Instead of helm repo add, you push/pull charts like container images: helm push anihpj-1.0.0.tgz oci://registry.io/charts. This unifies container and chart storage.

Q7
What does `helm lint` do? When should you run it?
Show Answer
Answer

helm lint checks a chart for YAML syntax errors, missing required fields, template rendering issues, and best practice violations. Run it in CI on every pull request that changes chart files — catch errors before they reach production.

Q8
How do Helm values work? What's the override precedence?
Show Answer
Answer

Values override in this order (later overrides earlier): 1. Chart's values.yaml, 2. Parent chart's values, 3. User's -f my-values.yaml, 4. --set key=value flags. --set has the highest priority and overrides everything else.

Q9
What is a Helm dependency? How do you manage them?
Show Answer
Answer

A dependency is a sub-chart your chart needs (e.g., anihpj chart depends on a PostgreSQL chart). Define in Chart.yaml under dependencies. Run helm dependency update to download them. This enables composing complex applications from reusable charts.

Q10
You're packaging anihpj as a Helm chart. What templates would you include?
Show Answer
Answer

deployment.yaml (pod spec, replicas, image), service.yaml (ClusterIP), ingress.yaml (HTTP routing, TLS), configmap.yaml (Django settings), hpa.yaml (auto-scaling rules), serviceaccount.yaml (pod identity), and NOTES.txt (post-install instructions for the developer).

Chapter 12: Secret Management

Secrets (passwords, API keys, TLS certs) are the most sensitive data on your platform. Kubernetes Secrets are NOT encrypted by default — they're base64-encoded, which is NOT encryption. As a CNPE, you must implement proper secret management with tools like External Secrets Operator, Sealed Secrets, and SOPS.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 🔐 Security 🔐 Domain 5 (15%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Why K8s Secrets alone are insufficient for production
  • ✅ External Secrets Operator (ESO): syncing secrets from AWS, GCP, Vault
  • ✅ Sealed Secrets: encrypting secrets for safe Git storage
  • ✅ SOPS: encrypting YAML files with cloud KMS
  • ✅ How to choose the right secret management tool for your platform
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🔐 Security & Policy Enforcement 15%

12.1 The Secret Problem — Why K8s Secrets Alone Are Dangerous

Kubernetes Secrets are stored as base64-encoded (NOT encrypted) in etcd by default. Anyone with kubectl get secrets can decode them. And if you accidentally commit a Secret YAML to Git, it's in the git history forever — even if you delete the file later.

Secret Risk — Threat Vectors for Secrets in Kubernetes

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    THREAT VECTORS FOR SECRETS                     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────┐     ┌─────────────────────┐             │
  │  │ 1. ETCD DATASTORE   │     │ 2. KUBECTL ACCESS   │             │
  │  │                     │     │                     │             │
  │  │ Secrets base64 in   │     │ kubectl get secret  │             │
  │  │ etcd (NOT encrypted)│     │ db-credentials      │             │
  │  │ Anyone with etcd    │     │ -o yaml             │             │
  │  │ access → all secrets│     │ → decode base64     │             │
  │  │                     │     │ → PLAIN TEXT!       │             │
  │  │ ⚠️ Enable encryption│     │ ⚠️ Restrict RBAC    │             │
  │  │    at rest!         │     │    aggressively!    │             │
  │  └─────────────────────┘     └─────────────────────┘             │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────┐     ┌─────────────────────┐             │
  │  │ 3. GIT EXPOSURE     │     │ 4. ENV VARS / LOGS  │             │
  │  │                     │     │                     │             │
  │  │ git commit secret.yaml    │ env:                │             │
  │  │ → in history FOREVER      │   DB_PASS=secret    │             │
  │  │ → GitHub public repo      │ → printed in logs   │             │
  │  │   = instant compromise    │ → debug endpoints   │             │
  │  │                     │     │ → crash dumps       │             │
  │  │ ⚠️ Use gitleaks     │     │ ⚠️ Mount as files,  │             │
  │  │    pre-commit hooks!│     │    never env vars!  │             │
  │  └─────────────────────┘     └─────────────────────┘             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
⚠️ Critical — Enable etcd Encryption at Rest

Without etcd encryption, anyone with filesystem access to the etcd data directory can read ALL your Secrets in plain text. Enable it via --encryption-provider-config on the API server. This is a CKS-level topic, but every CNPE must verify it's enabled on all production clusters. If you remember ONE thing from this chapter: base64 ≠ encryption.

😱 The "I Accidentally Committed a Secret" Nightmare

A developer creates a K8s Secret YAML for testing, commits it to a public GitHub repo, and pushes. Within minutes, bots scan new commits for AWS keys, API tokens, and passwords. Your cloud account gets compromised. The fix: (1) Immediately rotate ALL exposed credentials, (2) Use git filter-branch or BFG Repo-Cleaner to purge the secret from history, (3) Implement gitleaks pre-commit hooks to prevent recurrence. Better yet — never have raw secrets in your repo in the first place. That's what this entire chapter is about.

12.2 External Secrets Operator (ESO)

ESO syncs secrets from external stores (AWS Secrets Manager, GCP Secret Manager, Azure Key Vault, HashiCorp Vault, 1Password) into K8s Secrets. You define a ClusterSecretStore (backend configuration) and an ExternalSecret (mapping external → K8s Secret). ESO periodically reconciles to keep them in sync — if the external secret rotates, your K8s pods get the new value automatically (after a restart/reload).

Supported Secret Backends — Quick Reference

BackendAuth MethodBest ForComplexity
AWS Secrets ManagerIRSA (IAM Roles for Service Accounts)AWS-native platforms🟡 Medium
GCP Secret ManagerWorkload IdentityGCP-native platforms🟡 Medium
Azure Key VaultManaged Identity (Azure AD)Azure-native platforms🟡 Medium
HashiCorp VaultK8s Auth / AppRole / JWTMulti-cloud, enterprise, regulated🔴 High
1PasswordConnect Token (1Password Connect Server)Small teams, shared vaults🟢 Low

External Secrets Operator — Architecture Flow

  ☁️ EXTERNAL SECRET STORES

  ┌──────────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────────┐
  │ AWS Secrets Manager  │    │ HashiCorp Vault      │
  │ ┌──────────────────┐ │    │ ┌──────────────────┐ │
  │ │ anihpj/db-pass   │ │    │ │ secret/anihpj/db │ │
  │ │ anihpj/api-key   │ │    │ │ secret/anihpj/tls│ │
  │ │ anihpj/redis-url │ │    │ │ dynamic/aws/creds│ │
  │ └──────────────────┘ │    │ └──────────────────┘ │
  └──────────┬───────────┘    └──────────┬───────────┘
             │                            │
             └─────────────┬──────────────┘
                           │
                           ▼
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │            EXTERNAL SECRETS OPERATOR (ESO)               │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ClusterSecretStore                                │  │
  │  │   → AWS: region + IAM role (IRSA)                 │  │
  │  │   → Vault: address + auth method (K8s/AppRole)    │  │
  │  │   → GCP: project + workload identity              │  │
  │  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤  │
  │  │ ExternalSecret                                    │  │
  │  │   spec:                                           │  │
  │  │     target: {name: db-credentials}                │  │
  │  │     data: [{secretKey: POSTGRES_PASSWORD,         │  │
  │  │             remoteRef: {key: anihpj/db-pass}}]    │  │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                           │
                           ▼
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ☸️ KUBERNETES SECRET: db-credentials                   │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ POSTGRES_USER=anihpj                              │  │
  │  │ POSTGRES_PASSWORD=supersecret                     │  │
  │  │ REDIS_URL=redis://...                             │  │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │  → Mounted as env vars / volumes in anihpj pods         │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

ESO Setup Example — AWS Secrets Manager

# 1. ClusterSecretStore — HOW to access AWS
apiVersion: external-secrets.io/v1beta1
kind: ClusterSecretStore
metadata:
  name: aws-secret-store
spec:
  provider:
    aws:
      service: SecretsManager
      region: us-east-1
      auth:
        jwt:
          serviceAccountRef:
            name: eso-sa  # IRSA-enabled service account

---
# 2. ExternalSecret — WHAT to sync
apiVersion: external-secrets.io/v1beta1
kind: ExternalSecret
metadata:
  name: anihpj-db-credentials
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  refreshInterval: 1h         # Auto-refresh every hour
  secretStoreRef:
    name: aws-secret-store
    kind: ClusterSecretStore
  target:
    name: db-credentials       # K8s Secret to create/update
    creationPolicy: Owner      # ESO owns and manages this secret
  data:
    - secretKey: POSTGRES_USER
      remoteRef:
        key: anihpj/prod/db
        property: username
    - secretKey: POSTGRES_PASSWORD
      remoteRef:
        key: anihpj/prod/db
        property: password
💡 ESO Auto-Refresh

ESO periodically reconciles (default: 1 hour). When the external secret value changes (e.g., after rotation), ESO detects the difference and updates the K8s Secret. Your pods need to reload/restart to pick up the new value — use Reloader or stakater to auto-restart pods on secret/configmap changes.

AWS Secrets Manager

Store secrets in AWS Secrets Manager. ESO uses IRSA (IAM Roles for Service Accounts) to authenticate — no static credentials. Define a ClusterSecretStore with the AWS region and an ExternalSecret referencing the secret ARN or name. Supports JSON secret properties (username/password in one secret).

HashiCorp Vault

Enterprise-grade secret management with dynamic secrets (auto-generated, short-lived DB credentials), audit logging, and fine-grained policies. ESO authenticates via Kubernetes auth method or AppRole. Vault is the go-to for multi-cloud, regulated industries, and when you need encryption-as-a-service.

GCP/Azure Backends

GCP Secret Manager and Azure Key Vault work similarly — ESO authenticates via workload identity (GCP) or managed identity (Azure). Both are deeply integrated with their respective cloud IAM systems, making them the simplest choice for single-cloud platforms.

12.3 Sealed Secrets — GitOps-Friendly Encryption

Sealed Secrets let you encrypt secrets for safe Git storage. You use kubeseal CLI to encrypt a plain K8s Secret into a SealedSecret CRD. Only the cluster's sealing controller can decrypt it — the sealed data is useless outside your cluster. Commit SealedSecret YAML to Git, and ArgoCD syncs it normally.

Sealed Secrets Workflow

  💻 DEVELOPER LAPTOP                    ☸️ KUBERNETES CLUSTER

  ┌──────────────────────┐              ┌──────────────────────────┐
  │ 1. Create plain      │              │                          │
  │    K8s Secret YAML   │              │   Sealed Secrets         │
  │    (db-credentials)  │              │   Controller             │
  │         │            │              │   ┌──────────────────┐   │
  │         ▼            │              │   │ Public Key       │   │
  │ 2. kubeseal encrypts │              │   │ (shared openly)  │───┼──▶ Can be in Git
  │    using public key  │◀─────────────│   ├──────────────────┤   │
  │         │            │   fetches    │   │ Private Key      │   │
  │         ▼            │   pub key    │   │ (NEVER leaves    │   │
  │ 3. SealedSecret CRD  │              │   │  the cluster!)   │   │
  │    (ENCRYPTED)       │              │   └──────────────────┘   │
  │         │            │              │          │               │
  │         ▼            │              │          ▼               │
  │ 4. git commit + push │──────────────│  5. Controller decrypts  │
  │    (safe for Git!)   │   ArgoCD     │     → plain K8s Secret   │
  └──────────────────────┘   syncs      │     → pods can use it!   │
                                        └──────────────────────────┘

Sealed Secrets CLI Workflow

# STEP 1: Create a plain K8s Secret (NEVER commit this to Git!)
kubectl create secret generic db-credentials \
  --from-literal=POSTGRES_PASSWORD=supersecret \
  --from-literal=API_KEY=sk-1234567890 \
  --dry-run=client -o yaml > secret.yaml

# STEP 2: Seal the secret (encrypt it for this cluster only)
kubeseal --format yaml < secret.yaml > sealed-secret.yaml

# STEP 3: Delete the plain secret.yaml (NEVER commit it!)
rm secret.yaml

# STEP 4: Commit ONLY sealed-secret.yaml to Git
git add sealed-secret.yaml
git commit -m "Add sealed DB credentials for anihpj"
git push

# STEP 5: ArgoCD syncs sealed-secret.yaml to the cluster
# → Sealed Secrets Controller automatically decrypts it
# → Plain K8s Secret is created → pods use it!
📖 When to Use Sealed Secrets

Sealed Secrets is ideal for GitOps-first teams that want everything in Git. It's simple, requires no external secret store, and the encryption is cluster-specific. Trade-off: secrets are tied to ONE cluster — if that cluster dies, you need the private key backup to decrypt.

12.4 SOPS (Secrets OPerationS) — Cloud KMS Encryption

SOPS encrypts YAML/JSON/ENV files using cloud KMS (AWS KMS, GCP KMS, Azure Key Vault) or age/PGP keys. Unlike Sealed Secrets (cluster-specific), SOPS-encrypted files are portable — anyone with KMS access can decrypt. It integrates natively with ArgoCD for GitOps.

SOPS + AWS KMS Workflow

# STEP 1: Create .sops.yaml config (which KMS keys to use)
cat > .sops.yaml << EOF
creation_rules:
  - kms: arn:aws:kms:us-east-1:123456789012:key/abc-123-def
    encrypted_regex: '^(password|secret|key|token)$'
EOF

# STEP 2: Create a plain secret file
cat > anihpj-secrets.yaml << EOF
apiVersion: v1
kind: Secret
metadata:
  name: db-credentials
stringData:
  POSTGRES_PASSWORD: supersecret
  API_KEY: sk-1234567890
EOF

# STEP 3: Encrypt with SOPS (only values matching regex are encrypted)
sops --encrypt anihpj-secrets.yaml > anihpj-secrets.enc.yaml

# STEP 4: Commit ONLY the encrypted file to Git
git add anihpj-secrets.enc.yaml .sops.yaml
git commit -m "Add encrypted secrets for anihpj"
git push

# STEP 5: ArgoCD decrypts automatically before applying
# (ArgoCD needs KMS decrypt permission via IRSA)

ArgoCD + SOPS Integration

ArgoCD can natively decrypt SOPS-encrypted files before applying them to the cluster. Configure ArgoCD with the KMS decrypt permission (via IRSA or service account), and it handles decryption transparently. The encrypted file sits in Git; the decrypted Secret only exists in the cluster.

AspectSealed SecretsSOPS
Encryption keyCluster-specific private keyCloud KMS (AWS, GCP, Azure) or age/PGP
PortabilityTied to one clusterPortable — any cluster with KMS access
Decrypt locationIn-cluster controllerArgoCD decrypts before apply (or CI)
ArgoCD integrationNative (CRD-based)Native (built-in SOPS support)
Best forSingle-cluster, simple GitOpsMulti-cluster, multi-cloud GitOps

ToolBest ForGit-Safe?ComplexityExternal Deps?

💡 CNPE Recommendation

Start with ESO + your cloud's secret manager. It's the simplest path to proper secret management with minimal operational overhead. Add Vault later if you need multi-cloud, dynamic secrets, or advanced audit requirements. And ALWAYS enable etcd encryption at rest on every cluster — this is non-negotiable for production.

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — Secret Management

Expect scenario-based questions: "Your team accidentally committed a Secret to GitHub. What should you do?" or "How would you implement secret management for a multi-cloud IDP?" Know the difference between ESO, Sealed Secrets, and SOPS, and when to recommend each. Remember: base64 is NOT encryption — this is one of the most tested concepts.

📝 Chapter 12 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
Are Kubernetes Secrets encrypted by default? What's the risk?
Show Answer
Answer

NO. K8s Secrets are base64-encoded, NOT encrypted. Anyone with kubectl get secret -o yaml can decode them. Enable etcd encryption at rest and use External Secrets Operator to keep secrets out of etcd entirely.

Q2
What is the External Secrets Operator? How does it work?
Show Answer
Answer

ESO syncs secrets from external stores (AWS Secrets Manager, Vault) into K8s Secrets. You define a ClusterSecretStore (backend config) and an ExternalSecret (mapping external secret → K8s Secret). ESO periodically refreshes to keep them in sync.

Q3
What is the difference between Sealed Secrets and SOPS?
Show Answer
Answer

Sealed Secrets: Encrypt via cluster-side controller, decryptable only by that specific cluster. SOPS: Encrypt via cloud KMS (AWS KMS, GCP KMS), decryptable wherever you have KMS access. Sealed Secrets are cluster-specific; SOPS is more portable across clusters.

Q4
You accidentally commit a K8s Secret YAML to GitHub. What's the risk? What should you do?
Show Answer
Answer

Risk: Anyone with repo access can decode the base64 values and obtain your secrets. They're also in Git history forever. Action: (1) Immediately rotate all exposed secrets, (2) Remove the file and rewrite Git history (BFG Repo-Cleaner), (3) Implement pre-commit hooks (gitleaks) to prevent recurrence.

Q5
What AWS resources do you need for ESO to sync secrets from AWS Secrets Manager?
Show Answer
Answer

1. IAM Role (with IRSA — IAM Roles for Service Accounts) granting secretsmanager:GetSecretValue. 2. ClusterSecretStore referencing the AWS region. 3. ExternalSecret referencing the secret ARN and target K8s Secret name.

Q6
Why would you choose HashiCorp Vault over cloud-native secret managers?
Show Answer
Answer

Vault offers: multi-cloud (one tool for all clouds), dynamic secrets (auto-generated, short-lived DB credentials), audit logging, fine-grained policies, and encryption-as-a-service. Cloud secret managers are simpler but tied to a single cloud.

Q7
How does Sealed Secrets enable GitOps-friendly secret management?
Show Answer
Answer

You can safely commit SealedSecret CRDs to Git — they're encrypted and useless outside the cluster. ArgoCD syncs the SealedSecret, the cluster controller decrypts it into a regular K8s Secret, and your pods use it normally. Git remains the single source of truth, even for secrets.

Q8
What is etcd encryption at rest? Why is it critical for secret security?
Show Answer
Answer

It encrypts all data stored in etcd using a KMS plugin. Without it, anyone with filesystem access to the etcd data directory can read Secrets in plain text. Enable it via --encryption-provider-config on the API server. This is a cluster-level security MUST.

Q9
How does ESO handle secret rotation? Does it auto-refresh K8s Secrets?
Show Answer
Answer

Yes. ESO periodically reconciles (default: every 1 hour, configurable). If the external secret value changes (e.g., after rotation), ESO detects the difference and updates the K8s Secret. Pods need to restart or reload to pick up the new value.

Q10
You're building the anihpj platform. Which secret management strategy would you recommend and why?
Show Answer
Answer

ESO + cloud secret manager (AWS/GCP/Azure). Store DB passwords, API keys, and TLS certs in the cloud secret manager. ESO syncs them to K8s Secrets automatically. Enable etcd encryption at rest. Use Sealed Secrets or SOPS for any secrets that MUST be in Git (like ArgoCD credentials). This gives you defense in depth with manageable complexity.

Chapter 13: Observability Fundamentals — The Three Pillars

Observability is your platform's nervous system — it tells you what's happening inside every service, every node, and every request. As a CNPE, you're responsible for implementing complete visibility across the entire platform: Metrics, Logs, and Traces. If you can't see it, you can't manage it.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~2 hours 📊 Observability 📊 Domain 4 (20%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ The difference between Monitoring and Observability
  • ✅ The Three Pillars: Metrics (WHAT), Logs (WHERE), Traces (WHY)
  • ✅ SLIs, SLOs, SLAs, and Error Budgets — measuring reliability
  • ✅ OpenTelemetry — the universal standard for instrumentation
  • ✅ Choosing the right observability stack for your platform
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
📊 Observability & Operations 20%

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — Observability

The CNPE exam expects you to implement observability, not just understand it. You may be asked to deploy Prometheus + Grafana, configure alerts in Alertmanager, or set up Loki for log aggregation. Know the difference between SLI, SLO, and SLA — this is a frequently tested concept. Also expect questions about OpenTelemetry as the emerging standard for instrumentation.

13.1 What is Observability?

Monitoring tells you WHAT is wrong (CPU is at 95%). Observability tells you WHY it's wrong (a database query is slow because a missing index causes full table scans). Observability lets you ask arbitrary questions about your system without needing to deploy new code — it's the difference between seeing a symptom and understanding the root cause.

Monitoring vs Observability — The Key Differences

AspectMonitoringObservability
QuestionAnswers known questionsAnswers ANY question (even unanticipated)
DataPredefined metrics & dashboardsMetrics + Logs + Traces = rich context
Setup"I'll monitor CPU, memory, 500 errors"Instrument once, ask later
Debugging"CPU is high — something is wrong""Request abc123 timed out because Postgres query took 28s due to missing index"
AnalogyDashboard in your car (speed, fuel)Black box flight recorder (full history)

The Three Pillars of Observability

  ┌──────────────────┐  ┌──────────────────┐  ┌──────────────────┐
  │   📊 METRICS     │  │   📋 LOGS        │  │  🔍 TRACES       │
  │                  │  │                  │  │                  │
  │ CPU: 67%         │  │ [ERROR] DB conn  │  │  request         │
  │ Mem: 512Mi       │  │ timeout at       │  │  ┌──────┐       │
  │ Req/s: 450       │  │ 10:32:15 UTC     │  │  │nginx │       │
  │ Err rate: 2%     │  │ [INFO] Pod       │  │  └──┬───┘       │
  │                  │  │ restarted on     │  │     │           │
  │ Aggregated       │  │ node-3           │  │  ┌──▼───┐       │
  │ numeric data     │  │                  │  │  │anihpj│       │
  │ over time        │  │ Timestamped      │  │  └──┬───┘       │
  │                  │  │ text records     │  │     │           │
  │ Answers:         │  │                  │  │  ┌──▼───┐       │
  │ WHAT & WHEN      │  │ Answers:         │  │  │  DB  │       │
  │                  │  │ WHAT & WHERE     │  │  └──────┘       │
  │ Prometheus       │  │                  │  │                  │
  │                  │  │ Loki/ELK         │  │ Answers:         │
  │                  │  │                  │  │ WHY & HOW LONG   │
  │                  │  │                  │  │                  │
  │                  │  │                  │  │ Tempo/Jaeger     │
  └──────────────────┘  └──────────────────┘  └──────────────────┘

📊 Metrics (Prometheus)

Metrics are numeric measurements collected over time — CPU usage, request count, error rate, latency percentiles. They're lightweight, cheap to store, and perfect for alerting and dashboards. Prometheus is the CNCF-standard metrics system. Metrics answer: "How many? How fast? How often?"

📋 Logs (Loki)

Logs are timestamped text records of events — "User 42 created a job posting at 10:32:15". They're verbose, moderately expensive to store, and essential for debugging specific issues. Grafana Loki stores logs efficiently by indexing only labels (like Prometheus for logs). Logs answer: "What exactly happened at 10:32:15?"

🔍 Traces (Tempo)

Traces follow a single request as it travels through multiple services — nginx → anihpj → PostgreSQL → Redis. Each service adds a "span" to the trace. Traces show you exactly where time is spent and where failures occur. Grafana Tempo is the CNCF-standard tracing backend. Traces answer: "Why is this request slow? Where did it fail?"

📖 MELT — The Modern Acronym

The observability industry now uses MELT: Metrics, Events, Logs, Traces. Events are discrete occurrences (deployment happened, config changed, autoscaler fired). They provide crucial context that bridges metrics, logs, and traces — especially useful for correlating infrastructure changes with application behavior.

13.2 SLIs, SLOs, SLAs, and Error Budgets

These are the building blocks of measuring reliability:

TermDefinitionExample for anihpj
SLI (Service Level Indicator)WHAT you measureRequest latency p99, error rate, uptime %
SLO (Service Level Objective)The TARGET you commit top99 latency < 200ms, error rate < 1%
SLA (Service Level Agreement)CONTRACT with consequences99.9% uptime or refund 10% of fees
Error Budget1 − SLO = allowed unreliability0.1% downtime = 43 min/month allowed

Platform SLO Dashboard — What to Measure for anihpj

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │              PLATFORM HEALTH DASHBOARD                            │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                                                  │
  │  SERVICE         SLI                  SLO         STATUS         │
  │  ─────────────── ──────────────────── ─────────── ────────       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  anihpj API      Request Latency      p99 < 200ms  ✅ 156ms      │
  │  anihpj API      Availability          99.9%        ✅ 99.95%     │
  │  anihpj API      Error Rate            < 1%         ✅ 0.3%      │
  │  ─────────────── ──────────────────── ─────────── ────────       │
  │  jobpost DB      Query Latency        p99 < 50ms   ✅ 32ms       │
  │  jobpost DB      Availability          99.95%       ✅ 99.99%     │
  │  ─────────────── ──────────────────── ─────────── ────────       │
  │  Redis Cache     Hit Rate              > 95%        ⚠️ 92%       │
  │  Redis Cache     Latency               p99 < 5ms   ✅ 3ms        │
  │  ─────────────── ──────────────────── ─────────── ────────       │
  │  K8s API Server  Request Latency      p99 < 1s    ✅ 0.4s        │
  │  etcd            Disk Sync Duration    p99 < 10ms  ✅ 6ms        │
  │  Node CPU        Utilization            < 80%       ✅ 45%        │
  │  Node Memory     Utilization            < 80%       ✅ 62%        │
  │                                                                  │
  │  🔴 ERROR BUDGET BURN: Redis Cache hit rate below SLO           │
  │     → Cache TTL may be too short → Investigate                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
💡 Error Budgets — Your Release Safety Net

If your SLO is 99.9% uptime, your error budget is 0.1% = ~43 minutes of allowed downtime per month. When you've "burnt" your error budget (too many incidents), you freeze all feature releases and focus on reliability. This creates a healthy tension between "move fast" (devs) and "keep it stable" (platform team). Google's SRE book calls this the most important concept in production engineering.

📖 Real-World SLO: anihpj Job Post API

You define an SLO for the anihpj job posting endpoint (POST /api/jobs/):

SLI: p99 latency measured over a 28-day rolling window.
SLO: 99% of requests complete in under 200ms over 28 days.
Error Budget: 1% of requests can exceed 200ms = ~40,000 slow requests/month (at 100 req/s).

This means: if you burn through those 40,000 slow requests early in the month, the platform team freezes all new feature deploys and the dev team must fix performance before shipping anything new. This is how SLOs align incentives between platform and dev teams.

13.3 OpenTelemetry — The Universal Standard

OpenTelemetry (OTel) is a CNCF project that provides vendor-neutral APIs, SDKs, and tools for instrumenting applications. Instead of using Datadog's library, then switching to Grafana's library when you change vendors, you use OTel once — and export to any backend.

OpenTelemetry Architecture

  ┌──────────────┐    ┌──────────────┐    ┌──────────────┐
  │ anihpj       │    │ jobpost      │    │ Redis        │
  │ (Django)     │    │ (Worker)     │    │ (Cache)      │
  │ OTel SDK     │    │ OTel SDK     │    │ OTel SDK     │
  │ auto-instr.  │    │ auto-instr.  │    │ auto-instr.  │
  └──────┬───────┘    └──────┬───────┘    └──────┬───────┘
         │                   │                   │
         │  OTLP Protocol    │                   │
         └───────────────────┼───────────────────┘
                             │
                             ▼
              ┌──────────────────────────────┐
              │    OTel Collector            │
              │    ┌──────────────────────┐  │
              │    │ Receivers  │ Process │  │
              │    │ (OTLP)     │ (batch, │  │
              │    │            │ filter, │  │
              │    │            │ enrich) │  │
              │    └──────────────┬───────┘  │
              │                   │          │
              │    ┌──────────────▼───────┐  │
              │    │ Exporters            │  │
              │    │ → Prometheus (metrics)│  │
              │    │ → Loki (logs)        │  │
              │    │ → Tempo (traces)     │  │
              │    └──────────────────────┘  │
              └──────────────────────────────┘

Auto-Instrumentation — Zero-Code Observability

OTel supports auto-instrumentation for Python, Java, Node.js, Go, and more. For anihpj (Django), you install opentelemetry-instrumentation-django and add a few lines of config — OTel automatically traces every HTTP request, database query, and Redis call without modifying your application code.

# Instrument anihpj Django with OpenTelemetry
pip install opentelemetry-distro[otlp] opentelemetry-instrumentation-django
opentelemetry-bootstrap --action=install
# Then run Django with auto-instrumentation:
opentelemetry-instrument python manage.py runserver

📝 Chapter 13 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What is the difference between Monitoring and Observability?
Show Answer
Answer

Monitoring tells you WHAT is wrong (CPU is high, error rate spiked) using predefined metrics and dashboards. Observability lets you ask NEW questions without deploying new code — it combines metrics, logs, and traces so you can understand WHY something is wrong. Monitoring is a subset of observability.

Q2
What are the Three Pillars of Observability? What question does each answer?
Show Answer
Answer

Metrics: Numeric data over time — "How many? How fast?" (Prometheus). Logs: Timestamped text records — "What exactly happened?" (Loki). Traces: Request flow across services — "Why is it slow? Where did it fail?" (Tempo). All three together give you complete visibility.

Q3
Define SLI, SLO, and SLA. Give an example for anihpj.
Show Answer
Answer

SLI: What you measure (anihpj API request latency). SLO: Target you commit to (p99 latency < 200ms). SLA: Contract with consequences (99.9% uptime or pay penalty). SLOs are internal goals; SLAs are external promises. As a CNPE, you primarily manage SLOs.

Q4
What is an Error Budget? If your SLO is 99.9% uptime, how much downtime is allowed per month?
Show Answer
Answer

Error Budget = 1 − SLO = 0.1% = ~43 minutes per month (30 days × 24 hours × 60 minutes × 0.001). This is your "allowed unreliability." Use it for: canary releases, chaos experiments, and planned maintenance. When it's burnt, freeze releases and invest in reliability.

Q5
What is OpenTelemetry? Why is it important for observability?
Show Answer
Answer

OpenTelemetry is a vendor-neutral CNCF project that standardizes how applications emit telemetry data (metrics, logs, traces). It means you instrument your code ONCE with OTel, and export to ANY backend (Prometheus, Datadog, Grafana Cloud). No vendor lock-in for observability.

Q6
What is the OTel Collector? What are its three main components?
Show Answer
Answer

The OTel Collector is a standalone proxy that receives, processes, and exports telemetry data. Three components: Receivers (ingest OTLP, Jaeger, Prometheus data), Processors (batch, filter, enrich, sample data), Exporters (send to Prometheus, Loki, Tempo, Datadog, etc.).

Q7
Compare the Grafana LGTM stack with the ELK stack. When would you choose each?
Show Answer
Answer

LGTM (Loki, Grafana, Tempo, Mimir): Label-based, cost-efficient, Prometheus-compatible, designed for K8s. Best for cloud-native platforms. ELK: Full-text search on logs, heavier resource usage, excellent for log-heavy security/compliance use cases. Most CNPEs choose LGTM for K8s-native observability.

Q8
What are the 4 levels of the Observability Maturity Model? Where should CNPEs aim?
Show Answer
Answer

Level 1 (Reactive): Basic metrics, manual debugging. Level 2 (Proactive): SLO-based alerting, structured logs. Level 3 (Predictive): Distributed traces, anomaly detection, error budgets. Level 4 (Self-Healing): Auto-remediation, chaos engineering. CNPEs should aim for Level 2-3 for exam readiness.

Q9
How does auto-instrumentation work with OpenTelemetry? Does it require code changes?
Show Answer
Answer

Auto-instrumentation hooks into language runtimes (Python, Java, Node.js) to automatically trace HTTP requests, DB queries, and message queues — no code changes needed. For Django: install opentelemetry-instrumentation-django, run opentelemetry-instrument python manage.py runserver. OTel traces every request automatically.

Q10
You're building the anihpj observability stack. Your team has no budget for SaaS. What do you deploy and in what order?
Show Answer
Answer

Deploy in this order: (1) Prometheus + Grafana for metrics and dashboards (day 1). (2) Alertmanager for alerting (day 2). (3) Loki + Promtail for log aggregation (week 1). (4) Tempo + OTel Collector for distributed tracing (week 2). Start with metrics, add logs, then traces. The LGTM stack is free, open-source, and K8s-native.

Chapter 14: Metrics & Monitoring with Prometheus & Grafana

Prometheus is the de-facto standard for K8s monitoring — it's a CNCF graduated project and the metrics backbone of every serious platform. Grafana turns those metrics into beautiful, actionable dashboards. Together they form the core of your platform's observability. This is 20% of the CNPE exam.

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — Prometheus & Grafana

This chapter is heavily tested on the CNPE exam. Expect hands-on tasks: deploy Prometheus via Helm, write PromQL queries to answer specific questions ("What's the p99 latency of the anihpj API?"), configure Alertmanager rules for platform alerts, and build Grafana dashboards. Key concepts to master: rate() vs irate(), Histogram vs Summary, RED vs USE methods, and how Alertmanager grouping prevents alert storms. The exam tests practical observability — not just theory.

14.1 Prometheus Deep Dive

Prometheus uses a pull-based model: it actively scrapes HTTP endpoints on your services to collect metrics. Unlike push-based systems (where services send data), Prometheus reaches out and pulls — this means Prometheus knows exactly what's up and what's down based on whether scraping succeeds. Its data model is simple but powerful: every metric has a name (e.g., http_requests_total) and labels — key-value pairs that add dimensions (e.g., {job="anihpj", method="GET", status="200"}). Labels are what make PromQL queries so flexible — you can slice and dice metrics across any dimension, like "show me the error rate for POST requests to /api/jobs/ on the production cluster."

Prometheus Architecture

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     PROMETHEUS ECOSYSTEM                          │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │              PROMETHEUS SERVER                              │ │
  │  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌────────────────────────┐   │ │
  │  │  │ Retrieval│  │   TSDB   │  │   HTTP Server          │   │ │
  │  │  │ (scraper)│──▶│(time-ser │──▶│   (PromQL API + UI)    │   │ │
  │  │  │          │  │ ies DB)  │  │                        │   │ │
  │  │  └────┬─────┘  └──────────┘  └────────────────────────┘   │ │
  │  └───────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │          │ Pulls metrics every 15-60s                            │
  │          ▼                                                       │
  │  ┌───────────────┐  ┌───────────────┐  ┌───────────────┐        │
  │  │ Node Exporter │  │ Kube-State-   │  │ anihpj        │        │
  │  │ (CPU/Mem/Disk)│  │ Metrics (K8s) │  │ /metrics      │        │
  │  │ :9100/metrics │  │ :8080/metrics │  │ :8000/metrics │        │
  │  └───────────────┘  └───────────────┘  └───────────────┘        │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌───────────────┐                         ┌───────────────┐     │
  │  │ Alertmanager  │ ◀── Alerts from Prom ── │ Grafana       │     │
  │  │ → Slack/Pager │                         │ → Dashboards  │     │
  │  └───────────────┘                         └───────────────┘     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Metric Types — The Four Fundamental Types

TypeWhat It IsExampleNever Does
CounterOnly goes up (or resets to 0)http_requests_totalGo down
GaugeGoes up AND downnode_memory_usage_bytesN/A — flexible
HistogramObservations in bucketshttp_request_duration_secondsN/A — pre-defined buckets
SummaryQuantiles on client sidehttp_request_duration_quantileBe aggregated across instances
📖 Histogram vs Summary — Which to Choose?

Histograms are aggregatable across instances (you can sum bucket counts from multiple pods) and are the standard choice for K8s. Summaries compute quantiles on the client side but cannot be aggregated — if you have 3 pods, you can't average their p99 values. For platform monitoring, always use Histograms.

14.2 PromQL — Querying Like a Pro

PromQL (Prometheus Query Language) is how you extract meaning from raw metrics. Mastering these queries is essential for both the CNPE exam and real-world platform operations.

Instant Vector vs Range Vector

TypeSyntaxReturns
Instant Vectorhttp_requests_totalCurrent value for each label set
Range Vectorhttp_requests_total[5m]All values in the last 5 minutes

Essential PromQL Functions

FunctionWhat It DoesExample
rate(counter[5m])Per-second rate of increase (smooth)rate(http_requests_total[5m])
irate(counter[5m])Instant rate (last 2 samples, spiky)irate(http_requests_total[5m])
increase(counter[5m])Total increase over the rangeincrease(http_requests_total[5m])
histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(...[5m]))p99 latency from histogramhistogram_quantile(0.99, rate(http_duration_bucket[5m]))
avg_over_time(gauge[5m])Average of gauge over rangeavg_over_time(node_cpu_usage[5m])

Label Matching — Filtering Your Metrics

# Exact match
http_requests_total{job="anihpj", status="500"}

# Negative match (does not equal)
http_requests_total{job!="anihpj"}

# Regex match
http_requests_total{path=~"/api/.*"}

# Negative regex match
http_requests_total{path!~"/healthz|/readyz"}

# Aggregation — sum by label
sum(rate(http_requests_total[5m])) by (job, status)

14.3 Alerting with Alertmanager

Alertmanager handles alerts from Prometheus — it deduplicates, groups, and routes them to the right team via Slack, PagerDuty, Opsgenie, email, or webhook. Without Alertmanager, every Prometheus server fires its own alerts, causing duplicate pages.

Alert Rules — When to Fire

# Critical alert: anihpj API error rate is too high
groups:
  - name: anihpj-critical
    rules:
      - alert: HighErrorRate
        expr: |
          sum(rate(http_requests_total{job="anihpj",status=~"5.."}[5m]))
          /
          sum(rate(http_requests_total{job="anihpj"}[5m])) > 0.05
        for: 5m  # Must be true for 5 minutes before firing
        labels:
          severity: critical
        annotations:
          summary: "anihpj error rate > 5%"
          description: "Error rate is {{ $value | humanizePercentage }}"

      - alert: HighLatency
        expr: |
          histogram_quantile(0.99,
            rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{job="anihpj"}[5m])
          ) > 0.2
        for: 10m
        labels:
          severity: warning
        annotations:
          summary: "anihpj p99 latency > 200ms"

Alert Severity Levels — CNPE Standard

SeverityMeaningResponseExample
🔴 CriticalUser-facing outageImmediate page (PagerDuty)anihpj API returns 500 errors
🟡 WarningDegraded but workingPage during business hoursp99 latency > 200ms
🔵 InfoFYI, no action neededSlack notification onlyDeployment completed

Alert Receivers — Where Alerts Go

ReceiverBest ForConfiguration
PagerDutyOn-call rotation, critical alertsAPI key + service integration
SlackTeam notifications, warningsWebhook URL + channel
OpsgenieOn-call scheduling (Atlassian)API key + team routing
EmailDaily summaries, non-urgentSMTP config or sendmail
WebhookCustom integrations (remediation)HTTP POST to any URL
📖 Alert Routing — Getting the Right Alert to the Right Team

Alertmanager uses routing trees: all alerts enter at the top, then match against label matchers. Example: alerts with severity="critical" and team="platform" → PagerDuty for the platform on-call. Alerts with severity="warning" → Slack #platform-alerts. Grouping prevents alert storms — 10 pods all failing generates ONE grouped notification, not 10.

14.4 Grafana — Visualization & Dashboards

Grafana connects to Prometheus (and 50+ other data sources) to create beautiful, interactive dashboards. As a CNPE, you'll provision dashboards as code so every cluster boots with the same monitoring views.

Grafana Architecture — Data Sources, Dashboards, Panels

Grafana connects to data sources (Prometheus, Loki, Tempo, Elasticsearch, 50+ others) and organizes them into dashboards containing panels (individual charts, gauges, tables, heatmaps). As a CNPE, you'll provision dashboards as code via ConfigMaps so every cluster boots with the same views.

💡 Dashboards as Code — GitOps for Monitoring

Provision Grafana dashboards via ConfigMaps or the Grafana Helm chart's dashboardProviders and dashboards values. Store dashboard JSON in Git, deploy via ArgoCD. When you create a new cluster, your "anihpj Overview", "K8s Cluster Health", and "Node Metrics" dashboards appear automatically — no manual clicking in the Grafana UI.

14.5 Platform Health Metrics — What to Monitor at Every Layer

LayerWhat to MonitorWhy It MattersPrometheus Source
Control PlaneAPI server latency, etcd disk sync, scheduler queue depthControl plane is your brain — if it's slow, everything is slowkube-apiserver, etcd metrics endpoints
