📖 DORA Metrics — Measuring Platform Success

The CNPE exam may reference DORA metrics (DevOps Research and Assessment): Deployment Frequency (how often you deploy), Lead Time for Changes (commit → production), Change Failure Rate (% of deploys causing incidents), Time to Restore Service (MTTR). These measure not just system health, but platform engineering effectiveness.

📝 Chapter 14 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
How does Prometheus collect metrics — push or pull? Why is this important?
Show Answer
Answer

Prometheus uses a pull model — it actively scrapes HTTP /metrics endpoints on targets. This means Prometheus knows which targets are UP or DOWN based on scrape success. It also simplifies service discovery in K8s — Prometheus auto-discovers pods via K8s API and scrapes them directly.

Q2
What are the 4 Prometheus metric types? When would you use each?
Show Answer
Answer

Counter: Only increases — use for request counts, error counts. Gauge: Up and down — use for CPU, memory, temperature. Histogram: Bucketed observations — use for latency distributions (aggregatable). Summary: Client-side quantiles — use when you can't use Histograms (rare in K8s).

Q3
What's the difference between rate() and irate()? When should you use each?
Show Answer
Answer

rate(): Average per-second rate over the range window (smooth, good for dashboards and alerting). irate(): Instant rate using only the last 2 samples (spiky, good for graphing volatile counters). For alerting, always use rate() — irate() can miss spikes.

Q4
Write a PromQL query to get the p99 latency for the anihpj service over the last 5 minutes.
Show Answer
Answer

histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{job="anihpj"}[5m])). This calculates the 99th percentile from a Histogram metric's buckets, using rate() to convert the cumulative counters to per-second rates over 5 minutes.

Q5
How does Alertmanager prevent duplicate alerts? What is alert grouping?
Show Answer
Answer

Deduplication: Alertmanager deduplicates identical alerts from multiple Prometheus servers. Grouping: Multiple similar alerts (e.g., 10 pods all failing) are grouped into ONE notification. This prevents alert storms — instead of 10 PagerDuty pages, you get 1 grouped alert with details.

Q6
What is the RED method? Write PromQL for each of the 3 metrics for anihpj.
Show Answer
Answer

Rate: rate(http_requests_total{job="anihpj"}[5m]) — requests/sec. Errors: rate(http_requests_total{job="anihpj",status=~"5.."}[5m]) — failed requests/sec. Duration: histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{job="anihpj"}[5m])) — p99 latency. RED is for service-level monitoring.

Q7
What is the difference between the RED method and the USE method?
Show Answer
Answer

RED (Rate, Errors, Duration): For services — measures user-facing behavior. USE (Utilization, Saturation, Errors): For infrastructure — measures resource health (CPU, memory, disk). Use RED for anihpj API dashboards, USE for Node and K8s infrastructure dashboards.

Q8
What are DORA metrics? Name all four and their ideal targets.
Show Answer
Answer

Deployment Frequency: Multiple per day (elite). Lead Time for Changes: Less than 1 hour (elite). Change Failure Rate: 0-15%. Time to Restore Service: Less than 1 hour. These measure platform engineering effectiveness — not just whether your servers are up, but whether your platform enables developer velocity.

Q9
How do you provision Grafana dashboards as code for GitOps?
Show Answer
Answer

Store dashboard JSON in Git, deploy via the Grafana Helm chart's dashboardProviders and dashboards config. When ArgoCD syncs, Grafana auto-loads the dashboards. Example: dashboards.default.anihpj-overview.json in a ConfigMap → Grafana picks it up. Any cluster you deploy gets the same dashboards automatically.

Q10
You get paged at 3AM: "anihpj error rate > 5%". Walk through your debugging process using Prometheus + Grafana.
Show Answer
Answer

1. Open Grafana → anihpj RED dashboard → confirm error spike. 2. Check which endpoints are failing (add path label). 3. Check if a deployment happened recently (correlate with deploy time). 4. Check DB metrics — is PostgreSQL up? Slow queries? 5. Check node metrics — is the node under resource pressure? 6. Check Loki logs for the failing pods around the error time. 7. Identify root cause, fix, verify RED dashboard returns green.

Chapter 15: Logging and Tracing — Completing the Observability Picture

Metrics tell you what is wrong. Logs tell you exactly what happened. Traces tell you where it went wrong across services. Together with metrics, they form the complete observability trifecta. As a CNPE, you'll deploy centralized logging and distributed tracing so developers never need to SSH into pods to read logs.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~2 hours 📊 Observability 📊 Domain 4 (20%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Centralized logging architecture: collect → ship → store → search
  • ✅ Grafana Loki: label-based, cost-efficient log storage
  • ✅ Distributed tracing: spans, traces, context propagation
  • ✅ Correlating metrics, logs, and traces for incident debugging
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
📊 Observability & Operations 20%

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — Logging & Tracing

The CNPE exam tests your ability to deploy and configure Loki (via Helm), write LogQL queries to find specific log patterns, and understand distributed tracing concepts. Key exam topics: why Loki only indexes labels (cost efficiency), LogQL rate() for counting log lines (like PromQL for logs), head vs tail sampling for traces, and the exemplar-based metric→trace correlation. You may also be asked to walk through a debugging scenario using all three pillars.

15.1 Centralized Logging Architecture

Without centralized logging, debugging means kubectl logs into individual pods — and ephemeral pod logs disappear when the pod restarts. Centralized logging solves this with a pipeline: collect → ship → store → search.

Centralized Logging Pipeline

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                   LOGGING PIPELINE                                │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────┐    ┌──────────────┐    ┌──────────────┐       │
  │  │  COLLECT     │    │   SHIP       │    │   STORE      │       │
  │  │              │    │              │    │              │       │
  │  │ ┌──────────┐ │    │ ┌──────────┐ │    │ ┌──────────┐ │       │
  │  │ │Fluent Bit│ │───▶│ │Fluentd   │ │───▶│ │Loki      │ │       │
  │  │ │Promtail  │ │    │ │Vector    │ │    │ │Elastic-  │ │       │
  │  │ │Vector    │ │    │ │Logstash  │ │    │ │search    │ │       │
  │  │ └──────────┘ │    │ └──────────┘ │    │ └──────────┘ │       │
  │  │              │    │              │    │              │       │
  │  │ Read pod     │    │ Buffer,      │    │ Indexed &     │       │
  │  │ stdout/stderr│    │ enrich,      │    │ searchable    │       │
  │  │ and log files│    │ route logs   │    │               │       │
  │  └──────────────┘    └──────────────┘    └──────────────┘       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────┐                                                │
  │  │  SEARCH/VIS  │                                                │
  │  │ ┌──────────┐ │                                                │
  │  │ │Grafana   │ │  ← Unified view with your metrics & traces     │
  │  │ │Kibana    │ │                                                │
  │  │ └──────────┘ │                                                │
  │  └──────────────┘                                                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Log Collectors & Storage — Full Comparison

ComponentTypeResource UsageBest ForCNPE Pick?
Fluent BitCollectorVery low (C, < 10MB)K8s DaemonSet — read all pod logs✅ Primary
PromtailCollectorLow (Go)Loki-native, auto-labels from K8s✅ With Loki
VectorCollector/AggregatorLow (Rust)High performance, transforms🟡 Emerging
FluentdAggregatorHigher (Ruby, ~100MB)Rich plugin ecosystem, buffering🟡 Aggregator

Log Storage — Where Logs Live

StorageIndexingCostBest For
LokiLabels only (like Prometheus)Low — object storage (S3)K8s-native, metrics-aligned
ElasticsearchFull-text index on every wordHigh — lots of CPU/RAM/diskFull-text search, compliance
OpenSearchFull-text index (AWS fork of ES)High — managed or self-hostedAWS-native shops, ES API compatible
📖 Why Centralized Logging Matters

Without centralized logging: (1) Pod logs disappear when pods restart, (2) You SSH into 5 different pods to piece together what happened, (3) No correlation between error logs and metrics spikes. With centralized logging: search ALL pod logs from a single UI, correlate log timestamps with Prometheus metrics, and never SSH into a pod just to read logs again.

15.2 Grafana Loki — Prometheus for Logs

Loki is designed like Prometheus but for logs: it indexes only labels (not the full text of every log line), making it extremely cost-efficient. Logs are stored as compressed chunks in object storage (S3, GCS). Full-text search is available but not indexed — it's done on-demand.

LogQL — Query Language for Loki

# LogQL queries — same vibe as PromQL

# All logs from anihpj pods in the last 5 minutes
{job="anihpj"}

# Only ERROR-level logs
{job="anihpj"} |= "ERROR"

# Logs NOT matching a pattern
{job="anihpj"} != "health check"

# Regex filter
{job="anihpj"} |~ "status=[45][0-9]{2}"

# Count log lines over time (like Prometheus rate!)
rate({job="anihpj"} |= "ERROR" [5m])

# Find logs with specific trace ID (correlate with Tempo!)
{job="anihpj"} |= "trace_id=abc123def456"

Setting Up Loki for anihpj (via Helm)

# Install the Loki stack (Loki + Promtail + Grafana integration)
helm upgrade --install loki grafana/loki-stack \
  --namespace loki --create-namespace \
  --set loki.persistence.enabled=true \
  --set loki.persistence.size=50Gi \
  --set promtail.tolerations[0].operator=Exists

# Promtail runs as DaemonSet — automatically reads all pod logs
# Loki stores logs in object storage (S3/GCS) or local PVC
# Grafana connects to Loki as a data source — one UI for everything
🔍 Debugging with LogQL — A Real Example

A developer reports: "anihpj returns 500 errors whenever someone posts a job with more than 10 skills." Your debugging flow with LogQL:

Step 1: {job="anihpj"} |= "500" → finds the error log line.
Step 2: Notice trace ID abc123 in the log. Jump to Tempo → see PostgreSQL INSERT failing with "value too long for type varchar(255)".
Step 3: Count how often: rate({job="anihpj"} |~ "value too long" [1h]) → 47 occurrences in the last hour.
Step 4: Fix the DB schema (TEXT instead of VARCHAR), deploy, verify: {job="anihpj"} |~ "value too long" returns zero results.

⏱️ Total debugging time: ~3 minutes with Loki + Tempo. Without them: SSH into 5 pods, grep log files, piece together manually — 20+ minutes.

15.3 Distributed Tracing — Following the Request Trail

When a user clicks "Create Job Posting" in anihpj, the request might flow: nginx → anihpj Django → PostgreSQL → Redis → back to user. Distributed tracing follows this entire journey, showing you exactly how long each step took and where failures occur.

Trace Waterfall — anihpj "Create Job" Request

  TRACE: create_job_abc123  (Total: 342ms)

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐  nginx         │
  │  │ ████ 4ms                                 │  proxy         │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────┘                │
  │       │                                                     │
  │       ▼                                                     │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐  anihpj        │
  │  │ ██████████████████████████████ 280ms      │  Django        │
  │  │  ├── ████████████ 120ms (validate form)   │                │
  │  │  ├── ████████████████████ 150ms (save DB) │                │
  │  │  └── ██ 10ms (render response)           │                │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────┘                │
  │       │                                                     │
  │       ├──────────────────┐                                  │
  │       ▼                  ▼                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────┐  ┌──────────────────┐                │
  │  │ ██████ 45ms       │  │ ███ 8ms           │                │
  │  │ PostgreSQL        │  │ Redis (cache)     │                │
  │  │ INSERT INTO jobs  │  │ SET job:abc123    │                │
  │  └──────────────────┘  └──────────────────┘                │
  │                                                              │
  │  🔍 BOTTLENECK: PostgreSQL INSERT takes 45ms                │
  │     → Check if index is missing on jobs.skills FK           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Key Tracing Concepts

ConceptDefinitionExample
SpanA single unit of work"PostgreSQL INSERT" span = 45ms
TraceCollection of spans for one requestcreate_job_abc123 = nginx + Django + DB + Redis
Context PropagationPassing trace ID across servicesHTTP header: traceparent: 00-abc123-def456-01
SamplingOnly record X% of tracesHead-based (at start) or tail-based (keep errors, sample successes)

Grafana Tempo — Tracing That Just Works

Tempo is designed for minimal operational complexity: no indexing, no Elasticsearch, just object storage. It uses the trace ID → object storage lookup model — you provide the trace ID (from logs or exemplars), and Tempo retrieves the full trace. This makes it extremely cheap to operate.

Instrumenting anihpj Django for Distributed Tracing

With OpenTelemetry, instrumenting Django for tracing is straightforward. The OTel Django instrumentation automatically creates spans for every HTTP request, database query, and cache operation — no manual code changes needed for basic tracing.

# Install OTel packages for Django tracing
pip install opentelemetry-distro[otlp]
pip install opentelemetry-instrumentation-django
pip install opentelemetry-instrumentation-psycopg2  # PostgreSQL
pip install opentelemetry-instrumentation-redis      # Redis cache

# settings.py — minimal config
OTEL_SERVICE_NAME = "anihpj"
OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT = "http://otel-collector:4317"

# Run Django with auto-instrumentation
opentelemetry-instrument \
  --traces_exporter otlp \
  --metrics_exporter otlp \
  python manage.py runserver

# Result: Every HTTP request → automatically traced!
# Spans: nginx → Django view → DB query → Redis cache → response
📖 Span Attributes — The Details That Matter

OTel automatically adds useful attributes to each span: http.method (GET/POST), http.status_code (200/500), db.statement (the SQL query), db.system (postgresql). You can add custom attributes: span.set_attribute("user.id", request.user.id). These attributes make traces searchable and meaningful during debugging.

15.4 Correlating Metrics, Logs, and Traces — The Power of Three

The real magic happens when you jump seamlessly between the three pillars. Grafana's Explore view lets you start from a metric spike, drill into related logs, and from a log line jump directly to the full distributed trace.

Incident Debugging Walkthrough — The Complete Flow

  🚨 ALERT: anihpj API p99 latency > 200ms

  STEP 1: METRICS (Grafana + Prometheus)
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Open anihpj RED dashboard → p99 latency spiked at 10:32   │
  │ → Drill down: which endpoint? → POST /api/jobs/ is slow   │
  │ → Which pod? → pod-7 is 5× slower than pod-6              │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                          │
                          ▼
  STEP 2: LOGS (Grafana + Loki)
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Search logs for pod-7 at 10:32:                            │
  │ {pod="anihpj-7"} |= "ERROR"                                │
  │ → [ERROR] PostgreSQL query timeout after 30s               │
  │ → trace_id=abc123 found in log line                        │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                          │
                          ▼
  STEP 3: TRACES (Grafana + Tempo)
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Open trace abc123 in Tempo:                                │
  │ → PostgreSQL INSERT span = 28 SECONDS (not ms!)            │
  │ → Query: "INSERT INTO jobs ... WHERE skills IN (500 IDs)"  │
  │ → ROOT CAUSE: Missing index on jobs.skills FK              │
  │ → FIX: CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY idx_jobs_skills           │
  │ → Deploy fix → RED dashboard returns green                 │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ⏱️ Time to diagnose: 3 minutes (vs 30+ without correlation)

Exemplars — The Missing Link

Exemplars link specific metric data points to specific traces. When you see a latency spike on your Grafana dashboard, you can click the spike point and jump directly to the trace that caused it. This is the ultimate observability superpower — it closes the loop between metrics, logs, and traces.

💡 CNPE Golden Rule

Your platform isn't truly observable until a developer can go from alert → metric spike → log line → distributed trace → root cause in under 5 minutes — without SSH-ing into a single pod. This is the bar. Everything you deploy in Part 5 should serve this goal.

📝 Chapter 15 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
Why is centralized logging essential for Kubernetes? What problem does it solve?
Show Answer
Answer

K8s pod logs are ephemeral — they disappear when pods restart, get evicted, or are replaced by new replicas. Centralized logging ships logs off-node to persistent storage. It also enables searching ALL logs from a single UI, correlating logs with metrics, and never needing to kubectl logs individual pods.

Q2
What makes Loki different from Elasticsearch for log storage?
Show Answer
Answer

Loki only indexes labels (like Prometheus), not full-text content. This makes it dramatically cheaper to operate — logs are stored as compressed chunks in object storage (S3). Elasticsearch indexes every word, giving faster full-text search but requiring much more CPU, memory, and disk. Loki is the K8s-native choice.

Q3
What is a span? What is a trace? How do they relate?
Show Answer
Answer

A span is a single unit of work with a start time, duration, and metadata (e.g., "PostgreSQL query: 45ms"). A trace is a collection of spans that share the same trace ID, representing one complete request through the system. A trace answers: "What happened to THIS specific request?"

Q4
What is context propagation? How is it implemented?
Show Answer
Answer

Context propagation passes the trace ID and span ID across service boundaries so downstream services can add spans to the same trace. Implemented via HTTP headers: W3C Trace Context (traceparent: 00-traceId-spanId-flags). OTel SDKs handle this automatically — you just need to propagate the headers through your HTTP client.

Q5
What is the difference between head-based and tail-based sampling for traces?
Show Answer
Answer

Head-based: Sampling decision made at the START of a trace (e.g., sample 10% of all requests). Simpler but might miss rare errors. Tail-based: Sampling decision made at the END — you keep ALL traces in a buffer, then decide "keep errors, sample successes." More complex but ensures you never miss an error trace.

Q6
Write a LogQL query to find all ERROR logs from anihpj pods containing "database" in the last hour, and count them per minute.
Show Answer
Answer

Search: {job="anihpj"} |= "ERROR" |~ "(?i)database". Count rate: rate({job="anihpj"} |= "ERROR" |~ "(?i)database" [1h]). This shows you how many database errors per second, grouped by time — just like Prometheus rate() for metrics.

Q7
How does Grafana Tempo find a trace without indexing spans? Isn't that slow?
Show Answer
Answer

Tempo stores traces as blobs in object storage keyed by trace ID. You provide the trace ID (from logs, exemplars, or a metric), and Tempo does a direct lookup — no scanning, no indexing. It's fast because it's O(1) lookup by ID. The trade-off: you can't search traces by arbitrary attributes (that's what indexing-based systems do).

Q8
What are exemplars? How do they connect metrics to traces?
Show Answer
Answer

Exemplars attach a specific trace ID to a metric data point. When your Grafana dashboard shows a latency spike at 10:32:15, you can click the spike and jump directly to the exact trace that caused it. They're configured in Prometheus: --enable-feature=exemplar-storage. This closes the metrics→traces loop.

Q9
What collector would you use for K8s log aggregation and why?
Show Answer
Answer

Use Fluent Bit as a DaemonSet (collects all pod logs, extremely lightweight at ~10MB RAM) or Promtail if using Loki (auto-discovers K8s metadata, adds pod/namespace/labels automatically). Fluent Bit sends to Fluentd for buffering/enrichment, then to Loki/Elasticsearch for storage. Both are industry standard for K8s.

Q10
Walk through debugging an anihpj latency spike using all three pillars. What's your flow?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Metrics: Grafana RED dashboard → p99 spike at 10:32, POST /api/jobs/ affected. 2. Logs: Loki query {job="anihpj"} |= "ERROR" at 10:32 → "PostgreSQL timeout" with trace_id=abc123. 3. Traces: Open trace abc123 in Tempo → PostgreSQL INSERT span = 28s → missing index found. 4. Fix, deploy, verify. Total time: 3-5 minutes. Without observability: 30+ minutes of SSH + grep.

Chapter 16: Platform Security Fundamentals

Security is NOT an afterthought — it's a design constraint baked into every layer of your platform. As a CNPE, you own the security of the Cluster and Container layers, and you provide the tools that help developers secure the Code layer. This chapter covers the 4Cs, RBAC, Pod Security, Network Policies, and Supply Chain Security — 15% of the exam.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~2.5 hours 🔐 Security 🔐 Domain 5 (15%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ The 4Cs of Cloud Native Security: Cloud → Cluster → Container → Code
  • ✅ RBAC: Users, ServiceAccounts, Roles, ClusterRoles, Bindings
  • ✅ Pod Security Standards: Privileged, Baseline, Restricted
  • ✅ Network Policies: default-deny, podSelector, namespaceSelector
  • ✅ Supply Chain Security: image scanning, signing, SBOMs
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🔐 Security & Policy Enforcement 15%

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — Security

Security is hands-on on the CNPE exam. Expect tasks like: create a NetworkPolicy that only allows anihpj pods to talk to PostgreSQL, configure RBAC so developers can only deploy to their namespace, enforce PSS Restricted on all namespaces, or set up image signature verification with Kyverno. Least privilege and defense in depth are the guiding principles for every security decision.

16.1 The 4Cs of Cloud Native Security

Cloud Native security is a layered defense. You secure every layer because if one layer fails, the others still protect you. The 4Cs are: Cloud → Cluster → Container → Code. As a CNPE, you own layers 2 and 3, and enable layers 1 and 4.

The 4Cs — Platform Engineer's View

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
  │  │  CLOUD (or Datacenter) — Infrastructure Security           ││
  │  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  ││
  │  │  │ • IAM roles & policies (AWS IAM, GCP IAM, Azure AD)  │  ││
  │  │  │ • Network security groups / firewalls                │  ││
  │  │  │ • Encryption at rest (EBS, S3, Cloud Storage)        │  ││
  │  │  │ • API access controls & audit logging                │  ││
  │  │  │ • Provider: YOU manage this layer                    │  ││
  │  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  ││
  │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤│
  │  │  CLUSTER — Kubernetes Security                             ││
  │  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  ││
  │  │  │ • RBAC (Roles, ClusterRoles, Bindings)               │  ││
  │  │  │ • etcd encryption at rest                            │  ││
  │  │  │ • API server audit logging                           │  ││
  │  │  │ • NetworkPolicies (pod-level firewall)               │  ││
  │  │  │ • Pod Security Standards (PSS)                       │  ││
  │  │  │ • Admission Controllers (Kyverno, OPA)               │  ││
  │  │  │ 🎯 Domain 5 (15%): THIS is the CNPE security focus  │  ││
  │  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  ││
  │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤│
  │  │  CONTAINER — Image & Runtime Security                      ││
  │  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  ││
  │  │  │ • Non-root user (runAsUser: 1000)                    │  ││
  │  │  │ • Read-only root filesystem                          │  ││
  │  │  │ • Drop ALL capabilities, add only needed             │  ││
  │  │  │ • Image scanning (Trivy, Grype)                      │  ││
  │  │  │ • Image signing (cosign, Sigstore)                   │  ││
  │  │  │ • Minimal base images (distroless, scratch)          │  ││
  │  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  ││
  │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤│
  │  │  CODE — Application Security                               ││
  │  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  ││
  │  │  │ • SAST (Static Analysis: Semgrep, SonarQube)         │  ││
  │  │  │ • Dependency scanning (Dependabot, Snyk)             │  ││
  │  │  │ • Secret detection (gitleaks, truffleHog)            │  ││
  │  │  │ • Secure coding practices (Django security)          │  ││
  │  │  │ • Devs + Platform share responsibility               │  ││
  │  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  ││
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
  │                                                                  │
  │  🛡️ "A chain is only as strong as its weakest link"            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
📖 Shared Responsibility Model

Security in the cloud is a shared responsibility: the cloud provider secures the physical infrastructure (servers, networking, storage), while YOU secure everything running on top (IAM, cluster config, container images, code). In managed K8s (EKS, GKE, AKS), the provider secures the control plane; you secure worker nodes, pods, RBAC, and network policies. If a pod gets compromised because you ran it as root — that's on you, not the cloud provider.

☁️ Cloud Security

The cloud provider secures the physical infrastructure. YOU secure your account: IAM roles with least privilege, security groups as firewalls, encryption at rest for all storage, and CloudTrail/audit logging enabled everywhere. For anihpj: the EKS cluster should run in private subnets with only the API server exposed via a locked-down security group.

☸️ Cluster Security

This is the CNPE's primary security domain. You control who can access the K8s API (RBAC), what pods can do (PSS), which pods can talk to each other (NetworkPolicies), and what resources can be created (Admission Controllers). Every cluster must have etcd encryption at rest enabled, API audit logging configured, and RBAC following the principle of least privilege.

📦 Container Security

Containers should run as non-root users, with read-only root filesystems, and all Linux capabilities dropped except those explicitly needed. Images must be scanned for CVEs before deployment. Use minimal base images (distroless, scratch) to reduce attack surface. An anihpj container should NOT run as root — it should use runAsUser: 1000.

💻 Code Security

Security starts in the code: SAST scans (Semgrep for Python/Django), dependency vulnerability checks (Dependabot, pip-audit), and secret detection (gitleaks pre-commit hooks). The platform team provides these tools in the CI pipeline — developers get feedback before they even open a PR.

16.2 Kubernetes RBAC Deep Dive

RBAC (Role-Based Access Control) controls who can do what in your cluster. It answers: "Can this user create pods in the anihpj namespace?" or "Can this ServiceAccount read Secrets cluster-wide?"

RBAC — Subject → RoleBinding → Role → Permissions

  SUBJECT                    BINDING                     ROLE               PERMISSIONS
  ┌──────────────────┐     ┌──────────────────┐     ┌──────────────────┐  ┌────────────┐
  │ 👤 User (human)  │     │ RoleBinding      │     │ Role             │  │ get pods   │
  │ 🤖 ServiceAccount│────▶│ (or ClusterRole  │────▶│ (or ClusterRole) │─▶│ list pods  │
  │ 👥 Group         │     │  Binding)        │     │                  │  │ create pods│
  └──────────────────┘     └──────────────────┘     └──────────────────┘  │ delete pods│
                                                                          └────────────┘

Users vs ServiceAccounts — Who Can Do What?

AspectUser (Human)ServiceAccount (Machine)
IdentityAuthenticated via OIDC, X.509 certs, or webhookK8s-native identity — tied to a namespace
Use CasePlatform engineers running kubectlPods accessing the K8s API, CI pipelines, operators
Tokenkubeconfig with cert or tokenAuto-mounted token in /var/run/secrets/...
LifecycleManaged externally (IdP)Created/deleted with namespace or explicitly

RBAC Components — At a Glance

ComponentScopePurposeExample
RoleNamespaceDefines permissions within ONE namespace"Can get, list, create pods in anihpj"
ClusterRoleCluster-wideDefines permissions across ALL namespaces (or cluster resources like Nodes)"Can list all pods in all namespaces"
RoleBindingNamespaceGrants a Role to a subject in ONE namespace"Alice can manage pods in anihpj"
ClusterRoleBindingCluster-wideGrants a ClusterRole to a subject cluster-wide"Platform team can view all resources"
 
# Role: Developer can manage pods in anihpj namespace
apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: Role
metadata:
  namespace: anihpj
  name: developer
rules:
  - apiGroups: [""]
    resources: ["pods", "services", "configmaps", "secrets"]
    verbs: ["get", "list", "create", "update", "delete"]
  - apiGroups: ["apps"]
    resources: ["deployments"]
    verbs: ["get", "list", "create", "update"]

---
# RoleBinding: Bind the "anihpj-devs" group to the developer role
apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: RoleBinding
metadata:
  namespace: anihpj
  name: developer-binding
subjects:
  - kind: Group
    name: anihpj-devs
    apiGroup: rbac.authorization.k8s.io
roleRef:
  kind: Role
  name: developer
  apiGroup: rbac.authorization.k8s.io
⚠️ Least Privilege — The Golden Rule

Never give cluster-admin to developers. Never use the default ServiceAccount in pods. Every subject (human, ServiceAccount, group) should have exactly the permissions needed and nothing more. If a developer can't deploy to production, they can't accidentally break production. This is not about trust — it's about blast radius reduction.

16.3 Pod Security Standards (PSS)

PSS defines three security levels for pods. Enforce them via namespace labels — the Pod Security Admission controller (built into K8s 1.25+) will reject pods that violate the standard.

LevelDescriptionAllowsBlocksUse Case
🔴 PrivilegedUnrestricted — everything allowedHost namespaces, privileged containers, hostPath volumesNothingSystem pods only (CSI drivers, CNI)
🟡 BaselineMinimal restrictions, prevents known privilege escalationsStandard pod capabilitiesHost namespaces, hostPath, privileged modeLegacy apps that need some flexibility
🟢 RestrictedHeavily restricted, best practiceNon-root, no capabilities, read-only root FSAlmost everything riskyModern workloads (anihpj!)
 
# Label a namespace to enforce PSS Restricted
apiVersion: v1
kind: Namespace
metadata:
  name: anihpj
  labels:
    pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce: restricted
    pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce-version: latest
    pod-security.kubernetes.io/warn: restricted
    pod-security.kubernetes.io/audit: restricted

What Each PSS Level Blocks — Detailed Comparison

ControlPrivilegedBaselineRestricted
Host Namespaces✅ Allowed❌ Blocked❌ Blocked
Privileged Containers✅ Allowed❌ Blocked❌ Blocked
hostPath Volumes✅ Allowed❌ Blocked❌ Blocked
Linux Capabilities✅ All allowed⚠️ Drops NET_RAW❌ Drops ALL (must explicitly add)
Root User (UID 0)✅ Allowed⚠️ Allowed❌ Must be non-root
Privilege Escalation✅ Allowed⚠️ Allowed❌ Must be false
Seccomp/AppArmor✅ Unrestricted⚠️ RuntimeDefault❌ RuntimeDefault or stricter
💡 CNPE Standard

Every application namespace should be labeled pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce: restricted. Only system namespaces (kube-system, cert-manager, ingress-nginx) get Baseline or Privileged. This single label prevents 90% of container escape vulnerabilities.

16.4 Network Policies — Pod-Level Firewall

By default, all pods can talk to all pods in K8s. NetworkPolicies are your pod-level firewall — they define which pods can send/receive traffic. They control both Ingress (incoming traffic — who can reach my pod?) and Egress (outgoing traffic — can my pod reach external services?). The golden rule: default deny all, then allow specific traffic.

NetworkPolicy for anihpj — Defense in Depth

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    anihpj NAMESPACE                               │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────┐         ┌──────────────┐                      │
  │  │ anihpj-web   │────────▶│ PostgreSQL   │  ✅ ALLOWED           │
  │  │ (app pods)   │  :5432  │ (db pods)    │                      │
  │  └──────┬───────┘         └──────────────┘                      │
  │         │                                                        │
  │         │              ┌──────────────┐                         │
  │         └──────────────│ Redis Cache  │  ✅ ALLOWED              │
  │                :6379   │ (cache pods) │                         │
  │                        └──────────────┘                         │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────┐         ┌──────────────┐                      │
  │  │ Other Pods   │────────▶│ anihpj-web   │  ❌ DENIED            │
  │  │ (different   │         │              │                       │
  │  │  namespace)  │         │              │                       │
  │  └──────────────┘         └──────────────┘                      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Egress NetworkPolicy — Controlling Outbound Traffic

Egress controls traffic LEAVING pods. If a pod gets compromised, egress rules prevent it from phoning home or accessing external services. A complete zero-trust posture requires BOTH ingress and egress controls.

# Egress policy: Allow anihpj pods to ONLY reach DNS and PostgreSQL
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: restrict-egress
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  podSelector:
    matchLabels:
      app: anihpj-web
  policyTypes:
    - Egress
  egress:
    - to:                           # Allow DNS resolution
        - namespaceSelector: {}
          podSelector:
            matchLabels:
              k8s-app: kube-dns
      ports:
        - port: 53
          protocol: UDP
    - to:                           # Allow PostgreSQL
        - podSelector:
            matchLabels:
              app: postgresql
      ports:
        - port: 5432
  # All other egress DENIED — compromised pod can't phone home!
# Default deny all ingress traffic in anihpj namespace
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: deny-all-ingress
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  podSelector: {}  # Applies to ALL pods
  policyTypes:
    - Ingress
  # No ingress rules = deny all inbound traffic

---
# Allow anihpj-web pods to receive traffic from the ingress controller
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: allow-from-ingress
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  podSelector:
    matchLabels:
      app: anihpj-web
  policyTypes:
    - Ingress
  ingress:
    - from:
        - namespaceSelector:
            matchLabels:
              name: ingress-nginx
        - ipBlock:           # Allow specific external IPs
            cidr: 10.0.0.0/8
            except:
              - 10.0.1.0/24  # Block this subnet
      ports:
        - port: 8000

16.5 Supply Chain Security — Securing the Path from Code to Cluster

Supply chain security ensures that the code you deploy is exactly what you built, nothing more, nothing less. The pipeline: scan → sign → attest → verify → deploy.

Supply Chain Security Pipeline for anihpj

  ① CODE          ② BUILD           ③ SCAN          ④ SIGN
  ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐
  │Git Push  │──▶│Docker    │────▶│Trivy     │───▶│cosign    │
  │          │   │Build     │     │scan image│    │sign image│
  │• SAST    │   │          │     │          │    │          │
  │• Secrets │   │• SBOM    │     │• CVE check│   │• Keyless │
  │  scan    │   │  (Syft)  │     │• Policy   │    │  signing │
  └──────────┘   └──────────┘     └────┬─────┘    └────┬─────┘
                                       │               │
                          ┌────────────┴───────────────┘
                          ▼
  ⑤ ATTEST          ⑥ PUSH            ⑦ ADMIT         ⑧ DEPLOY
  ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐
  │Attest    │──▶│Push to   │────▶│Kyverno   │───▶│ArgoCD    │
  │image     │   │Registry  │     │Verify    │    │Sync to   │
  │          │   │          │     │Signature │    │Cluster   │
  │• Provenance│ │• OCI     │     │• Check   │    │          │
  │• Attestation││• Artifact│     │  SBOM    │    │☸️ Running│
  └──────────┘   └──────────┘     └──────────┘    └──────────┘

  🎯 Domain 5 (15%): Supply chain security IS on the CNPE exam

Key Tools in the Supply Chain

ToolWhat It DoesAlternativesStage
TrivyScans images for known CVEs (vulnerabilities)Grype, Snyk, Clair③ SCAN
SyftGenerates SBOM (Software Bill of Materials)Trivy (also generates SBOMs), SPDX② BUILD
cosignSigns images with keyless signing via OIDCNotary v2④ SIGN

📝 Chapter 16 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What are the 4Cs of Cloud Native Security? Which layers does the CNPE own?
Show Answer
Answer

Cloud → Cluster → Container → Code. CNPEs directly own the Cluster (RBAC, NetworkPolicies, PSS, audit logging) and Container (non-root, image scanning, signing) layers. They enable the Cloud layer (IAM, encryption) and Code layer (CI scanning tools). Defense in depth means securing ALL four — one weak layer compromises everything.

Q2
What is the difference between a Role and a ClusterRole? When use each?
Show Answer
Answer

Role: Namespace-scoped — permissions apply within one namespace (e.g., "create pods in anihpj"). ClusterRole: Cluster-scoped — for cluster-wide resources (Nodes, PVs) or permissions across ALL namespaces. Use ClusterRole for platform team access and for resources that aren't namespaced.

Q3
What is the difference between a RoleBinding and a ClusterRoleBinding?
Show Answer
Answer

RoleBinding: Binds a Role (or ClusterRole!) to subjects in ONE namespace. You can bind a ClusterRole via RoleBinding to grant cluster-wide permissions only in that namespace. ClusterRoleBinding: Binds a ClusterRole to subjects across ALL namespaces — use sparingly, typically only for platform admins.

Q4
What are the three Pod Security Standard levels? Which should anihpj use?
Show Answer
Answer

Privileged: Everything allowed (system pods only). Baseline: Minimal restrictions, blocks known escalations. Restricted: Heavy restrictions — non-root, no capabilities, read-only root FS. anihpj should use Restricted — it's a modern Django app that doesn't need host access or root privileges.

Q5
By default, can any pod in K8s talk to any other pod? How do you change this?
Show Answer
Answer

Yes — by default, all pods can communicate with all pods across all namespaces. To restrict this, create a NetworkPolicy with podSelector: {} and no ingress/egress rules — this is a "default deny all." Then create specific NetworkPolicies to allow only necessary traffic (e.g., anihpj-web → PostgreSQL on port 5432).

Q6
Write a NetworkPolicy that only allows anihpj-web pods to receive traffic from the ingress-nginx namespace on port 8000.
Show Answer
Answer

Apply default-deny, then: podSelector: {matchLabels: {app: anihpj-web}}, ingress: [{from: [{namespaceSelector: {matchLabels: {name: ingress-nginx}}}]}, {ports: [{port: 8000}]}]. This ensures ONLY the ingress controller can route traffic to anihpj — nothing else in the cluster can reach it.

Q7
What is an SBOM? Why does it matter for supply chain security?
Show Answer
Answer

SBOM (Software Bill of Materials) is a list of every component, library, and dependency in your software. Generated by tools like Syft. It matters because: when a CVE is announced (e.g., Log4Shell), you can instantly check every image's SBOM to see if you're affected — without scanning everything again.

Q8
How does cosign keyless signing work? Why is it better than managing private keys?
Show Answer
Answer

Cosign uses OIDC (OpenID Connect) — your CI system authenticates to an OIDC provider (GitHub, GCP), gets a short-lived identity token, and cosign uses Sigstore's Fulcio CA to issue a certificate. No private keys to manage, rotate, or lose. The signature is tied to your CI identity, not a key file.

Q9
What is the principle of least privilege? Give an RBAC example for anihpj.
Show Answer
Answer

Every subject gets ONLY the permissions needed to do its job — nothing more. For anihpj: the CI ServiceAccount can create/update Deployments and Services in the anihpj namespace, but CANNOT delete Namespaces, read Secrets from other namespaces, or access Nodes. If CI gets compromised, the blast radius is contained to one namespace.

Q10
You're securing the anihpj platform. Walk through the security measures at each of the 4Cs.
Show Answer
Answer

Cloud: Private subnets, IAM roles with least privilege, KMS encryption, CloudTrail enabled. Cluster: RBAC (dev Role bound to anihpj namespace), PSS Restricted on all app namespaces, NetworkPolicies (deny-all + specific allows), etcd encryption, audit logging. Container: Non-root user (1000), read-only root FS, drop all capabilities, Trivy scan in CI, cosign sign, Kyverno verify. Code: Semgrep SAST, pip-audit dependency scan, gitleaks pre-commit hook.

Chapter 17: Policy as Code with Kyverno & OPA

Policy as Code means defining security and compliance rules as version-controlled code, not tribal knowledge or wiki pages. Kyverno (K8s-native, YAML policies) and OPA/Gatekeeper (Rego language, flexible) are the two dominant policy engines. As a CNPE, you'll use them to enforce governance automatically — 15% of the exam.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~2 hours 🔐 Policy 🔐 Domain 5 (15%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ What Policy as Code is and why it's essential for platform governance
  • ✅ Kyverno: Validate, Mutate, Generate, and Verify Images policies in YAML
  • ✅ 20 essential platform policies every CNPE should deploy
  • ✅ OPA/Gatekeeper — when to use Rego over Kyverno
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🔐 Security & Policy Enforcement 15%

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — Policy as Code

The CNPE exam tests your ability to write and apply policies. Expect to be asked: "Write a Kyverno policy that requires all pods to have resource limits" or "Configure a ClusterPolicy to block images with the :latest tag." Know the difference between Validate, Mutate, Generate, and Verify Images policy types. Also know when to choose Kyverno (YAML, K8s-native) vs OPA (Rego, cross-system) — this comparison question appears frequently.

17.1 What is Policy as Code?

Policy as Code defines rules programmatically: "All pods MUST have resource limits," "Images MUST come from our private registry," "Ingress hosts MUST use TLS." These rules are enforced automatically by admission controllers — they block non-compliant resources before they're created, and they audit existing resources for violations.

Policy as Code — The Admission Control Flow

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  ① DEV CREATES         ② API SERVER         ③ MUTATING           │
  │  ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     │
  │  │ kubectl      │────▶│ Validate     │────▶│ Mutate:       │     │
  │  │ apply pod.yaml│     │ request      │     │ Add defaults  │     │
  │  │              │     │              │     │ (labels,      │     │
  │  │ No resource  │     │              │     │  limits)      │     │
  │  │ limits! 😱   │     └──────────────┘     └──────┬───────┘     │
  │  └──────────────┘                                 │              │
  │                                                   ▼              │
  │  ④ VALIDATING                              ⑤ RESULT             │
  │  ┌──────────────┐                         ┌──────────────┐     │
  │  │ Validate:    │                         │ ❌ REJECTED   │     │
  │  │ Has resource │────────────────────────▶│ "Pod must     │     │
  │  │ limits?      │                         │  have resource│     │
  │  │ NO → BLOCK!  │                         │  limits"      │     │
  │  └──────────────┘                         └──────────────┘     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  🔑 Mutating policies run FIRST, then Validating.               │
  │     If mutate adds limits, validate passes!                      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

17.2 Kyverno Deep Dive

Kyverno is Kubernetes-native — policies are written in YAML, the same language as your K8s manifests. No new language to learn. Four policy types cover every use case.

Validate — Block Non-Compliant Resources

Validate policies check resources and reject them if they don't meet your rules. This is the most common type.

# Require resource limits on all pods
apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1
kind: ClusterPolicy
metadata:
  name: require-resource-limits
spec:
  validationFailureAction: Enforce  # Block the resource!
  rules:
    - name: check-limits
      match:
        any:
          - resources:
              kinds: [Pod]
      validate:
        message: "Every pod must have CPU and memory limits"
        pattern:
          spec:
            containers:
              - resources:
                  limits:
                    cpu: "?*"       # Must exist, any value
                    memory: "?*"    # Must exist, any value

Mutate — Automatically Fix Resources

Mutate policies modify resources before they're created — adding default labels, injecting sidecars, or setting security contexts automatically.

# Auto-add non-root security context to all pods
apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1
kind: ClusterPolicy
metadata:
  name: add-nonroot-securitycontext
spec:
  rules:
    - name: add-nonroot
      match:
        any:
          - resources:
              kinds: [Pod]
      mutate:
        patchStrategicMerge:
          spec:
            securityContext:
              runAsNonRoot: true
              runAsUser: 1000
            containers:
              - (name): "*"
                securityContext:
                  allowPrivilegeEscalation: false

Generate & Verify Images

Generate policies create resources automatically (e.g., a NetworkPolicy for every new namespace). Verify Images checks image signatures and blocks unsigned or tampered images — this is your supply chain security gate.

17.3 Common Platform Policies — The Essential 20

#PolicyTypeWhy It Matters
1Require resource limitsValidatePrevents resource starvation
2Enforce non-root containersMutatePrevents container escape
3Block latest tagValidatePrevents untracked deploys
4Require specific labelsValidateCost tracking, ownership
5Default deny NetworkPolicyGenerateZero-trust networking
6Require PodDisruptionBudgetValidateEnsures HA during node drains
7Block hostPath volumesValidatePrevents node escape
8Require probes (liveness/readiness)ValidateEnsures self-healing works
9Restrict image registriesValidateOnly allow corp registry
10Verify image signaturesVerify ImagesSupply chain integrity
💡 CNPE Policy Strategy

Start with Validate policies in Audit mode (warn but don't block). Review violations for a week. Fix legitimate issues. Then switch to Enforce mode. This avoids breaking existing workloads while you tighten security. Kyverno Policy Reports show you exactly what's violating before you block.

Kyverno Policy Reports — Built-in Compliance Auditing

Kyverno's background scanner continuously evaluates ALL existing resources against your policies and generates Policy Reports (per-namespace) and ClusterPolicy Reports (cluster-wide). Each report shows Pass/Fail/Warn for every policy on every resource — giving you a real-time compliance dashboard with no external tools. Run kubectl get policyreport -A to see exactly which resources violate which policies right now. This is how you audit compliance across the entire cluster without manual checks.

17.4 OPA/Gatekeeper — The Alternative

OPA (Open Policy Agent) uses Rego, a purpose-built policy language that's more powerful (but more complex) than Kyverno's YAML. Gatekeeper is the K8s-native integration of OPA.

AspectKyvernoOPA/Gatekeeper
Policy LanguageYAML — K8s-native, no new languageRego — powerful but steep learning curve
Mutate Support✅ Built-in, simple YAML patches⚠️ Limited — mainly validate
Generate Resources✅ Auto-create NetworkPolicies, etc.❌ Not supported
Policy Reports✅ Built-in audit reports per resource✅ Audit via Constraint status
Complex LogicLimited — pattern matching✅ Full programming language (Rego)
Best ForK8s-only platforms, rapid adoptionMulti-system policies, complex logic
CNPE RecommendationStart with Kyverno (simpler). Add OPA only if you need cross-system policies or complex Rego logic.

📝 Chapter 17 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What is Policy as Code? Why is it essential for platform governance?
Show Answer
Answer

Policy as Code defines compliance rules as version-controlled code, enforced automatically by admission controllers. It eliminates manual "did you remember to set resource limits?" checks. Policies are peer-reviewed in Git, auditable, and consistently enforced — no exceptions unless explicitly allowed.

Q2
What are the 4 types of Kyverno policies? Give an example of each.
Show Answer
Answer

Validate: Require resource limits — blocks pods without them. Mutate: Auto-add non-root securityContext to all pods. Generate: Auto-create a default-deny NetworkPolicy for every new namespace. Verify Images: Check cosign signature before admitting an image. Each serves a different governance need.

Q3
What is the difference between Audit and Enforce mode in Kyverno?
Show Answer
Answer

Audit: Reports violations in Policy Reports but does NOT block resources. Safe for initial deployment. Enforce: Blocks violating resources — the API server rejects the request. The CNPE best practice: deploy in Audit first, review violations, then switch to Enforce after fixing legitimate issues.

Q4
Write a Kyverno policy that requires all pods to have CPU and memory limits.
Show Answer
Answer

A ClusterPolicy with validationFailureAction: Enforce, matching Pod resources, validating that spec.containers[].resources.limits.cpu and memory exist with "?*" (any non-empty value). This blocks any pod creation request that doesn't specify resource limits — preventing resource starvation on shared nodes.

Q5
How does a Kyverno Mutate policy differ from a Validate policy?
Show Answer
Answer

Validate: Checks and REJECTS non-compliant resources. Mutate: MODIFIES resources before they're created — it doesn't reject, it fixes. Example: Mutate auto-adds runAsNonRoot: true to pods that forgot it. Mutate runs BEFORE Validate, so a mutate policy that adds resource limits will make a subsequent validate policy pass.

Q6
When would you choose OPA/Gatekeeper over Kyverno? When would you choose Kyverno?
Show Answer
Answer

Choose Kyverno: K8s-only platform, want YAML-native policies, need mutate/generate capabilities, want faster team adoption (no new language). Choose OPA: Need complex policy logic (Rego is a full language), using OPA for non-K8s systems too (Terraform, Envoy, API auth), or already have Rego expertise. Most CNPEs start with Kyverno.

Q7
What's the purpose of a "block latest tag" policy? Write the Kyverno pattern for it.
Show Answer
Answer

The :latest tag is mutable — it can point to different images over time, making it impossible to know exactly what's running. The policy validates that spec.containers[].image does NOT contain :latest using a deny condition: image: "*:latest" with validationFailureAction: Enforce.

Q8
What does a Kyverno Generate policy do? Give a practical example.
Show Answer
Answer

Generate policies create new resources in response to events. Example: when a new Namespace is created, Kyverno auto-generates a default-deny NetworkPolicy and a resource quota for that namespace. This ensures every namespace has basic security from the moment it's created — no manual intervention.

Q9
What is the Kyverno Policy Report? How does it help with governance?
Show Answer
Answer

Policy Reports are per-resource compliance reports generated by Kyverno's background scanner. They show: Pass/Fail/Warn for each policy on each resource. Platform teams use them to audit compliance (e.g., "which pods in the cluster lack resource limits?"), generate compliance dashboards, and track remediation progress over time.

Q10
You're securing the anihpj platform. List 5 Kyverno policies you would deploy immediately.
Show Answer
Answer

1. Require resource limits (Validate). 2. Auto-add non-root securityContext (Mutate). 3. Block :latest tag (Validate). 4. Require app/team labels (Validate — for cost tracking and ownership). 5. Verify image signatures (Verify Images — only allow cosign-signed images from our registry). Deploy in Audit first, then Enforce.

Chapter 18: Certificate Management & mTLS

TLS certificates are the foundation of secure communication in your platform. cert-manager automates certificate lifecycle — issue, renew, rotate — so you never have an expired cert at 3 AM again. mTLS (mutual TLS) takes it further: both client AND server verify each other's identity, enabling zero-trust networking. This is 15% of the CNPE exam.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 🔐 Security 🔐 Domain 5 (15%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ TLS fundamentals: keys, certificates, CAs, chains, and trust
  • ✅ cert-manager: automated certificate lifecycle with Let's Encrypt
  • ✅ mTLS: zero-trust service-to-service authentication
  • ✅ Internal PKI: managing internal certificates for platform services
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🔐 Security & Policy Enforcement 15%

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — Certificate Management

Expect hands-on cert-manager tasks: deploy a ClusterIssuer for Let's Encrypt, create a Certificate resource for anihpj.example.com, or set up an internal CA for service-to-service TLS. Know the difference between Issuer (namespace) vs ClusterIssuer (cluster-wide), how ACME HTTP-01 vs DNS-01 challenges work, and what mTLS provides that standard TLS doesn't. The exam may also ask about cert-manager auto-renewal — this is the most-tested feature.

18.1 TLS Fundamentals for Platform Engineers

TLS (Transport Layer Security) encrypts communication between services. It uses asymmetric cryptography: a public key (shared openly) and a private key (kept secret). A certificate binds a public key to an identity (like anihpj.example.com) and is signed by a Certificate Authority (CA) that both parties trust.

TLS Handshake — How Secure Connections Are Established

  CLIENT (browser)                            SERVER (anihpj.example.com)

  ① ClientHello ─────────────────────────────▶
     "I support TLS 1.3, these ciphers..."

  ②                         ◀───────────────── ServerHello + Certificate
                            "Let's use TLS 1.3. Here's my cert
                             signed by Let's Encrypt."

  ③ Client verifies cert:
     • Is it signed by a trusted CA? ✅
     • Is it for anihpj.example.com? ✅
     • Is it expired? ❌ (not expired)
     → TRUST ESTABLISHED

  ④ ClientKeyExchange ───────────────────────▶
     (encrypted with server's public key)

  ⑤                               ◀─────────── Finished
     🔒 ENCRYPTED SESSION ESTABLISHED 🔒

Key TLS Concepts

ConceptWhat It IsPlatform Relevance
CA (Certificate Authority)Trusted entity that signs certificatesLet's Encrypt for public, internal CA for service-to-service
Certificate ChainChain of trust: your cert → intermediate CA → root CABrowsers trust the root; your cert must chain to it
Wildcard CertificateOne cert for *.example.com⚠️ Security risk — if compromised, ALL subdomains exposed
SAN (Subject Alternative Name)Multiple domains in one certOne cert can cover anihpj.com + www.anihpj.com
Server CertificateProves server identity ("I am anihpj.com")Used by ingress, databases, any server endpoint
Client CertificateProves client identity ("I am anihpj-web")Used in mTLS — client must present a valid cert

Common TLS Pitfalls in Cloud-Native Environments

⚠️ TLS Mistakes That Break Production

1. Expired Certificates: The #1 cause of production TLS outages. Fix: cert-manager auto-renews 30 days before expiry.
2. Missing Intermediate Certificates: Your cert is valid, but the chain is broken because the intermediate CA bundle wasn't included. Fix: cert-manager handles the full chain automatically.
3. Wrong DNS Name: Certificate is for anihpj.com but service is accessed via anihpj.namespace.svc.cluster.local. Fix: include SANs for all DNS names (external AND internal).
4. Weak Cipher Suites: Old TLS versions and weak ciphers leave you vulnerable. Fix: enforce TLS 1.2+ and modern cipher suites in ingress annotations.

18.2 cert-manager — Automated Certificate Lifecycle

cert-manager runs in your cluster and automatically handles certificate issuance, renewal, and rotation. It integrates with Let's Encrypt (ACME protocol), HashiCorp Vault, and internal CAs. No more expired certificates at 3 AM.

# 1. ClusterIssuer — HOW to get certificates (Let's Encrypt)
apiVersion: cert-manager.io/v1
kind: ClusterIssuer
metadata:
  name: letsencrypt-prod
spec:
  acme:
    server: https://acme-v02.api.letsencrypt.org/directory
    email: platform@example.com
    privateKeySecretRef:
      name: letsencrypt-prod-key
    solvers:
      - http01:
          ingress:
            class: nginx

---
# 2. Certificate — WHAT certificate to get
apiVersion: cert-manager.io/v1
kind: Certificate
metadata:
  name: anihpj-tls
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  secretName: anihpj-tls-secret      # K8s Secret to store cert+key
  dnsNames:
    - anihpj.example.com
    - api.anihpj.example.com
  issuerRef:
    name: letsencrypt-prod
    kind: ClusterIssuer
  # cert-manager auto-renews 30 days before expiry!

ACME Challenge Types — HTTP-01 vs DNS-01

AspectHTTP-01DNS-01
How it worksPlaces a file at /.well-known/acme-challenge/ on your serverCreates a TXT record in your DNS zone
RequiresPublicly accessible HTTP endpoint (port 80)DNS API access (Route53, CloudDNS)
Wildcard certs❌ Not supported✅ Supports *.example.com
Internal services❌ Must be internet-facing✅ Works for internal-only services
CNPE PickSimple public ingressesProduction platforms (more flexible)

Issuer vs ClusterIssuer

TypeScopeUse Case
IssuerSingle namespaceTeam-specific CA, namespace-scoped certs
ClusterIssuerAll namespacesPlatform-wide Let's Encrypt, shared internal CA
💡 Auto-Renewal — Never Expire Again

cert-manager automatically renews certificates 30 days before expiry. It updates the K8s Secret, and ingress controllers (nginx, Traefik) automatically reload the new cert. Zero downtime, zero manual intervention. This single feature justifies cert-manager's existence on every cluster.

18.3 mTLS (Mutual TLS) — Zero-Trust Networking

In standard TLS, only the server proves its identity. In mTLS, both client and server present certificates and verify each other. This means Service A can cryptographically prove "I am the anihpj-web service" to Service B (PostgreSQL) — no IP-based trust, no shared secrets.

mTLS — Mutual Authentication

  STANDARD TLS (Server Only)              mTLS (Mutual)
  ┌──────────────────────────┐           ┌──────────────────────────┐
  │                          │           │                          │
  │  anihpj-web              │           │  anihpj-web              │
  │  ┌──────────────────┐   │           │  ┌──────────────────┐   │
  │  │ 🔒 Trust server? │   │           │  │ 🔒 Trust server? │   │
  │  │    ✅ Yes         │   │           │  │    ✅ Yes         │   │
  │  └──────────────────┘   │           │  │ 🔒 Server trusts  │   │
  │            │            │           │  │    me? ✅ Cert!   │   │
  │            ▼            │           │  └──────────────────┘   │
  │  ┌──────────────────┐   │           │            │            │
  │  │ PostgreSQL       │   │           │            ▼            │
  │  │ "I'm Postgres,   │   │           │  ┌──────────────────┐   │
  │  │  here's my cert" │   │           │  │ PostgreSQL       │   │
  │  └──────────────────┘   │           │  │ "I trust you     │   │
  │                          │           │  │  because you     │   │
  │  Server: ✅ Verified     │           │  │  proved identity"│   │
  │  Client: ❓ Who are you? │           │  └──────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────┘           │                          │
                                         │ Both: ✅ Verified        │
                                         └──────────────────────────┘

Service Mesh mTLS — Istio & Linkerd

Service meshes like Istio and Linkerd provide automatic mTLS between all services in the mesh — no application code changes. They inject sidecar proxies (Envoy for Istio, linkerd-proxy for Linkerd) that handle all TLS negotiation transparently.

Istio mTLS — Concrete Example for anihpj

# 1. Label the namespace for Istio sidecar injection
kubectl label namespace anihpj istio-injection=enabled

# 2. Deploy anihpj as usual — Istio auto-injects Envoy sidecar
#    Every pod now has TWO containers: anihpj + istio-proxy

# 3. Enable STRICT mTLS for the anihpj namespace
apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: PeerAuthentication
metadata:
  name: anihpj-strict-mtls
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  mtls:
    mode: STRICT  # All traffic to/from anihpj MUST use mTLS

# Result:
# anihpj-web → PostgreSQL: auto-mTLS (both have Envoy proxies)
# anihpj-web → Redis: auto-mTLS
# External caller → anihpj-web: rejected without valid cert
# ANY pod without Envoy → anihpj namespace: connection REFUSED
📖 When Do You NEED mTLS?

mTLS is essential when: (1) You're in a regulated industry (finance, healthcare) requiring service-to-service authentication, (2) You're running multi-tenant clusters where different teams' services share infrastructure, (3) You need zero-trust networking where network-level controls (NetworkPolicy) aren't sufficient. For most platforms, start with NetworkPolicies and add mTLS (via Linkerd) as security requirements mature.

18.4 Internal PKI with cert-manager

Not every certificate needs to be publicly trusted. For internal service-to-service communication, you can create your own CA using cert-manager and issue internal certificates. This is free, fast, and gives you full control.

Internal PKI Architecture

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    INTERNAL PKI (cert-manager)                    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐                                       │
  │  │ Self-Signed CA       │  ← cert-manager creates this          │
  │  │ (ClusterIssuer)      │    (or you bring your own)            │
  │  │ ┌──────────────────┐ │                                       │
  │  │ │ CA Certificate   │ │  ← Distributed to all services        │
  │  │ │ CA Private Key   │ │    via trust bundle ConfigMap         │
  │  │ └──────────────────┘ │                                       │
  │  └──────────┬───────────┘                                       │
  │             │ Signs:                                             │
  │     ┌───────┼──────────┬──────────────┐                         │
  │     ▼       ▼          ▼              ▼                         │
  │  ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐     ┌──────────┐                   │
  │  │anihpj│ │Post- │ │Redis │     │Internal  │                   │
  │  │-web  │ │greSQL│ │      │     │Ingress   │                   │
  │  │cert  │ │cert  │ │cert  │     │TLS cert  │                   │
  │  └──────┘ └──────┘ └──────┘     └──────────┘                   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  🔑 All services trust the same CA → mutual trust established   │
  │  🔄 cert-manager auto-rotates all certs before expiry            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

# 1. Create a self-signed CA Issuer
apiVersion: cert-manager.io/v1
kind: ClusterIssuer
metadata:
  name: internal-ca-issuer
spec:
  selfSigned: {}

---
# 2. Create the CA Certificate
apiVersion: cert-manager.io/v1
kind: Certificate
metadata:
  name: internal-ca
  namespace: cert-manager
spec:
  isCA: true
  commonName: internal-ca
  secretName: internal-ca-secret
  privateKey:
    algorithm: ECDSA
    size: 256
  issuerRef:
    name: internal-ca-issuer
    kind: ClusterIssuer

---
# 3. Create a ClusterIssuer backed by our CA
apiVersion: cert-manager.io/v1
kind: ClusterIssuer
metadata:
  name: internal-issuer
spec:
  ca:
    secretName: internal-ca-secret
# Now any namespace can request certs from this issuer!
💡 CNPE Practice

Deploy an internal CA with cert-manager on day 1. Use it for all internal service-to-service TLS (postgres, redis, internal ingresses). Use Let's Encrypt ONLY for public-facing endpoints. This gives you encrypted internal traffic without depending on external internet access for certificate issuance.

Certificate Rotation Strategies

StrategyHow It WorksDowntimeBest For
cert-manager Auto-RenewalRenews 30 days before expiry, updates Secret, ingress auto-reloadsZero downtimePublic ingresses, standard TLS
Rolling RestartNew cert in Secret → restart pods to pick up new certBrief (rolling update)Pods that cache certs on startup
Dual CertificateIssue new cert before old expires, serve both during transitionZero downtimemTLS where both sides need coordinated rotation
Trust Bundle ConfigMapMount CA bundle via ConfigMap, update ConfigMap → pods auto-reloadZero downtime (with reloader)Internal PKI — all services trust the same CA
📖 Trust Distribution — Mounting CA Bundles

For internal PKI, every service needs to trust your internal CA. Create a ConfigMap with the CA certificate, mount it to pods at /etc/ssl/certs/internal-ca.crt, and configure your application to trust that path. Use stakater/Reloader to automatically restart pods when the ConfigMap changes — ensuring all services trust the latest CA without manual intervention.

📝 Chapter 18 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
How does TLS work? Explain the handshake in simple terms.
Show Answer
Answer

1. Client sends "hello" with supported ciphers. 2. Server responds with its certificate (public key + identity). 3. Client verifies the cert against trusted CAs. 4. Client generates a session key, encrypts it with server's public key, sends it. 5. Both now share a secret session key for symmetric encryption. All subsequent traffic is encrypted with this key.

Q2
What is cert-manager? What problem does it solve?
Show Answer
Answer

cert-manager automates certificate lifecycle in K8s: issuance (via Let's Encrypt, Vault, internal CA), renewal (30 days before expiry), and rotation (updates Secrets, ingress controllers auto-reload). It eliminates manual certificate management — no more expired cert alerts at 3 AM.

Q3
What's the difference between an Issuer and a ClusterIssuer in cert-manager?
Show Answer
Answer

Issuer: Namespace-scoped — only usable within its own namespace. ClusterIssuer: Cluster-wide — any namespace can reference it. Use ClusterIssuer for platform-wide services (Let's Encrypt, internal CA) so every team namespace can request certificates without creating their own issuer.

Q4
Write a cert-manager Certificate resource for anihpj.example.com using Let's Encrypt.
Show Answer
Answer

A Certificate with dnsNames: ["anihpj.example.com"], secretName: anihpj-tls, issuerRef: {name: letsencrypt-prod, kind: ClusterIssuer}. cert-manager handles the ACME challenge (HTTP-01 or DNS-01), obtains the cert from Let's Encrypt, stores it in the K8s Secret, and auto-renews 30 days before expiry.

Q5
What is mTLS? How does it differ from standard TLS?
Show Answer
Answer

Standard TLS: Only the server proves its identity to the client (you know you're talking to the real anihpj.com). mTLS: BOTH parties prove their identity — the anihpj-web service proves to PostgreSQL "I really am anihpj-web" and PostgreSQL proves "I really am the database." Neither trusts the other without cryptographic proof.

Q6
How does Linkerd provide automatic mTLS without application code changes?
Show Answer
Answer

Linkerd injects a transparent sidecar proxy into every pod. This proxy intercepts all traffic and handles mTLS negotiation automatically. The application code just talks to localhost:5432 as usual — the proxy transparently upgrades the connection to mTLS. No code changes, no cert management by developers.

Q7
How do you create an internal CA with cert-manager? Why would you?
Show Answer
Answer

Create a self-signed ClusterIssuer, then a Certificate with isCA: true to establish the CA. Create a CA-backed ClusterIssuer that references the CA secret. Internal CAs are free, fast, and work offline — perfect for service-to-service TLS within the cluster where public trust isn't needed.

Q8
What is a Certificate Chain? Why does it matter?
Show Answer
Answer

A chain links your certificate through intermediate CAs to a trusted root CA: Your Cert → Intermediate CA → Root CA. Browsers/clients must be able to follow this chain to a trusted root. If an intermediate cert is missing or expired, the entire chain fails — even if your cert is valid. cert-manager handles chain assembly automatically.

Q9
What happens when a cert-manager managed certificate is about to expire?
Show Answer
Answer

cert-manager automatically renews the certificate 30 days before expiry. It requests a new cert from the issuer, stores it in the same K8s Secret (updating the key and cert), and ingress controllers (nginx) automatically detect the change and reload. Zero downtime, zero manual steps. This is the "why cert-manager exists" feature.

Q10
You're securing anihpj. What TLS strategy do you deploy for public vs internal traffic?
Show Answer
Answer

Public: Let's Encrypt ClusterIssuer in cert-manager → auto-TLS for anihpj.example.com via HTTP-01 challenge on nginx ingress. Auto-renews, zero cost. Internal: Self-signed internal CA ClusterIssuer → issue certs for PostgreSQL and Redis → mount CA trust bundle to anihpj-web. Optionally add Linkerd for transparent mTLS between all services. Both layers automated via cert-manager.

Chapter 19: Designing Developer-Friendly Platforms

A platform that no one wants to use is a failed platform. Developer Experience (DX) is not "nice to have" — it's the measure of whether your platform reduces friction or creates frustration. As a CNPE, you design the interfaces, workflows, and documentation that make developers productive. This is 25% of the exam (Domain 3: Self-Service).

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 🎨 DX 🔌 Domain 3 (25%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ DX mindset: reduce cognitive load, make the right thing easy
  • ✅ Understanding developer workflows and identifying friction points
  • ✅ Platform interface design: CLI, Web Portal, API, ChatOps
  • ✅ Platform documentation: Docs-as-Code, runbooks, ADRs
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🔌 Platform APIs & Self-Service 25%

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — Developer Experience

The CNPE exam tests your ability to design self-service experiences. Expect scenario questions: "A developer needs to deploy a new microservice. What interfaces should your platform provide?" or "How would you reduce friction in the deployment workflow?" Know the four platform interfaces (CLI, Web Portal, API, ChatOps) and when to use each. The exam emphasizes self-service — no tickets, no manual approvals for standard operations.

19.1 The Developer Experience (DX) Mindset

DX means treating your platform like a product and developers like customers. If developers hate using your platform, they'll work around it — running their own clusters, deploying outside the platform, creating shadow IT. Your platform must be easier to use than to avoid.

DX Principles — Making the Right Thing Easy

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    DX PRINCIPLES                                   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ① REDUCE COGNITIVE LOAD                                         │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Don't make devs learn K8s to deploy their Django app       │  │
  │  │ → `platform deploy` instead of `kubectl apply -f 10 files` │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ② SELF-SERVICE — NO TICKETS, NO WAITING                         │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Need a database? → `platform db create anihpj-db`          │  │
  │  │ Need a namespace? → `platform ns create team-anihpj`       │  │
  │  │ ❌ "File a JIRA ticket and wait 3 days"                    │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ③ CLEAR DOCUMENTATION                                           │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ If it's not documented, it DOESN'T EXIST                   │  │
  │  │ → 5-minute quickstart, runbooks, API reference             │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ④ CONSISTENT INTERFACES                                         │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ CLI, Web Portal, API should all feel like ONE platform     │  │
  │  │ → Same naming, same concepts, same error messages          │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

19.2 Understanding Developer Workflows

To build a great platform, you must understand the daily developer journey. Where do developers spend time? What frustrates them? Map their workflow and identify every friction point.

Developer Journey Map — The anihpj Team

  CODE ────────▶ TEST ────────▶ DEPLOY ────────▶ MONITOR
  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐
  │Write code│  │Run pytest│  │Create PR │  │Check     │
  │          │  │          │  │          │  │dashboards│
  │😊 Happy  │  │😊 Happy  │  │😐 CI wait│  │😊 Happy  │
  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └────┬─────┘  └──────────┘
                                   │
                                   ▼
                          ┌──────────────┐
                          │CI passes?    │
                          │YES → Deploy  │
                          │NO → Fix      │
                          │😤 Frustration│
                          │(flakey tests)│
                          └──────────────┘

  KEY FRICTION POINTS:
  ① Waiting for CI (15 min) → Add build caching
  ② Flakey integration tests → Quarantine flakey tests
  ③ Manual DB schema changes → Auto-migrate in CI
  ④ Unknown deploy status → Slack notification on deploy

Developers interact with your platform through four interfaces. Each serves a different purpose — choose the right one for each task.
