19.4 Platform Documentation — Docs as Code

Documentation is part of your platform product. Treat it like code: version-controlled, reviewed, tested, and continuously deployed.

Doc TypePurposeExample
Quickstart GuideGet a dev deploying in 5 minutes"Deploy your first app to anihpj"
RunbooksWhat to do when X breaks"anihpj API returning 500s → steps to diagnose"
API ReferenceComplete API documentationSwagger/OpenAPI for platform REST API
ADR (Architecture Decision Records)Why we made a decision"ADR-001: We chose ArgoCD over FluxCD because..."
Backstage TechDocsDocs alongside code in the catalogMarkdown in repo → auto-published in Backstage

API References — Documenting Platform APIs

Every platform API should have an OpenAPI/Swagger specification that's auto-published. Developers should be able to explore your platform API, understand request/response formats, and test endpoints — all from the documentation. For anihpj's Django REST API: use drf-spectacular to auto-generate OpenAPI specs, publish them via Backstage TechDocs, and provide curl examples for every endpoint.

# Auto-generate OpenAPI spec for anihpj Django REST API
pip install drf-spectacular

# settings.py
REST_FRAMEWORK = {
    'DEFAULT_SCHEMA_CLASS': 'drf_spectacular.openapi.AutoSchema',
}
SPECTACULAR_SETTINGS = {
    'TITLE': 'anihpj Platform API',
    'DESCRIPTION': 'Job posting and management API',
    'VERSION': '2.1.0',
}
# Now /api/schema/ serves the OpenAPI spec automatically
💡 Docs-as-Code Workflow

Write docs in Markdown, store them in the same repo as the code, review docs in PRs just like code, and auto-publish via CI. Backstage TechDocs renders Markdown files from your repo automatically — docs live next to the service they describe. This ensures docs stay in sync with code changes.

📝 Chapter 19 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What is Developer Experience (DX)? Why does it matter for platform engineering?
Show Answer
Answer

DX is how developers feel when using your platform — is it friction or flow? Good DX means developers deploy faster, break things less often, and don't work around the platform. Bad DX leads to shadow IT, frustrated engineers, and the platform being abandoned. The platform must be easier to use than to avoid.

Q2
What are the 4 key DX principles for platform design?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Reduce cognitive load — hide K8s complexity. 2. Self-service — no tickets, no waiting. 3. Clear documentation — if it's not documented, it doesn't exist. 4. Consistent interfaces — CLI, Web Portal, API should feel cohesive. Each principle removes friction from the developer workflow.

Q3
What is a developer journey map? How does it help platform teams?
Show Answer
Answer

A journey map visualizes the developer's workflow across stages (code → test → deploy → monitor) and identifies emotion peaks and valleys — where developers are happy and where they're frustrated. It helps platform teams prioritize which friction points to fix first, based on actual developer experience, not assumptions.

Q4
What are the 4 platform interfaces? Give an example of when to use each.
Show Answer
Answer

CLI: Daily dev workflow (platform deploy). Web Portal: Service discovery, dashboards (Backstage). API: CI/CD integration, programmatic access. ChatOps: Quick actions from Slack (/deploy anihpj to staging). Choose the interface that matches the task — CLI for power users, portal for exploration.

Q5
What is Docs-as-Code? How does it improve platform documentation?
Show Answer
Answer

Docs-as-Code treats documentation like software: written in Markdown, stored in Git, reviewed in PRs, and auto-published via CI. This ensures docs are versioned alongside code, reviewed by peers, and never go stale. Backstage TechDocs renders repo Markdown automatically — docs live next to the service.

Q6
What is an ADR? Give an example for the anihpj platform.
Show Answer
Answer

ADR (Architecture Decision Record) documents WHY a technical decision was made, with context and tradeoffs. Example: "ADR-003: We chose PostgreSQL over MongoDB for anihpj because the job posting data is highly relational (Users, Skills, Locations, JobPosts with complex JOINs). MongoDB would require denormalization that adds complexity."

Q7
What is cognitive load in the context of developer experience? How do you reduce it?
Show Answer
Answer

Cognitive load is the mental effort required to use the platform. Reduce it by: hiding K8s complexity behind simple commands (platform deploy instead of kubectl apply -f 10 files), providing sensible defaults, and creating golden paths for common tasks. A developer deploying a Django app shouldn't need to understand Deployments, Services, Ingress, and ConfigMaps.

Q8
What are runbooks? Why are they essential for platform operations?
Show Answer
Answer

Runbooks are step-by-step guides for handling specific incidents: "When anihpj API returns 500s → check logs in Loki for trace ID → open trace in Tempo → check DB connection → restart if needed." They reduce MTTR (Mean Time to Recovery) by eliminating guesswork during outages. Store runbooks alongside service code in Backstage TechDocs.

Q9
What is self-service in platform engineering? Why is it important?
Show Answer
Answer

Self-service means developers can provision resources (namespaces, databases, TLS certs) WITHOUT filing tickets or waiting for platform team approval. Important because: it eliminates wait time (from days to seconds), empowers developers, and frees platform engineers from repetitive manual tasks. The platform enforces guardrails (policies) automatically.

Q10
You're designing the anihpj platform. What's your 5-minute developer quickstart experience?
Show Answer
Answer

1. platform login (authenticate once). 2. platform init anihpj (scaffold a new service from template). 3. Add code. 4. git push (CI builds, scans, and deploys automatically). 5. Open Backstage → see your service live with URL, metrics, and logs. Total: 5 minutes from idea to running in staging. No K8s YAML needed.

Chapter 20: Service Catalogs and InnerSource

A service catalog is the single source of truth for every service in your platform — who owns it, what it depends on, and whether it's healthy. InnerSource applies open-source practices inside your company, turning the platform into a shared community project. Together, they make your platform discoverable and collaborative.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 📋 Catalog 🔌 Domain 3 (25%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Building a service catalog: metadata, dependencies, health, ownership
  • ✅ Service scorecards: measuring production readiness
  • ✅ InnerSource: open-source practices inside your organization
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🔌 Platform APIs & Self-Service 25%

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — Service Catalogs

Expect scenario questions about service discovery and ownership: "A developer needs to find who owns the anihpj API during an incident. How does your platform provide this?" Know the service catalog metadata fields (owner, repo, on-call, dependencies, SLO), how scorecards measure production readiness, and how InnerSource practices (CONTRIBUTING.md, CODEOWNERS) enable cross-team collaboration. Backstage is the reference implementation — know its key plugins.

20.1 Building a Service Catalog

A service catalog answers the questions every developer asks: "What services exist? Who owns this service? Is it healthy? How do I contact the team?" Backstage is the leading open-source developer portal that implements the service catalog pattern.

20.2 Service Scorecards — Measuring Maturity

Scorecards evaluate services against production readiness criteria. They gamify quality — teams see their scores and compete to improve. Backstage's Tech Insights plugin automates scorecard evaluation.

CategoryCriteriaWeightanihpj Score
🔐 SecurityImage scanned? RBAC? mTLS? No critical CVEs?30%✅ 28/30
📊 ObservabilityMetrics? Dashboards? Alerts? Logs? Traces?25%✅ 23/25
📋 DocumentationREADME? Runbook? ADRs? API docs?20%⚠️ 12/20
🚀 DeploymentCI/CD? Canary? Rollback? GitOps?15%✅ 15/15
🧪 TestingUnit? Integration? E2E? Coverage > 80%?10%⚠️ 7/10
TOTAL85/100 (Bronze)
💡 Scorecard Tiers

Gold (95+): Fully production-ready, all best practices. Silver (85-94): Good, minor gaps. Bronze (70-84): Needs improvement. Below 70: Not production-ready — deploy blocked. Teams naturally compete for higher scores, creating a healthy quality culture without mandates. Backstage's Tech Insights plugin automates scorecard evaluation by checking facts about each service — owner exists? SLOs defined? CVEs patched? — and produces live scores.

20.3 InnerSource — Open Source Inside Your Company

InnerSource applies open-source practices internally: anyone can read the code, submit PRs, and contribute improvements — even if they're not on the owning team. The platform itself becomes an InnerSource project.

InnerSource Workflow

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    INNERSOURCE FLOW                               │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ① DISCOVER                                                      │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Developer from Team B finds anihpj in the service catalog  │  │
  │  │ → Sees it's missing Redis connection pooling               │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                          │                                       │
  │                          ▼                                       │
  │  ② CONTRIBUTE                                                    │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Reads CONTRIBUTING.md → forks repo → adds pooling config   │  │
  │  │ → Opens PR with description and tests                      │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                          │                                       │
  │                          ▼                                       │
  │  ③ REVIEW                                                        │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ CODEOWNERS (@anihpj-team) reviews PR → ✅ Approved          │  │
  │  │ → Merged → anihpj now has connection pooling!              │  │
  │  │ → Team B developer gets credit in CONTRIBUTORS.md          │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  🔑 Key: CODEOWNERS protects quality, CONTRIBUTING.md guides    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Open READMEClear architecture, decisions, getting startedArchitecture diagram + 5-min quickstart
Good First IssuesTagged issues for new contributors"Add Redis connection pooling — good first issue"

📝 Chapter 20 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What is a service catalog? What key information should it contain?
Show Answer
Answer

A service catalog is the single source of truth for every service. It should contain: metadata (owner, repo, Slack channel, on-call), dependencies (what it depends on, what depends on it), health (SLO compliance, error rates, build status), and documentation links. Backstage is the leading open-source implementation.

Q2
What are service scorecards? How do they improve platform quality?
Show Answer
Answer

Scorecards evaluate services against production readiness criteria (security, observability, docs, deployment, testing) and produce a score. They improve quality through gamification — teams naturally compete for higher scores. Gold/Silver/Bronze tiers create clear quality expectations without requiring top-down mandates.

Q3
What is InnerSource? How does it differ from traditional internal development?
Show Answer
Answer

InnerSource applies open-source practices inside a company: anyone can read code, submit PRs, and contribute — even from other teams. Traditional development: only the owning team touches the code. InnerSource breaks down silos, accelerates improvements, and spreads knowledge across teams organically.

Q4
What files are essential for an InnerSource project?
Show Answer
Answer

CONTRIBUTING.md: How to set up, test, and submit PRs. CODEOWNERS: Who must approve changes. README.md: Architecture, getting started, decisions. Good First Issues labels: Tagged tasks for new contributors. These four files lower the barrier for cross-team contributions.

Q5
How does Backstage implement a service catalog? What plugin provides scorecards?
Show Answer
Answer

Backstage uses catalog-info.yaml files in each repo to register services. The Tech Insights plugin evaluates scorecards automatically by checking facts about each service (is there an owner? are SLOs defined? are CVEs patched?). Services are auto-scored and ranked.

Q6
What's the difference between CODEOWNERS and contributing guidelines?
Show Answer
Answer

CODEOWNERS: Defines WHO must approve changes to specific files — it's a gate. CONTRIBUTING.md: Defines HOW to contribute — setup instructions, coding standards, PR process. CODEOWNERS protects quality; CONTRIBUTING.md enables contributions. Both are essential for InnerSource.

Q7
You're building an anihpj service catalog entry. What 5 metadata fields are mandatory?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Owner (team + Slack channel). 2. Repository (GitHub/GitLab URL). 3. On-call (PagerDuty service). 4. Dependencies (what services it depends on). 5. Health/SLO (current uptime/latency vs targets). Without these 5, you can't answer "who owns this?" or "is it healthy?" — the two most critical questions during incidents.

Q8
What are the benefits of gamifying service quality with scorecards?
Show Answer
Answer

Gamification creates intrinsic motivation: teams want to see their score go up, so they proactively improve documentation, add tests, and fix security issues. It's more effective than mandates ("you MUST have 80% coverage") because teams feel ownership. Leaderboards create healthy competition without punitive enforcement.

Q9
How do you prevent InnerSource from becoming chaos? What guardrails are needed?
Show Answer
Answer

Guardrails: (1) CODEOWNERS ensures the owning team always approves changes. (2) CI checks (tests, linting, security scans) must pass before merge. (3) CONTRIBUTING.md sets clear expectations. (4) Branch protection prevents direct pushes to main. InnerSource is NOT "anyone can merge anything" — it's "anyone can propose changes, owners decide."

Q10
Your anihpj platform has 50 microservices. How do you ensure every service has a catalog entry?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Require catalog-info.yaml in every repo (enforced by repo template). 2. Backstage auto-discovers services from GitHub. 3. Scorecard penalizes services without catalog entries ("Documentation: 0/20"). 4. Make the catalog the DEFAULT way to find services — if you're not in the catalog, you don't exist to other teams. This creates organic adoption.

The CLI is the primary interface for power users and automation. As a CNPE, you'll build custom kubectl plugins, integrate developer tools (Tilt, Skaffold, Telepresence), and create scaffolding templates that make spinning up new services a one-command experience. This is how you make the platform feel like magic.

  • ✅ Developer CLI tools: Tilt, Skaffold, DevSpace, Telepresence

🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🔌 Platform APIs & Self-Service 25%

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — Platform CLI & Developer Tools

The CNPE exam tests your understanding of developer productivity tools. Expect questions like: "How would you reduce the inner dev loop from 5 minutes to 10 seconds?" or "What tool would you recommend for intercepting staging traffic to a local machine?" Know Tilt (live update), Telepresence (traffic intercept), and Skaffold (build→push→deploy). Also know how kubectl plugins and krew distribute platform-specific CLI tools, and how Backstage Software Templates automate service scaffolding.

21.1 Building Custom kubectl Plugins

kubectl plugins extend the CLI with platform-specific commands. Any executable named kubectl-* on your PATH becomes a kubectl command. Distribute plugins via krew — the kubectl plugin manager.

#!/bin/bash
# kubectl-anihpj — Custom plugin for anihpj platform operations
# Usage: kubectl anihpj deploy [service] [env]

case "$1" in
  deploy)
    SERVICE=${2:-anihpj-api}
    ENV=${3:-staging}
    echo "🚀 Deploying $SERVICE to $ENV..."
    # Tag image with git SHA
    TAG=$(git rev-parse --short HEAD)
    docker build -t registry.io/$SERVICE:$TAG .
    docker push registry.io/$SERVICE:$TAG
    # Update image tag in Git (triggers ArgoCD sync)
    yq -i ".image.tag = \"$TAG\"" "overlays/$ENV/values.yaml"
    git add "overlays/$ENV/values.yaml"
    git commit -m "deploy: $SERVICE to $ENV ($TAG)"
    git push
    echo "✅ Deployed! ArgoCD syncing..."
    ;;
  logs)
    # Stream logs from Loki for a specific service
    kubectl logs -l app="$2" --tail=100 -f
    ;;
  status)
    # Show deployment status from ArgoCD
    argocd app get "$2" --refresh
    ;;
  *)
    echo "anihpj CLI — Platform Operations"
    echo "  deploy [service] [env]  Deploy a service"
    echo "  logs [service]          Stream service logs"
    echo "  status [app]            Show ArgoCD app status"
    ;;
esac

# Install: chmod +x kubectl-anihpj && mv kubectl-anihpj /usr/local/bin/
# Now: kubectl anihpj deploy anihpj-api prod
📖 krew — The kubectl Plugin Manager

Krew lets you discover, install, and update kubectl plugins. Publish your kubectl-anihpj plugin to the krew-index, and developers install it with kubectl krew install anihpj. This is how you distribute platform CLI tools at scale — no manual PATH setup, no version management headaches.

21.2 Developer CLI Tools — Local K8s Development

These tools make developing against Kubernetes fast and enjoyable. They handle the "code → build → push → deploy → test" loop automatically.

ToolHow It WorksBest ForKey Feature
TiltWatches code → rebuilds → live-updates pods in-clusterFast inner dev loop, multi-serviceLive Update — no full rebuild
SkaffoldDetects changes → builds → pushes → deploys to K8sSimple single-service devBuilt-in profiling & debugging
DevSpaceDevelop inside K8s with hot reloadComplex apps needing real K8s contextTerminal + port-forward to pod
TelepresenceConnect local machine to remote K8s clusterDebugging prod-like issues locallyIntercept remote traffic to local
GardenFull-stack K8s dev + test + CITeams needing consistent envsDeclarative dev environments
🔧 When to Use What

Tilt: Your daily driver — write code, save, see changes in 2 seconds (not 2 minutes). Telepresence: "This bug only happens in staging" — intercept staging traffic to your local machine, debug with your IDE, then disconnect. Skaffold: Simple Go/Node.js services where you just need build→push→deploy. DevSpace: You need actual K8s networking (Service Meshes, NetworkPolicies) to work during development.

21.3 Scaffolding Tools — One Command to a Running Service

Scaffolding generates a complete, production-ready service skeleton from a template. Developers run one command and get: a repo with Dockerfile, Helm chart, CI pipeline, and deployment config — all following your platform standards.

Scaffolding Tools — Comparison

ToolHow It WorksBest For
Backstage TemplatesUI form → cookiecutter → GitHub repo + CI + catalogPlatform-native, full integration
CookiecutterJinja2 templates with interactive promptsFlexible scaffolding for any project type
YeomanNode.js-based generator ecosystemJavaScript/Node.js projects, rich plugin library
Custom ScriptBash/Python script + template repoSimple, lightweight, no external dependencies

Backstage Software Templates

Backstage's most powerful feature: developers fill out a form in the portal, click "Create," and Backstage scaffolds a new service with everything included. The template is defined in YAML and can use Cookiecutter underneath for flexible code generation.

# Backstage Template — Scaffold a new microservice
apiVersion: scaffolder.backstage.io/v1beta3
kind: Template
metadata:
  name: anihpj-microservice
  title: Create a new anihpj Microservice
spec:
  owner: platform-team
  type: service
  parameters:
    - title: Service Details
      required: [name, owner]
      properties:
        name:
          title: Service Name
          type: string
        owner:
          title: Team Owner
          type: string
        python_version:
          title: Python Version
          type: string
          enum: ["3.10", "3.11", "3.12"]
          default: "3.12"
  steps:
    - id: fetch-base
      action: fetch:cookiecutter
      input:
        url: https://github.com/anihpj/cookiecutter-service
    - id: publish
      action: publish:github
      input:
        repoUrl: github.com/anihpj/{{ parameters.name }}
    - id: register
      action: catalog:register
      input:
        catalogInfoPath: /catalog-info.yaml
💡 The Scaffolding Golden Path

A well-designed scaffold gives developers: (1) Dockerfile with best practices (non-root, minimal base), (2) Helm chart with sensible defaults, (3) CI pipeline (GitHub Actions), (4) ArgoCD Application manifest, (5) Backstage catalog-info.yaml, (6) README with getting started. One command, 30 seconds, and the developer is writing business logic — not wiring up infrastructure.
