Q1
How do kubectl plugins work? How would you distribute a platform-specific plugin?
Show Answer
Answer

Any executable named kubectl-* on your PATH becomes a kubectl command via kubectl *. Distribute plugins through krew (the kubectl plugin manager) by publishing to the krew-index. Developers install with kubectl krew install anihpj. This provides versioned, discoverable distribution without manual PATH management.

Q2
Write a simple kubectl plugin that deploys a service. What commands would it include?
Show Answer
Answer

A bash script named kubectl-anihpj with commands: deploy [service] [env] (build, tag, push, update Git → ArgoCD syncs), logs [service] (stream logs from Loki), status [app] (show ArgoCD sync status). The plugin abstracts K8s complexity: developers don't need to know kubectl, Docker, or ArgoCD — just kubectl anihpj deploy.

Q3
Compare Tilt, Skaffold, and Telepresence. When would you recommend each?
Show Answer
Answer

Tilt: Fast inner dev loop with live updates — best daily driver for multi-service apps. Skaffold: Simple build→push→deploy for single services. Telepresence: Debug prod-like issues locally by intercepting remote cluster traffic. Tilt for development speed, Telepresence for debugging, Skaffold for simplicity.

Q4
What is scaffolding in platform engineering? What should a scaffolded service include?
Show Answer
Answer

Scaffolding generates a production-ready service skeleton from a template. It should include: Dockerfile (non-root, minimal base), Helm chart (sensible defaults), CI pipeline (GitHub Actions), ArgoCD Application manifest, catalog-info.yaml (Backstage), and a getting-started README. One command → 30 seconds → developer is writing business logic.

Q5
How do Backstage Software Templates work? What problem do they solve?
Show Answer
Answer

Backstage Templates provide a UI form → fill in service name, owner, language → Backstage scaffolds a complete repo with Dockerfile, CI, Helm chart, and registers it in the catalog. They solve the "blank page problem" — developers know what to build but not how to set up all the infrastructure. Templates encode your platform standards.

Q6
What is krew? How does it help distribute platform tools?
Show Answer
Answer

Krew is the kubectl plugin manager — like brew/apt for kubectl plugins. It provides discovery (kubectl krew search), installation (kubectl krew install), and updates. Publishing your platform plugin to krew-index means developers anywhere can install it with one command. No manual downloads, no PATH setup, no version management.

Q7
What is the "inner dev loop"? How do Tilt/Telepresence improve it?
Show Answer
Answer

The inner dev loop is: write code → build → push to registry → deploy to K8s → test → repeat. Without tools: 2-5 minutes per iteration. With Tilt: 2 seconds (live-update without rebuild). With Telepresence: run locally, intercept remote K8s traffic. Faster loops = happier developers = more features shipped.

Q8
What should a "golden path" scaffold include for a Django service like anihpj?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Dockerfile (Python 3.12, distroless base, non-root). 2. Helm chart (Deployment + Service + Ingress + HPA + ConfigMap). 3. GitHub Actions CI (lint → test → build → scan → push). 4. ArgoCD Application manifest. 5. catalog-info.yaml (Backstage). 6. values files for dev/staging/prod. 7. CONTRIBUTING.md. 8. README with 5-min quickstart.

Q9
How does DevSpace differ from Tilt? When would you choose DevSpace?
Show Answer
Answer

Tilt: Updates code inside running pods — fast, lightweight. DevSpace: Opens a terminal INSIDE a pod — you're literally developing inside K8s with full network context. Choose DevSpace when you need actual K8s networking (Service Mesh traffic routing, NetworkPolicy enforcement) to work during development. Choose Tilt for pure speed.

Q10
You're building the anihpj platform CLI. What's the ONE command a new developer runs to deploy their first service?
Show Answer
Answer

kubectl anihpj create my-service — this single command: (1) scaffolds a new repo from the Backstage template, (2) clones it locally, (3) sets up Tilt for live development, (4) creates the ArgoCD Application, (5) prints the service URL. The developer is now coding with live updates within 60 seconds. This is the holy grail of platform DX.

Chapter 22: Multi-Cluster Kubernetes

One cluster is great. Multiple clusters are the reality for any serious platform. Multi-cluster architectures provide high availability, environment isolation, geographic distribution, and blast radius reduction. As a CNPE, you'll use Cluster API to manage cluster lifecycles and ArgoCD to deploy across them all. This is 15% of the exam.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~2 hours 🌐 Multi-Cluster 🏗️ Domain 1 (15%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Why multi-cluster: HA, isolation, geo-distribution, blast radius
  • ✅ Hub-and-Spoke vs Mesh topologies — tradeoffs and use cases
  • ✅ Cluster API (CAPI): declarative cluster lifecycle management
  • ✅ Multi-cluster GitOps with ArgoCD ApplicationSets
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🏗️ Platform Architecture & Infrastructure 15%

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — Multi-Cluster

Expect scenario questions: "Your production cluster went down. How does your multi-cluster architecture handle this?" Know the pros/cons of hub-and-spoke vs mesh, how Cluster API manages cluster lifecycles declaratively, and how ArgoCD ApplicationSets fan out deployments across multiple clusters. The exam tests your ability to design for resilience at the cluster level — not just pod level.

22.1 Why Multi-Cluster?

A single cluster is a single point of failure. Multi-cluster architectures solve real problems:

ReasonProblem Solvedanihpj Example
High AvailabilityCluster failure doesn't take everything down2 prod clusters in us-east-1 and us-west-2
Environment IsolationDev can't break StagingSeparate clusters for dev/staging/prod
Geographic DistributionUsers routed to nearest clusterEU cluster for European anihpj users
Blast Radius ReductionBad change affects 1 cluster, not allCanary cluster before full rollout
Team AutonomyTeams own their clustersanihpj-team cluster, mobile-team cluster
💥 Blast Radius — Why One Cluster Isn't Enough

Imagine anihpj runs in a SINGLE production cluster. A developer deploys a new version with a bug that corrupts the database. Everything goes down — API, job board, admin panel. Now imagine two clusters: the bad deploy hits cluster-A. Cluster-B keeps serving users from a read replica. The platform team shifts 100% traffic to cluster-B via Route53 DNS failover. Blast radius: 50% of users affected for 2 minutes instead of 100% of users for 2 hours. Multi-cluster isn't about avoiding failures — it's about surviving them.

22.2 Multi-Cluster Architectures

Multi-Cluster Topologies — Platform Engineer's Guide

  🏗️ HUB-AND-SPOKE                     🌐 MESH
  ┌──────────────────────┐            ┌──────────────────────┐
  │                      │            │                      │
  │    ┌──────────┐      │            │  ┌────┐    ┌────┐   │
  │    │ HUB (Mgmt│      │            │  │K8s │◀══▶│K8s │   │
  │    │ Cluster) │      │            │  │ A  │    │ B  │   │
  │    └────┬─────┘      │            │  └──┬─┘    └──┬─┘   │
  │    ┌────┼────┐       │            │     │        │      │
  │    ▼    ▼    ▼       │            │     └───┬────┘      │
  │  ┌───┐┌───┐┌───┐    │            │         ▼           │
  │  │Dev││Stg││Prod│    │            │      ┌────┐         │
  │  └───┘└───┘└───┘    │            │      │K8s │         │
  │                      │            │      │ C  │         │
  │  ✅ Simple           │            │      └────┘         │
  │  ✅ Central mgmt     │            │                      │
  │  ❌ Hub is SPOF      │            │  ✅ No SPOF           │
  └──────────────────────┘            │  ❌ Complex networking│
                                      └──────────────────────┘

  🎯 anihpj: Hub-and-spoke (1 mgmt + dev/staging/prod workload clusters)

Hub-and-Spoke

One management cluster runs ArgoCD, monitoring, and policy engines. Workload clusters (dev, staging, prod) run applications only. Simple to manage, but the hub becomes a critical dependency.

Mesh Topology

All clusters are peers — no central hub. Each cluster can communicate with others via service mesh federation. More resilient (no SPOF) but significantly more complex to network and manage. Used by large enterprises with 50+ clusters.

Fleet Management

Managing many clusters requires fleet-level tools: Cluster API for lifecycle, ArgoCD for deployments, and centralized observability (Thanos/Cortex for multi-cluster Prometheus). The goal: manage 50 clusters with the same effort as managing 5.

22.3 Cluster API (CAPI) — Declarative Cluster Lifecycle

Cluster API lets you manage clusters like any other K8s resource — create, update, scale, and delete clusters via kubectl. It uses a management cluster to provision and manage workload clusters. Infrastructure providers (AWS, GCP, Azure, vSphere, Docker) handle the actual resource creation. Bootstrap providers (kubeadm, EKS, MicroK8s) handle cluster setup. The Docker provider is especially useful — it creates local kind-like clusters for testing CAPI configurations without cloud costs.

# Define a new cluster declaratively
apiVersion: cluster.x-k8s.io/v1beta1
kind: Cluster
metadata:
  name: anihpj-prod-us-west
spec:
  clusterNetwork:
    pods:
      cidrBlocks: ["192.168.0.0/16"]
    services:
      cidrBlocks: ["10.96.0.0/12"]
  infrastructureRef:
    apiVersion: infrastructure.cluster.x-k8s.io/v1beta2
    kind: AWSManagedCluster
    name: anihpj-prod-us-west
---
# Define the control plane (EKS managed)
apiVersion: infrastructure.cluster.x-k8s.io/v1beta2
kind: AWSManagedControlPlane
metadata:
  name: anihpj-prod-us-west
spec:
  region: us-west-2
  version: v1.29.0
  eksClusterName: anihpj-prod-us-west
---
# Define worker nodes
apiVersion: cluster.x-k8s.io/v1beta1
kind: MachineDeployment
metadata:
  name: anihpj-prod-us-west-workers
spec:
  clusterName: anihpj-prod-us-west
  replicas: 3
  template:
    spec:
      bootstrap:
        configRef:
          apiVersion: bootstrap.cluster.x-k8s.io/v1beta1
          kind: KubeadmConfigTemplate
          name: anihpj-workers
      infrastructureRef:
        apiVersion: infrastructure.cluster.x-k8s.io/v1beta2
        kind: AWSMachineTemplate
        name: anihpj-workers-t3-large

22.4 Multi-Cluster GitOps — ArgoCD Fan-Out

One ArgoCD instance can manage many clusters. Use ApplicationSets to deploy the same application to multiple clusters with cluster-specific configuration.

Multi-Cluster GitOps — anihpj Setup

  🔄 ARGOCD (Management Cluster)
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  App-of-Apps (Root App)                                    │
  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐               │
  │  │AppSet    │  │AppSet    │  │AppSet    │               │
  │  │Platform  │  │Dev Teams │  │Infra     │               │
  │  │(Argo,    │  │(anihpj,  │  │(Crossplane│              │
  │  │ Prom,    │  │ jobpost, │  │ comps)    │              │
  │  │ Kyverno) │  │ redis)   │  │          │               │
  │  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘               │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         │                  │                  │
         ▼                  ▼                  ▼
  ┌────────────┐    ┌────────────┐    ┌────────────┐
  │ ☸️ DEV     │    │ ☸️ STAGING │    │ ☸️ PROD    │
  │ Cluster    │    │ Cluster    │    │ Cluster    │
  │ Auto-deploy│    │ Auto-deploy│    │ Manual     │
  │ on push    │    │ on PR merge│    │ promotion  │
  └────────────┘    └────────────┘    └────────────┘

📝 Chapter 22 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What are 5 reasons to adopt a multi-cluster architecture?
Show Answer
Answer

1. High Availability — cluster failure doesn't take everything down. 2. Environment Isolation — dev can't break prod. 3. Geographic Distribution — serve users from nearest cluster. 4. Blast Radius Reduction — limit damage from bad changes. 5. Team Autonomy — teams own their clusters independently.

Q2
Compare Hub-and-Spoke vs Mesh topology. When would you choose each?
Show Answer
Answer

Hub-and-Spoke: Central management cluster, simple operations, but hub is a SPOF. Best for most teams (1 hub + 3-10 workload clusters). Mesh: All clusters are peers, no SPOF, but complex networking. Best for 50+ clusters with geo-distribution. For anihpj: Hub-and-spoke is the pragmatic choice.

Q3
What is Cluster API (CAPI)? How does it manage cluster lifecycles?
Show Answer
Answer

CAPI manages K8s clusters declaratively via the K8s API. You define Cluster, ControlPlane, and MachineDeployment resources — CAPI controllers reconcile them to create real clusters on AWS, GCP, Azure, vSphere, or even Docker. Same pattern as K8s: desired state → controller → actual state.

Q4
How does ArgoCD manage deployments across multiple clusters?
Show Answer
Answer

ArgoCD can register multiple clusters as deployment targets. Use ApplicationSets to fan out deployments — one ApplicationSet can deploy the same app to dev, staging, and prod clusters with cluster-specific values. The management cluster runs ArgoCD; workload clusters just receive deployments via their K8s API.

Q5
What is a management cluster? What runs there vs workload clusters?
Show Answer
Answer

A management cluster runs platform infrastructure: ArgoCD, Prometheus, Grafana, Kyverno, Crossplane, cert-manager. Workload clusters run only applications (anihpj, jobpost, redis). This separation keeps platform tools isolated from application workloads, reducing the blast radius of platform failures.

Q6
What infrastructure providers does Cluster API support?
Show Answer
Answer

AWS (AWSManagedCluster, EKS), GCP (GCPManagedCluster, GKE), Azure (AzureManagedCluster, AKS), vSphere (on-prem VMware), Docker (local testing with kind-like clusters), and community providers for DigitalOcean, Hetzner, and more.

Q7
What happens if your management cluster (Hub) goes down in a hub-and-spoke setup?
Show Answer
Answer

Workload clusters continue running applications — they don't depend on the hub for runtime. However: you can't deploy new changes (ArgoCD is down), can't view centralized dashboards (Grafana is down), and can't manage cluster lifecycles (CAPI is down). The hub is a management SPOF, not a runtime SPOF. Restore the hub from backup.

Q8
How do you bootstrap a new cluster "from zero" with GitOps?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Provision infrastructure (VPC, subnets) with Tofu/Terraform. 2. Create the K8s cluster with CAPI or managed K8s. 3. Install ArgoCD via Helm (or Tofu Helm provider). 4. ArgoCD syncs the App-of-Apps from Git — which deploys all platform components and team applications. From bare metal to running apps entirely via Git.

Q9
What's the difference between an ArgoCD Application and ApplicationSet?
Show Answer
Answer

Application: Deploys ONE app to ONE cluster. ApplicationSet: Generation template that creates multiple Applications — e.g., deploy anihpj to dev, staging, AND prod clusters with different values per cluster. ApplicationSets are the multi-cluster fan-out mechanism in ArgoCD.

Q10
You're designing anihpj for global deployment. What's your multi-cluster architecture?
Show Answer
Answer

Hub-and-spoke: 1 management cluster in us-east-1 running ArgoCD + monitoring. Workload clusters: dev (us-east-1), staging (us-east-1), prod-us (us-east-1), prod-eu (eu-west-1). ApplicationSet deploys anihpj to all 4 workload clusters. Global DNS (Route53 latency-based) routes users to nearest prod cluster. Crossplane provisions per-cluster DB instances.

Chapter 23: Service Mesh — Istio & Linkerd

A service mesh is a dedicated infrastructure layer that handles service-to-service communication transparently. It provides mTLS, traffic splitting, retries, circuit breaking, and observability — all without changing application code. Istio is the most feature-rich; Linkerd is the simplest. This is 15% of the exam.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~2 hours 🕸️ Service Mesh 🏗️ Domain 1 (15%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Service mesh architecture: sidecar proxy, data plane, control plane
  • ✅ Istio: VirtualService, DestinationRule, Gateway, PeerAuthentication
  • ✅ Istio for anihpj: canary, circuit breaking, fault injection, mTLS
  • ✅ Linkerd: the simpler alternative — when to choose it
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🏗️ Platform Architecture & Infrastructure 15%

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — Service Mesh

Expect scenario-based questions: "How would you implement canary deployments across multiple versions of anihpj?" or "What's the simplest way to enable mTLS between all services?" Know the Istio resources (VirtualService, DestinationRule, Gateway, PeerAuthentication), the difference between PERMISSIVE and STRICT mTLS, and when to choose Linkerd over Istio (simplicity vs features). The exam tests practical mesh configuration, not just theory.

23.1 What is a Service Mesh?

A service mesh separates business logic (your app code) from communication logic (TLS, retries, routing). It injects a sidecar proxy (Envoy for Istio, linkerd-proxy for Linkerd) alongside every pod. All traffic flows through the proxy, which handles everything transparently.

Service Mesh Architecture — Sidecar Proxy Pattern

  WITHOUT SERVICE MESH                    WITH SERVICE MESH
  ┌──────────────────────┐              ┌──────────────────────┐
  │                      │              │                      │
  │  anihpj-web          │              │  ┌────────┐ ┌──────┐│
  │                      │              │  │ anihpj │ │Envoy ││
  │  App handles:        │              │  │ -web   │ │Proxy ││
  │  • TLS               │              │  └────────┘ └──┬───┘│
  │  • Retry logic       │              │                 │    │
  │  • Circuit breaking  │              │  ┌────────┐ ┌──▼───┐│
  │  • Timeouts          │              │  │Redis   │ │Envoy ││
  │  • Metrics           │              │  │        │ │Proxy ││
  │                      │              │  └────────┘ └──────┘│
  │  App is complex      │              │                      │
  │  and bloated         │              │  App is simple.      │
  │                      │              │  Proxy handles comm. │
  └──────────────────────┘              └──────────────────────┘

  CONTROL PLANE (istiod / Linkerd controller):
  • Distributes certificates for mTLS
  • Pushes routing configuration to proxies
  • Collects telemetry
🤔 Do You NEED a Service Mesh?

YES if: you need mTLS everywhere, complex traffic routing (canary, A/B testing), circuit breaking, or distributed tracing without code changes. NO if: you have simple apps, use NetworkPolicies + cert-manager for security, and your observability tools already trace requests. Service meshes add operational complexity — deploy one only when the benefits outweigh the costs. For anihpj: start without a mesh. Add Linkerd later if mTLS or traffic splitting becomes essential.

23.2 Istio Deep Dive

Istio uses istiod (unified control plane) and Envoy (data plane sidecar). It provides the richest feature set of any service mesh. Key Istio resources define traffic behavior:

ResourceWhat It DoesExample
VirtualServiceRoutes traffic based on headers, paths, weights"10% → v2, 90% → v1" (canary)
DestinationRuleTraffic policy: load balancing, TLS, circuit breaking"Max 5 concurrent connections to DB"
GatewayIngress traffic into the mesh"Allow HTTPS on port 443 for anihpj.example.com"
PeerAuthenticationmTLS enforcement (PERMISSIVE or STRICT)"All traffic in anihpj namespace MUST use mTLS"

23.3 Istio for anihpj Platform

# Canary deployment: 10% traffic → v2, 90% → v1
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: VirtualService
metadata:
  name: anihpj-canary
spec:
  hosts:
    - anihpj-api
  http:
    - route:
        - destination:
            host: anihpj-api
            subset: v1
          weight: 90
        - destination:
            host: anihpj-api
            subset: v2
          weight: 10

---
# Circuit breaking: protect anihpj from DB failures
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: DestinationRule
metadata:
  name: postgres-circuit-breaker
spec:
  host: postgresql.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
  trafficPolicy:
    connectionPool:
      tcp:
        maxConnections: 100
      http:
        http1MaxPendingRequests: 10
        maxRequestsPerConnection: 5
    outlierDetection:
      consecutive5xxErrors: 5
      interval: 30s
      baseEjectionTime: 60s
      maxEjectionPercent: 50

With these rules: if PostgreSQL starts failing, Istio ejects failing instances from the pool for 60 seconds, protecting anihpj from cascading failures. The canary routes 10% of users to the new version — if errors spike, rollback by setting v2 weight to 0.

Fault Injection & Observability

Istio can inject faults to test resilience: add 5-second delays to 10% of requests or return HTTP 500 for 2% of traffic — all via VirtualService configuration, no code changes. This lets you chaos-test anihpj's error handling in production-like conditions. Telemetry is automatic: Istio generates metrics (request rate, latency, error rate), distributed traces, and access logs for every request through the mesh — exported to Prometheus, Tempo, and Loki for full observability. Kiali visualizes the service graph, showing real-time traffic flow, error rates, and latency between all services in the mesh.

23.4 Linkerd — The Simpler Alternative

AspectIstioLinkerd
ComplexityHigh — 10+ CRDs, many conceptsLow — install in 5 minutes
Resource Usage~150MB per sidecar (Envoy)~10MB per sidecar (Rust proxy)
mTLSPer-namespace/per-workload configAuto-mTLS everywhere — no config
Traffic SplittingRich (headers, paths, weights)Basic (weight-based only)
Circuit Breaking✅ Advanced outlier detection❌ Not supported (by design)
DashboardsKiali (service graph, topology)linkerd-viz (built-in, simpler)
Best ForComplex traffic management, multi-clusterSimple zero-trust mTLS, smaller teams
CNPE PickWhen you need advanced traffic controlDefault choice for most platforms
💡 CNPE Recommendation

Start with Linkerd if you just need mTLS everywhere with zero config. Upgrade to Istio only when you need advanced traffic splitting (canary by header), circuit breaking with outlier detection, or multi-cluster mesh federation. For the exam: know both, but Linkerd is the practical starting point for most platforms.

📝 Chapter 23 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What is a service mesh? What problems does it solve?
Show Answer
Answer

A service mesh is a dedicated infrastructure layer that handles service-to-service communication via sidecar proxies. It solves: mTLS (encryption + identity), traffic management (canary, retries, timeouts), circuit breaking (failure isolation), and observability (metrics, traces) — all without application code changes.

Q2
Explain the sidecar proxy pattern. What are the two planes in a service mesh?
Show Answer
Answer

Sidecar proxy: A proxy container (Envoy/linkerd-proxy) injected alongside every pod, intercepting all traffic transparently. Data plane: All sidecar proxies handling actual traffic. Control plane: Management component (istiod/Linkerd controller) that distributes certs, pushes config, and collects telemetry.

Q3
What is a VirtualService in Istio? What is a DestinationRule? How do they work together?
Show Answer
Answer

VirtualService: Routes traffic — "10% to v2, 90% to v1." DestinationRule: Applies traffic policy to a destination — "use LEAST_CONN load balancing, enable mTLS, circuit breaker settings." They work together: VirtualService decides WHERE traffic goes; DestinationRule decides HOW traffic gets there.

Q4
How would you implement a canary deployment for anihpj using Istio?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Deploy v1 and v2 as separate Deployments with labels version: v1 and version: v2. 2. Create DestinationRules defining subsets v1 and v2. 3. Create a VirtualService with weights: 90% → v1, 10% → v2. 4. Monitor error rates. If v2 is healthy, gradually increase weight to 100%. If errors spike, set v2 weight to 0 for instant rollback.

Q5
What is circuit breaking? How does Istio implement it?
Show Answer
Answer

Circuit breaking stops sending traffic to failing services to prevent cascading failures. Istio implements it via DestinationRule's outlierDetection: if PostgreSQL returns 5 consecutive 5xx errors, Istio ejects it from the pool for 60 seconds. During ejection, requests go to healthy instances. This protects anihpj from DB failures.

Q6
Compare Istio and Linkerd. When would you recommend each?
Show Answer
Answer

Linkerd: Simpler, 10MB sidecar, auto-mTLS, basic traffic splitting. Best for most teams that just need zero-trust mTLS. Istio: Feature-rich, 150MB Envoy sidecar, advanced routing (headers, paths), circuit breaking, multi-cluster mesh. Best for complex traffic management needs. Start with Linkerd; upgrade to Istio when needed.

Q7
What does PeerAuthentication STRICT mode do in Istio?
Show Answer
Answer

STRICT mode requires ALL traffic to use mTLS — any client that doesn't present a valid certificate is REJECTED. PERMISSIVE mode accepts both plaintext and mTLS, useful during migration. For anihpj: use STRICT in production namespaces to ensure all service-to-service communication is mutually authenticated and encrypted.

Q8
What is Kiali? How does it help platform engineers?
Show Answer
Answer

Kiali is Istio's observability console. It provides: service graph (visual topology of all services and their traffic), health dashboards (error rates, latency per service), traffic flow animation, and configuration validation (are your VirtualServices correct?). It's like having X-ray vision into your mesh's communication patterns.

Q9
Do you always need a service mesh? When should you NOT deploy one?
Show Answer
Answer

No — service meshes add complexity. Don't deploy one if: you have simple apps (few services), use NetworkPolicies + cert-manager for security, your observability tools already trace requests, or your team isn't ready for the operational overhead. For anihpj with 3 services: start without a mesh, add Linkerd only when mTLS or traffic management becomes essential.

Q10
You're running anihpj on Istio. PostgreSQL starts failing intermittently. How does the mesh help?
Show Answer
Answer

Istio's outlier detection (circuit breaker) detects PostgreSQL failures: after 5 consecutive 5xx errors within 30 seconds, Istio ejects that PostgreSQL instance for 60 seconds. Traffic is routed to healthy instances. Meanwhile, retries and timeouts configured in VirtualService prevent request pileup. anihpj users see degraded performance, not total failure.

Chapter 24: Edge Computing & Global Load Balancing

When your platform goes global, you need traffic routing across clusters, edge computing for low-latency workloads, and CDN integration for fast static content delivery. This chapter covers the infrastructure patterns that make your platform fast and available worldwide — 15% of the exam.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 🌍 Global 🏗️ Domain 1 (15%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Global traffic management: DNS, Anycast, multi-cluster load balancing
  • ✅ Kubernetes at the edge: K3s, MicroK8s, k0s for lightweight deployments
  • ✅ Content delivery & caching: CDN, static assets, API caching
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🏗️ Platform Architecture & Infrastructure 15%

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — Edge & Global Load Balancing

Expect questions about global traffic routing strategies: "How do you route European users to the EU cluster and US users to the US cluster?" Know DNS-based routing (Route53 latency), active-active vs active-passive tradeoffs, and CDN integration patterns for static assets. Also know the lightweight K8s distributions (K3s, MicroK8s, k0s) and their edge computing use cases. The exam tests your ability to design platforms that work at global scale.

24.1 Global Traffic Management

When you have clusters in multiple regions, you need to route users to the best cluster. Three approaches exist:

ApproachHow It WorksLatency-Based?Example
DNS-BasedDNS resolves to different IPs based on user location, latency, or health✅ Yes (latency routing)AWS Route53, GCP Cloud DNS, Azure Traffic Manager
AnycastSame IP advertised from multiple locations — network routes to nearest✅ Yes (BGP-based)Cloudflare, AWS Global Accelerator
Global LBL7 load balancer with backends in multiple clusters✅ Yes (proxy-based)GCP External HTTP(S) LB, Envoy global

Active-Active vs Active-Passive

ModeHow It WorksProsCons
Active-ActiveAll clusters serve traffic simultaneouslyFull resource utilization, zero failover timeData consistency challenges, higher cost
Active-PassiveOne cluster serves; standby on hot standbySimpler data model, lower costStandby resources idle, failover takes minutes
💡 CNPE Recommendation for anihpj

For anihpj (Django + PostgreSQL): use active-passive with Route53 failover. PostgreSQL replication is simpler with one writer. Use active-active only for stateless services (like the anihpj API frontend with a shared CDN-cached layer). Most platforms over-engineer active-active when active-passive is sufficient.

24.2 Kubernetes at the Edge

Edge computing brings compute closer to users — in retail stores, factories, cell towers, or IoT gateways. Lightweight K8s distributions make this practical on resource-constrained hardware.

DistributionResource UsageBest ForKey Feature
K3s~512MB RAM, single binaryEdge/IoT, ARM64, Raspberry PiPackaged as single <40MB binary
MicroK8s~600MB RAM, snap packageWorkstations, edge, CICanonical-supported, auto-updates
k0s~500MB RAM, single binaryAir-gapped, minimal footprintZero dependencies, no host deps
KubeEdge~256MB RAM (edge core)IoT/Edge with cloud connectionCloud-edge sync, offline autonomy

Edge Computing Patterns

Edge Architecture — Cloud + Edge Working Together

  ☁️ CLOUD LAYER (Central)                    📍 EDGE LAYER (Distributed)
  ┌────────────────────────────┐            ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ • Model training (GPU)     │            │ • Real-time inference (CPU) │
  │ • Global data aggregation  │◀───sync────│ • Local preprocessing       │
  │ • Long-term storage        │───models──▶│ • Offline-capable operation │
  │ • anihpj global API        │            │ • K3s cluster (512MB/node)  │
  │ • Central dashboards       │            │ • Local decision making     │
  └────────────────────────────┘            └────────────────────────────┘

  PATTERN 1: Pre-processing         PATTERN 2: Local Autonomy
  ┌────────────────────────┐        ┌────────────────────────┐
  │ Edge: Filter & compress│        │ Edge: Full local K3s   │
  │ raw data before upload │        │ cluster runs even when │
  │ → Reduce bandwidth 90% │        │ cloud is unreachable   │
  │ Example: IoT sensors   │        │ Example: Retail store   │
  │ compress video 10:1    │        │ POS works offline too   │
  └────────────────────────┘        └────────────────────────┘

24.3 Content Delivery & Caching

CDNs cache static content at edge locations worldwide — your users download images, CSS, and JS from a server near them, not from your origin cluster. For anihpj: Django serves dynamic API responses; CDN serves static assets (images, CSS, JavaScript).

CDN Architecture for anihpj

CDN Flow — User Request to Content Delivery

  👤 User in Tokyo
       │
       │ GET /static/logo.png
       ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ ① CLOUDFRONT EDGE (Tokyo, 5ms)                                   │
  │    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │    │ Is logo.png cached?  YES → Serve from edge (5ms) ✅      │  │
  │    │                       NO  → Fetch from origin ↓          │  │
  │    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
       │ (cache miss)
       ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ ② S3 ORIGIN (us-east-1, 150ms)                                   │
  │    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │    │ Static assets stored by collectstatic                      │  │
  │    │ → Serve to CloudFront → cached at edge                    │  │
  │    │ → Subsequent requests: 5ms from edge, not 150ms!          │  │
  │    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  CACHE HIERARCHY (fastest to slowest):
  ① CDN Edge (5ms) → ② Redis Cache (1ms, in-cluster) → ③ PostgreSQL (50ms)

# Django settings: static files → S3 → CloudFront CDN
STATIC_URL = "https://cdn.anihpj.com/static/"
MEDIA_URL = "https://cdn.anihpj.com/media/"

# Use django-storages for S3 backend
DEFAULT_FILE_STORAGE = "storages.backends.s3boto3.S3Boto3Storage"
STATICFILES_STORAGE = "storages.backends.s3boto3.S3StaticStorage"

# In CI/CD: collectstatic uploads to S3, CloudFront caches globally
python manage.py collectstatic --noinput
aws cloudfront create-invalidation --distribution-id ABC123 --paths "/*"

# Result: User in Tokyo downloads logo.png from Tokyo edge node (5ms)
#         not from us-east-1 origin server (200ms)

CDN Providers — Quick Comparison

ProviderEdge LocationsKey FeatureIntegration
CloudFront450+Lambda@Edge (compute at edge)S3 origin, Route53 DNS
Cloud CDN150+Global anycast IPGCS origin, Cloud DNS
Akamai4,100+Enterprise security featuresAny origin, advanced config
Cloudflare330+DDoS protection + WAF + CDNDNS proxying, Workers
📖 API Caching Strategies

Beyond static assets, cache dynamic API responses: (1) CDN cache — CloudFront caches GET /api/jobs/ for 60s at the edge. (2) Redis cache — anihpj caches frequent DB queries in Redis. (3) HTTP caching headers — Cache-Control: public, max-age=60 tells CDNs and browsers to cache responses. Layer these: CDN (fastest, edge) → Redis (fast, in-region) → PostgreSQL (slow, origin).

📝 Chapter 24 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What are the 3 approaches to global traffic management? Compare them.
Show Answer
Answer

DNS-Based (Route53): DNS resolves to different IPs based on user location/latency. Simple, most common. Anycast (Cloudflare): Same IP advertised globally, BGP routes to nearest. Fast failover. Global LB (GCP HTTPS LB): Single anycast VIP, proxies to nearest backend. Most feature-rich but provider-specific.

Q2
What's the difference between active-active and active-passive multi-cluster?
Show Answer
Answer

Active-Active: All clusters serve traffic. Pros: full utilization, zero failover. Cons: data consistency challenges (sync replication needed). Active-Passive: One active cluster, standby on hot standby. Pros: simpler data model. Cons: standby idle, failover takes minutes. For anihpj with PostgreSQL: active-passive is simpler.

Q3
What is K3s? When would you use it instead of full Kubernetes?
Show Answer
Answer

K3s is a lightweight, CNCF-certified K8s distribution packaged as a single <40MB binary. Uses ~512MB RAM vs 2GB+ for full K8s. Use it for: edge/IoT deployments, ARM64 devices (Raspberry Pi), CI environments, and resource-constrained scenarios. Not for: large production clusters with 100+ nodes.

Q4
How would you serve anihpj static assets (images, CSS) globally with low latency?
Show Answer
Answer

1. collectstatic uploads to S3. 2. CloudFront CDN sits in front of S3 as origin. 3. Users download assets from nearest edge location (450+ globally). 4. Cache-Control: max-age=31536000 for versioned assets ensures 1-year browser caching. Result: Tokyo user gets logo.png from Tokyo edge in 5ms, not from us-east-1 in 200ms.

Q5
Compare K3s, MicroK8s, and k0s. Which would you choose for edge?
Show Answer
Answer

K3s: Lightest (<40MB, ~512MB RAM), single binary, best for ARM64/IoT. MicroK8s: Snap-based, auto-updates, Canonical support, good for workstations. k0s: Zero dependencies, air-gapped ready. For edge: K3s is the gold standard — smallest footprint, broadest ARM64 support, and largest community.

Q6
What is a CDN? How does it reduce latency for global users?
Show Answer
Answer

A CDN caches content at edge locations worldwide. When a user requests an image, the request goes to the nearest edge node (5ms) instead of the origin server across the globe (200ms). CDNs cache static content (images, CSS, JS, API responses) and serve it from memory at the edge — reducing both latency and origin server load.

Q7
What's the caching strategy for anihpj API responses? What layers should you use?
Show Answer
Answer

Three layers: (1) CDN edge cache — CloudFront caches GET /api/jobs/ for 60s at edge locations. (2) Redis in-cluster cache — caches frequent DB queries. (3) HTTP headers — Cache-Control: public, max-age=60 + ETag for conditional requests. Each layer gets faster: CDN (5ms edge) → Redis (1ms in-region) → PostgreSQL (50ms origin).

Q8
What is the edge computing pattern? Give an example with K3s.
Show Answer
Answer

Edge computing runs compute near users instead of centralized cloud. Example: K3s cluster in a retail store runs real-time computer vision on camera feeds for inventory tracking. Processed data (not raw video) syncs to the cloud. If internet goes down, the K3s cluster operates autonomously. Cloud handles model training; edge handles inference.

Q9
How do you invalidate CDN cache when you deploy a new version of anihpj?
Show Answer
Answer

Versioned assets: logo.v2.png → new filename, no invalidation needed (best practice). Cache invalidation: aws cloudfront create-invalidation --distribution-id ABC --paths "/*" in CI/CD after collectstatic. Cache-Control headers: Set shorter TTLs during deployment window. Versioned filenames + long cache TTLs = best of both worlds.

Q10
You're deploying anihpj globally. Design the traffic routing from user → app.
Show Answer
Answer

1. Route53 latency-based DNS → routes user to nearest region. 2. CloudFront CDN → serves static assets from edge (5ms for images/CSS). 3. ALB/NLB in each region → ingress controller → anihpj pods. 4. Active-passive PostgreSQL → one writer in us-east-1, read replicas in eu-west-1. 5. Redis per region (no cross-region replication needed for cache). 6. User in Tokyo hits CloudFront Tokyo edge (5ms) + us-west-2 API (80ms) = total <100ms.

Chapter 25: Advanced Container Patterns

Beyond simple single-container pods lie powerful patterns: Sidecar, Ambassador, Adapter, and Init Containers — each solving specific platform problems. Combined with lifecycle hooks, resource management, and health probes, these patterns make your containers resilient, efficient, and self-healing. This is 15% of the exam.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~2 hours 📦 Containers 🏗️ Domain 1 (15%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ 4 multi-container pod patterns: Sidecar, Ambassador, Adapter, Init
  • ✅ Container lifecycle hooks: postStart and preStop
  • ✅ Resource management: Requests vs Limits, QoS Classes, OOM handling
  • ✅ Health probes: Liveness, Readiness, Startup — tuning for production
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🏗️ Platform Architecture & Infrastructure 15%
💡 Advanced Workloads — CNPE Exam Focus

These patterns are tested in the Architecture domain (15%) and appear in scenario-based questions:

  • Sidecar pattern — Most common. Service mesh (Istio), logging (Fluentd), TLS proxy. Same pod, shared network.
  • Init Containers — Run BEFORE main containers. Use for DB migrations, config setup, waiting for dependencies.
  • Health Probes — Liveness (restart if dead), Readiness (stop traffic if not ready), Startup (for slow-starting apps).
  • Resource Limits — ALWAYS set requests + limits. Without them, the scheduler is blind and pods can be OOMKilled.

25.1 Multi-Container Pods — 4 Essential Patterns

Multi-Container Pod Patterns

  SIDECAR                           AMBASSADOR
  ┌────────────────────────┐        ┌────────────────────────┐
  │ ┌────────┐ ┌──────────┐│        │ ┌────────┐ ┌──────────┐│
  │ │ anihpj │ │ Log      ││        │ │ anihpj │ │ Redis    ││
  │ │ -web   │ │ Forwarder││        │ │ -web   │ │ Proxy    ││
  │ │        │ │ (Fluent  ││        │ │        │ │ (Ambass.)││
  │ │        │ │  Bit)    ││        │ │        │ │          ││
  │ └────────┘ └──────────┘│        │ └────────┘ └────┬─────┘│
  │  App + helper sidecar   │        │                   │      │
  │  Share filesystem/logs  │        │  App → Proxy →    │      │
  └────────────────────────┘        │  External Redis    │      │
                                     └────────────────────────┘
  ADAPTER                           INIT CONTAINER
  ┌────────────────────────┐        ┌────────────────────────┐
  │ ┌────────┐ ┌──────────┐│        │ ① Init: Run DB         │
  │ │ anihpj │ │ Metrics  ││        │    migrations          │
  │ │        │ │ Adapter  ││        │ ② Init: Seed data      │
  │ │        │ │ (transform││        │ ③ Main: Start app     │
  │ │        │ │ Prometheus││        │                        │
  │ └────────┘ └──────────┘│        │ Init containers run    │
  │  App → Adapter → format │        │ BEFORE main containers │
  └────────────────────────┘        └────────────────────────┘
PatternPurposeExampleShared Resources
SidecarExtend/assist main containerFluent Bit ships logs from anihpjFilesystem (logs), network (localhost)
AmbassadorProxy/shield external dependenciesRedis proxy handles connection poolingNetwork (localhost:6379)
AdapterTransform/normalize outputConvert anihpj metrics to Prometheus formatNetwork (localhost:9090)
Init ContainerRun setup before main app startsRun Django migrations, seed DB, download configFilesystem (shared volume)

Init Containers — Setup Before Your App Runs

Init containers run to completion before any app container starts. They're perfect for: running Django migrations (python manage.py migrate), waiting for a database to be ready, downloading configuration files, or setting up shared volumes. If an init container fails, K8s restarts the pod (the app containers never start until all init containers succeed). Init containers run sequentially, in order — unlike sidecars which run alongside the main app.

25.2 Container Lifecycle Hooks — Graceful Start & Stop

# anihpj Deployment with lifecycle hooks
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
spec:
  template:
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: anihpj-web
          image: registry.io/anihpj:v2.1.0
          lifecycle:
            postStart:
              exec:
                # Run AFTER container starts — warm up the app
                command: ["/bin/sh", "-c", "python manage.py warmup_cache"]
            preStop:
              exec:
                # Run BEFORE container terminates — graceful shutdown
                command: ["/bin/sh", "-c", "sleep 15"]
          # Django handles SIGTERM gracefully:
          # 1. Stop accepting new requests
          # 2. Finish in-flight requests (up to 30s)
          # 3. Close DB connections
          # 4. Exit cleanly
          terminationGracePeriodSeconds: 30
📖 Graceful Shutdown — The SIGTERM Dance

K8s sends SIGTERM to the container, waits terminationGracePeriodSeconds (default 30s), then sends SIGKILL. Django must: catch SIGTERM, stop accepting requests, finish in-flight work, and exit within 30s. The preStop hook runs BEFORE SIGTERM — use it to drain connections or notify load balancers.

25.3 Resource Management Deep Dive

Requests = guaranteed minimum (scheduler uses this to place pods). Limits = hard ceiling (container can't exceed this). The ratio between them determines your QoS Class.

QoS ClassConditionOOM PriorityExample
GuaranteedRequests = Limits for CPU AND MemoryLast to be killed (-998)requests: {cpu: 500m, mem: 512Mi}, limits: {cpu: 500m, mem: 512Mi}
BurstableRequests < Limits OR not all setMiddle (0-999)requests: {mem: 256Mi}, limits: {mem: 512Mi}
BestEffortNo requests or limits at allFirst to be killed (1000)No resource spec
⚠️ OOMKilled — What Happens When Memory Limit Is Hit

When a container exceeds its memory limit, the kernel OOM killer terminates it with exit code 137. The pod restarts. If this happens repeatedly → CrashLoopBackOff. For anihpj Django: set memory limit high enough to handle peak requests + Gunicorn workers. Monitor with container_memory_working_set_bytes in Prometheus.

CPU Throttling — The Silent Performance Killer

When a container exceeds its CPU limit, K8s throttles it — pausing the container for the remainder of the scheduling period. Unlike memory OOMKilled (which is obvious — pod restarts), CPU throttling is silent: your app gets slower, latency spikes, but nothing crashes. Monitor container_cpu_cfs_throttled_seconds_total in Prometheus. If you see throttling, either raise the CPU limit or investigate why your app is CPU-hungry. For anihpj Django: CPU limits should be set high enough that throttling never occurs under normal load.

Right-Sizing with VPA (Vertical Pod Autoscaler)

The VPA analyzes historical resource usage and recommends optimal requests/limits. It has three modes: Off (recommendations only, safest), Auto (auto-updates pod specs — causes pod restarts!), and Initial (applies recommendations only on pod creation). Start with Off mode, review recommendations, then manually apply. VPA is complementary to HPA: VPA sizes your pods; HPA scales the count.

25.4 Probes — Health Checks Mastery

Liveness vs Readiness vs Startup — When Each Fires

  CONTAINER START
       │
       ▼
  STARTUP PROBE (runs FIRST)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ "Is the app done starting?" — Success → move to Liveness     │
  │ Django: check if manage.py check --deploy passes             │
  │ TIP: Use startup probe for slow-starting apps (AI models)    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
       │ (startup succeeds)
       ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────┐  ┌──────────────────────────┐
  │ LIVENESS PROBE           │  │ READINESS PROBE          │
  │ "Should I restart it?"   │  │ "Should I send traffic?" │
  │                          │  │                          │
  │ ✅ Success = container   │  │ ✅ Success = add to      │
  │    is alive, keep it     │  │    Service endpoints     │
  │ ❌ Failure = restart it  │  │ ❌ Failure = remove from │
  │                          │  │    Service (no restart!) │
  │ Example: /healthz        │  │ Example: /readyz         │
  └──────────────────────────┘  └──────────────────────────┘

Probe Types & Tuning Parameters

Probe TypeHow It ChecksBest For
HTTP GETHTTP request to a path/portWeb applications (Django, Node.js)
TCP SocketAttempts TCP connection to portNon-HTTP services (PostgreSQL, Redis)
Exec CommandRuns command inside containerCustom health checks (script-based)
gRPCgRPC health checking protocolgRPC services (K8s 1.24+)
 
# Probe configuration for anihpj Django
livenessProbe:
  httpGet:
    path: /healthz
    port: 8000
  initialDelaySeconds: 15  # Wait before first probe
  periodSeconds: 10        # Check every 10s
  timeoutSeconds: 5        # Timeout per check
  failureThreshold: 3      # 3 failures → restart

readinessProbe:
  httpGet:
    path: /readyz           # Returns 503 if DB/Redis unreachable
    port: 8000
  periodSeconds: 5
  failureThreshold: 2      # 2 failures → remove from service

startupProbe:
  httpGet:
    path: /healthz
    port: 8000
  failureThreshold: 30     # 30 × 10s = 300s startup window
  periodSeconds: 10

📝 Chapter 25 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What are the 4 multi-container pod patterns? Give an example of each for anihpj.
Show Answer
Answer

Sidecar: Fluent Bit ships anihpj logs. Ambassador: Redis proxy handles connection pooling. Adapter: Converts anihpj metrics to Prometheus format. Init Container: Runs Django migrations before the app starts. All containers in a pod share network (localhost) and can share volumes.

Q2
What is the difference between postStart and preStop hooks? When would you use each?
Show Answer
Answer

postStart: Runs immediately after container starts — for warmup, cache loading, or notifications. preStop: Runs just before container termination (before SIGTERM) — for draining connections, notifying load balancers, or saving state. Both are blocking: the container's main process doesn't start until postStart completes.

Q3
What are the 3 QoS classes in K8s? How do they affect pod eviction?
Show Answer
Answer

Guaranteed (requests=limits for CPU AND memory): Last evicted, OOM score -998. Burstable (requests < limits): Middle priority. BestEffort (no requests/limits): First evicted, OOM score 1000. During node pressure, K8s kills BestEffort pods first, then Burstable, then Guaranteed.

Q4
What's the difference between Requests and Limits? What if you set limits but no requests?
Show Answer
Answer

Requests: Guaranteed minimum — scheduler uses this to decide where to place pods (bin-packing). Limits: Hard ceiling — container can't exceed. If you set limits but no requests, K8s auto-sets requests = limits, making the pod Guaranteed QoS. If you set requests but no limits, the pod is Burstable and can use any available node resources.

Q5
What happens when a container exceeds its memory limit? What exit code?
Show Answer
Answer

The kernel OOM killer terminates the container. Exit code: 137 (128 + 9, where 9 is SIGKILL). The pod restarts. If it keeps happening, the pod enters CrashLoopBackOff. Monitor memory usage with Prometheus and set limits based on actual usage patterns (VPA recommendations).

Q6
What's the difference between a Liveness Probe and a Readiness Probe?
Show Answer
Answer

Liveness: "Is the container alive?" Failure → K8s RESTARTS the container. Use for deadlocks and unrecoverable states. Readiness: "Can the container serve traffic?" Failure → K8s removes pod from Service endpoints (NO restart). Use for temporary issues (DB connection lost).

Q7
When would you use a Startup Probe instead of initialDelaySeconds?
Show Answer
Answer

Use Startup Probe for apps with unpredictable startup times (ML model loading, large cache warmup). It runs FIRST — until it succeeds, liveness and readiness probes are disabled. This prevents slow-starting apps from being killed by liveness probes that are too aggressive. Django typically doesn't need it; AI inference servers do.

Q8
What's the graceful shutdown sequence in K8s? What role does preStop play?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Pod marked "Terminating" — removed from Service endpoints. 2. preStop hook executes (blocks). 3. SIGTERM sent to main process. 4. K8s waits terminationGracePeriodSeconds (default 30s). 5. SIGKILL sent if process still running. preStop is useful for draining in-flight requests or notifying external systems before shutdown.

Q9
How does CPU throttling work? What happens if you set CPU limit too low?
Show Answer
Answer

K8s uses CFS (Completely Fair Scheduler) quotas. If a container's CPU usage exceeds its limit, the kernel throttles it — pausing the container for the rest of the scheduling period. Result: increased latency, slower responses. Unlike memory OOM, CPU throttling doesn't kill the container — it just makes it slow. Monitor container_cpu_cfs_throttled_seconds_total.

Q10
Configure liveness, readiness, and startup probes for anihpj Django. What endpoints would you use?
Show Answer
Answer

Liveness: /healthz — returns 200 if Django is running (simple, no DB check). Readiness: /readyz — returns 200 only if DB + Redis + all critical services are reachable (returns 503 otherwise). Startup: /healthz with high failureThreshold (30) for slow migrations. Liveness = app process alive, Readiness = app can serve traffic.

Chapter 26: Serverless on Kubernetes

Serverless on K8s combines the developer experience of serverless (scale-to-zero, event-driven, no server management) with the flexibility of running on your own infrastructure. Knative is the CNCF-standard for serverless workloads on Kubernetes. Add Dapr for state management, pub/sub, and service invocation — and you have a powerful event-driven platform.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~1.5 hours ⚡ Serverless 🏗️ Domain 1 (15%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ What serverless is (and isn't) — scale-to-zero, event-driven, pay-per-use
  • ✅ Knative Serving: run serverless containers with auto-scaling
  • ✅ Knative Eventing + Dapr for event-driven architectures
  • ✅ When to use serverless on K8s — and when NOT to
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🏗️ Platform Architecture & Infrastructure 15%

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — Serverless on K8s

Expect questions about when to use serverless vs traditional deployments. Know Knative's scale-to-zero behavior, the tradeoff between cost savings and cold start latency (1-2s for containers, 10ms for Wasm), and the difference between KPA (request-based) and HPA (metric-based) autoscaling. Dapr's building blocks (state management, pub/sub, bindings) may also appear in event-driven architecture scenarios.

26.1 What is Serverless? (It's Still Servers!)

Serverless abstracts away infrastructure management: no provisioning, no patching, no capacity planning. Code runs only when triggered, and you pay only for execution time. On K8s, Knative provides this experience while keeping you in control of the underlying infrastructure.

Serverless on K8s — How Knative Works

  TRADITIONAL K8s DEPLOYMENT          KNATIVE SERVERLESS
  ┌──────────────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────────────┐
  │ Always-running pods      │        │ Scale-to-zero            │
  │                          │        │                          │
  │ ┌────┐┌────┐┌────┐      │        │ ┌────┐                   │
  │ │POD ││POD ││POD │      │        │ │POD │ ← Only when       │
  │ │ 1  ││ 2  ││ 3  │      │        │ │    │   request comes   │
  │ └────┘└────┘└────┘      │        │ └────┘                   │
  │  3 replicas × 24/7      │        │  0 replicas at idle      │
  │  Cost: $150/month        │        │  Cost: $2/month          │
  └──────────────────────────┘        └──────────────────────────┘

26.2 Knative — Serverless on K8s

Knative has two components: Knative Serving (run serverless containers) and Knative Eventing (event-driven triggers). The KPA (Knative Pod Autoscaler) scales based on concurrent requests — including scaling to zero when idle.

# Knative Service — deploy anihpj jobpost API as serverless
apiVersion: serving.knative.dev/v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: jobpost-processor
spec:
  template:
    spec:
      containers:
        - image: registry.io/jobpost-processor:v1.0
          env:
            - name: QUEUE_URL
              value: "redis://redis:6379/0"
  # Knative auto-scales: 0 replicas at idle → N replicas under load
  # Scale-to-zero: no pods after 60s of no requests
  # Cold start: ~1s for container startup on first request

26.3 OpenFunction & Dapr — Event-Driven Platform

Dapr (Distributed Application Runtime) provides building blocks for microservices: state management, pub/sub, service invocation, and bindings — all via HTTP/gRPC sidecar. OpenFunction builds on Knative + Dapr for function-as-a-service on K8s.

Event-Driven Job Posting with Dapr

  ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐
  │ anihpj   │────▶│ Dapr     │────▶│ Redis    │
  │ API      │ pub │ Pub/Sub  │ sub │ Queue    │
  │ "New job │     │ Building │     │          │
  │  posted" │     │ Block    │     │          │
  └──────────┘     └──────────┘     └────┬─────┘
                                         │
                                         ▼
                                  ┌──────────┐
                                  │ Dapr     │
                                  │ Bindings │──▶ SendGrid (email)
                                  │ Block    │──▶ Slack (notification)
                                  └──────────┘
  # Dapr handles: retries, dead-letter queues, exactly-once delivery
  # App code just calls Dapr API on localhost:3500

26.4 When to Use Serverless on K8s — Decision Matrix

WorkloadServerless?Why
Event-driven processing (image resize, email sending)✅ PerfectRuns only when event occurs, scales to zero
Infrequent internal tools (admin reports, cleanup jobs)✅ GreatNo pods running 24/7 for once-a-day jobs
Burst workloads (sale event, batch processing)✅ GoodScale-to-zero between bursts saves money
Steady high-traffic APIs (anihpj main API)❌ Not idealAlways-running pods are cheaper than constant cold starts
Latency-sensitive services (< 10ms required)❌ Not idealCold start latency (1-2s) is unacceptable
💡 CNPE Strategy

Use regular K8s Deployments for anihpj's main API (steady traffic). Use Knative for the jobpost notification processor (event-driven, low volume). This hybrid approach gives you the best of both: predictable performance for critical paths, cost savings for burst/event workloads.

📝 Chapter 26 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What is serverless? How does Knative provide serverless on Kubernetes?
Show Answer
Answer

Serverless abstracts infrastructure: no provisioning, auto-scaling (including to zero), pay-per-use. Knative Serving provides this on K8s — it auto-scales pods based on requests (KPA), scales to zero when idle, and starts pods on-demand. Developers deploy containers; Knative handles everything else.

Q2
What is scale-to-zero? What's the trade-off?
Show Answer
Answer

Scale-to-zero removes ALL pods when there are no requests for a configurable period (default 60s). Trade-off: cold start latency — the first request after idle must wait for a new pod to start (1-2s for containers, 10ms for Wasm). Great for infrequent workloads; bad for latency-sensitive services.

Q3
What is Dapr? What building blocks does it provide?
Show Answer
Answer

Dapr (Distributed Application Runtime) provides building blocks for microservices via sidecar: state management (Redis/PostgreSQL), pub/sub (Kafka/Redis), service invocation (mTLS + retries), bindings (connect to external services). Apps call Dapr API on localhost — Dapr handles the complexity.

Q4
How would you use Knative for the anihpj job posting notification system?
Show Answer
Answer

Deploy a Knative Service for the notification processor. When a new job is posted, publish event via Dapr pub/sub → triggers Knative to scale from 0→1 pod → pod processes the event (sends email via SendGrid, Slack notification) → pod scales back to 0 after 60s idle. Cost: pennies per day instead of $50/month for always-running pods.

Q5
When should you NOT use serverless on K8s? Give 3 scenarios.
Show Answer
Answer

1. Steady high-traffic APIs — always-running pods are cheaper than constant cold starts. 2. Latency-sensitive services — cold start latency (1-2s) is unacceptable for sub-10ms requirements. 3. Stateful workloads — databases, message brokers need persistent connections and stable pod identity.

Q6
What's the difference between Knative Serving and Knative Eventing?
Show Answer
Answer

Knative Serving: Run serverless HTTP containers — handles request routing, auto-scaling (KPA), scale-to-zero. Knative Eventing: Event-driven triggers — connect event sources (Kafka, GitHub webhooks) to Knative Services. Combined: an event arrives → triggers a Knative Service to scale up and process it.

Q7
How does Knative auto-scaling (KPA) differ from HPA?
Show Answer
Answer

HPA: Scales based on CPU/memory metrics — works for always-running pods. KPA: Scales based on concurrent requests — can scale to ZERO when no requests. KPA is designed for serverless (request-driven); HPA is designed for steady-state services. KPA also handles cold starts by holding requests until a pod is ready.

Q8
What is a cold start? How long does it take for Knative? How can you minimize it?
Show Answer
Answer

A cold start is the time from "request arrives" to "first response" when no pods are running. Knative: ~1-2s for containers. Minimize by: keeping at least 1 "minimum-scale" pod (defeats scale-to-zero cost savings), using smaller images, using Wasm (10ms cold start), or pre-warming with synthetic requests. Most platforms accept the 1-2s for non-critical paths.

Q9
What is OpenFunction? How does it relate to Knative?
Show Answer
Answer

OpenFunction is a function-as-a-service (FaaS) framework built on Knative + Dapr + KEDA. It lets developers write functions (not full containers) and deploy them as Knative Services. It auto-builds container images from function code. Think: AWS Lambda experience on your own K8s cluster.

Q10
Design the anihpj event-driven architecture. Which parts are serverless, which are not?
Show Answer
Answer

Serverless (Knative): jobpost notification processor (email + Slack on new job), thumbnail generator (resize images on upload), daily report generator (runs once/day). Steady Deployments (not serverless): anihpj API (steady traffic), PostgreSQL (stateful), Redis (stateful). Knative Services trigger via Dapr pub/sub from the anihpj API. Cost savings: ~$100/month by not running infrequent processors 24/7.

Chapter 27: WebAssembly (Wasm) on Kubernetes

WebAssembly is breaking out of the browser and onto the server. Wasm modules start in milliseconds (vs seconds for containers), run in a secure sandbox by default, and are 10x smaller than container images. For platform engineers, Wasm is the next frontier: lightweight, secure, portable workloads running alongside containers on K8s.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~1 hour ⚡ Wasm 🏗️ Domain 1 (15%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ What WebAssembly is and why it matters for platform engineering
  • ✅ Running Wasm on K8s: Krustlet, containerd-shim, SpinKube, RuntimeClass
  • ✅ Wasm use cases: plugins, edge functions, lightweight microservices
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🏗️ Platform Architecture & Infrastructure 15%

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — WebAssembly on K8s

The CNPE exam may include emerging technology awareness questions about Wasm. Know how RuntimeClass enables running Wasm alongside containers, the security benefits (capability-based sandbox vs Linux namespaces), and cold start advantages (10ms vs 1s). Understand that Wasm is complementary to containers — not a replacement. Key Wasm runtimes: WasmEdge, Wasmtime, and Spin.

27.1 What is WebAssembly (Wasm)?

Wasm is a portable binary format — compile once, run anywhere with near-native performance. Unlike containers (which package an entire OS userspace), Wasm modules are tiny (kilobytes to megabytes), start instantly (microseconds to milliseconds), and run in a secure sandbox by default — no filesystem access, no network access unless explicitly granted. Leading server-side Wasm runtimes include WasmEdge (CNCF, optimized for cloud-native), Wasmtime (Bytecode Alliance, reference implementation), and Spin (Fermyon, developer-focused framework).

Containers vs Wasm — Complementary, Not Competitive

  LINUX CONTAINERS                        WEBASSEMBLY MODULES
  ┌──────────────────────────┐            ┌──────────────────────────┐
  │ ┌──────────────────────┐ │            │ ┌──────────────────────┐ │
  │ │ App + Dependencies   │ │            │ │ App (compiled to    │ │
  │ │ (Python + Django +   │ │            │ │ Wasm bytecode)      │ │
  │ │  pip packages)       │ │            │ │                      │ │
  │ ├──────────────────────┤ │            │ │ • No OS filesystem   │ │
  │ │ OS Userspace         │ │            │ │ • No network (by     │ │
  │ │ (glibc, ca-certs...) │ │            │ │   default)           │ │
  │ ├──────────────────────┤ │            │ │ • Sandboxed by       │ │
  │ │ Container Runtime    │ │            │ │   runtime            │ │
  │ └──────────────────────┘ │            │ └──────────────────────┘ │
  │                          │            │                          │
  │ Size: 100MB-1GB          │            │ Size: 1-10MB             │
  │ Cold start: 1-5 seconds  │            │ Cold start: 1-10ms       │
  │ Security: namespaces     │            │ Security: capability-based│
  └──────────────────────────┘            └──────────────────────────┘

27.2 Running Wasm on Kubernetes

K8s can run Wasm modules alongside containers using RuntimeClass — a mechanism that lets you choose between different container runtimes per pod.

ApproachHow It WorksMaturity
KrustletK8s kubelet that runs Wasm instead of containers🟡 Experimental
containerd-wasm-shimContainerd plugin — run Wasm via containerd🟢 Production-ready
SpinKubeSpin operator for K8s — native K8s Wasm resources🟡 Growing rapidly
RuntimeClassK8s native: runtimeClassName: wasm selects Wasm runtime🟢 K8s 1.24+
 
# Run a Wasm workload via RuntimeClass
apiVersion: node.k8s.io/v1
kind: RuntimeClass
metadata:
  name: wasm
handler: wasm  # Maps to containerd-wasm-shim
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: wasm-hello
spec:
  runtimeClassName: wasm
  containers:
    - name: hello
      image: registry.io/wasm-hello:latest

27.3 Wasm Use Cases for Platform Engineers

Use CaseWhy Wasm WinsExample
Plugin SystemsSecure sandbox — user code can't escapeanihpj customers upload custom job-matching algorithms as Wasm plugins
Edge Functions10ms cold start vs 1s for containersRequest validation at CDN edge
Lightweight Microservices10MB image vs 200MB containerSimple data transform service
Multi-LanguageRust, Go, C, Python, JS → WasmTeams use preferred language; uniform platform runtime
📖 Wasm is NOT a Container Replacement

Wasm complements containers. Use containers for full applications (Django, databases). Use Wasm for lightweight, security-sensitive, or latency-critical functions (plugins, edge logic). Both run on the same K8s cluster via RuntimeClass — best of both worlds.

📝 Chapter 27 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What is WebAssembly? How does it differ from containers?
Show Answer
Answer

Wasm is a portable binary format running in a secure sandbox. Unlike containers: tiny (1-10MB vs 100MB+), millisecond cold starts (vs seconds), sandboxed by default with no filesystem/network access. Wasm is complementary — not a container replacement.

Q2
How can K8s run Wasm workloads alongside containers?
Show Answer
Answer

Via RuntimeClass: create a RuntimeClass with handler: wasm mapped to containerd-wasm-shim. Pods set runtimeClassName: wasm to use it. Other options: Krustlet (Wasm kubelet), SpinKube (K8s operator), and containerd-wasm-shim (most production-ready). Containers continue using default runc.

Q3
What are 3 Wasm use cases for platform engineers?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Plugin systems: Sandbox untrusted user code. 2. Edge functions: 10ms cold start for latency-critical CDN logic. 3. Lightweight microservices: 10MB images, instant scaling for simple transforms. Wasm excels where containers are too heavy or not secure enough by default.

Q4
Why is Wasm more secure than containers by default?
Show Answer
Answer

Wasm uses capability-based security: no filesystem, no network, no syscalls unless explicitly granted. Containers use Linux namespaces — which have a history of escape vulnerabilities. A compromised Wasm module can't access the host; a compromised container might via a kernel exploit.

Q5
Can Wasm replace containers? Why or why not?
Show Answer
Answer

No. Wasm can't run apps needing persistent filesystems, TCP networking, or system calls (databases, web servers). Wasm excels at lightweight, stateless, security-sensitive functions. Containers handle full applications. They're complementary — K8s runs both via RuntimeClass.

Q6
What is SpinKube? How does it simplify Wasm on K8s?
Show Answer
Answer

SpinKube is a K8s operator for Spin (Fermyon's Wasm framework). Instead of manual RuntimeClass + containerd config, you deploy SpinApp CRDs. SpinKube handles the Wasm runtime, scaling, and lifecycle. kubectl get spinapps shows Wasm workloads alongside regular pods — native K8s experience.

Q7
How would anihpj use Wasm for a plugin system?
Show Answer
Answer

Customers upload custom job-matching algorithms compiled to Wasm. Each plugin runs sandboxed via RuntimeClass — no filesystem/network access. Malicious plugins can't escape or access the main API. The platform provides a Wasm SDK; customers write matching logic in Rust/Go/Python. Platform maintains security without reviewing customer code.

Q8
What is a cold start? How do Wasm and containers compare?
Show Answer
Answer

Cold start = time from request to first response when no instances are running. Wasm: 1-10ms (tiny binary, no OS init). Containers: 1-5 seconds (image pull + OS userspace init + app startup). For edge functions and plugin systems where latency matters, Wasm cold start is 100x faster.

Q9
What languages compile to Wasm for server-side use?
Show Answer
Answer

Rust: First-class, best performance. Go: Via TinyGo compiler. C/C++: Via Emscripten. Python/JavaScript: Less mature but possible. Developers write code normally, compile to .wasm, and the platform handles runtime selection. The Wasm binary is portable — same file runs on any Wasm runtime.

Q10
Your K8s cluster runs containers AND Wasm workloads. How do you monitor both?
Show Answer
Answer

Both are visible via kubectl get pods — Wasm pods are just pods with runtimeClassName: wasm. Prometheus can scrape Wasm metrics if the runtime exposes them. Grafana dashboards show both container and Wasm pods. For logs: Wasm runtimes typically output to stdout — Fluent Bit/Promtail collect them like any pod. One observability stack for both runtimes.

Chapter 28: Cloud Native Cost Management

Cloud costs can spiral out of control without FinOps — the practice of making every team accountable for their cloud spend. As a CNPE, you implement the tools and processes that provide cost visibility, optimization, and accountability. This is 15% of the exam — the platform must be cost-efficient.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~2 hours 💰 FinOps 🏗️ Domain 1 (15%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ FinOps fundamentals: Inform → Optimize → Operate lifecycle
  • ✅ K8s cost visibility: kubectl top, Kubecost, Prometheus dashboards
  • ✅ Cost optimization: right-sizing, spot instances, bin packing
  • ✅ Chargeback & showback: per-team cost allocation
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🏗️ Platform Architecture & Infrastructure 15%

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — Cost Optimization

Expect scenario questions: "Your cloud bill is $50K/month. What's your optimization plan?" Know the FinOps lifecycle (Inform → Optimize → Operate), the cost savings hierarchy (spot instances 60-90%, right-sizing 30-50%, auto-scaling 20-40%), and the difference between chargeback (bill teams) vs showback (show costs). Also know Kubecost and OpenCost as the primary K8s cost tools. Label-based cost allocation (team, project, environment) is essential for both showback and chargeback.

28.1 FinOps Fundamentals

FinOps is a cultural practice — not just a tool. It brings finance, engineering, and operations together to manage cloud costs collaboratively. The FinOps lifecycle has three phases:

📊INFORM
→
⚡OPTIMIZE
→
🔄OPERATE
PhaseGoalActivity
InformSee costs clearlyTag all resources, allocate costs to teams, build dashboards
OptimizeReduce wasteRight-size resources, use spot instances, eliminate idle resources
OperateSustain efficiencyContinuous monitoring, automated policies, anomaly detection

28.3 Cost Optimization Strategies

Kubernetes Cost Savings Pyramid

Spot/Preemptible Instances60-90%Use spot for non-critical workloads (dev, batch, workers)
