Pod Bin Packing15-25%Schedule pods efficiently on fewer nodes
Node Affinity/Taints10-20%Use taints to reserve expensive GPU nodes only for ML workloads; use affinity to place batch jobs on spot instances. Ensures every workload runs on the most cost-efficient hardware.
📖 Shared Cost Responsibility

FinOps is not just the platform team's job — every team shares responsibility for their cloud costs. Platform engineers provide cost visibility (Kubecost dashboards, per-namespace breakdowns) and optimization tools (right-sizing recommendations, spot instance availability). Developers own their costs: they decide resource requests, choose instance types, and clean up unused resources. This shared accountability prevents the "someone else will pay for it" mentality.

28.4 Chargeback and Showback

ModelWhat It DoesImpact
ShowbackShows costs per team WITHOUT billingAwareness — "Wow, our dev cluster costs $5k/month"
ChargebackActually bills teams for their usageAccountability — team budgets reflect real costs

Both require namespace-level cost allocation via labels (team, project, environment). Kubecost automatically breaks down costs by K8s namespace, deployment, and label — enabling precise per-team visibility without manual accounting.

📝 Chapter 28 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What is FinOps? What are its three phases?
Show Answer
Answer

FinOps is a cultural practice bringing finance, engineering, and ops together to manage cloud costs. Three phases: Inform (see costs, allocate to teams), Optimize (reduce waste, right-size), Operate (continuous monitoring, automated policies). It's a cycle, not a one-time project.

Q2
What tools provide K8s cost visibility? Compare kubectl top, Prometheus, and Kubecost.
Show Answer
Answer

kubectl top: Instant CPU/Memory snapshot (needs Metrics Server). Prometheus+Grafana: Historical metrics, custom dashboards. Kubecost: Dedicated K8s cost tool — shows cost per namespace/deployment/label, right-sizing recommendations, and integrates with cloud billing APIs for actual dollar amounts.

Q3
What's the single biggest cost-saving strategy for Kubernetes?
Show Answer
Answer

Spot/Preemptible instances — save 60-90% compared to on-demand. Use them for stateless, fault-tolerant workloads (dev environments, batch workers, CI runners). NEVER for stateful databases or critical production services. Combine with Cluster Autoscaler to automatically replace terminated spot instances.

Q4
What's the difference between chargeback and showback?
Show Answer
Answer

Showback: Shows costs per team without billing — creates awareness. Chargeback: Actually bills teams for their usage — creates accountability. Start with showback to build trust and data accuracy; graduate to chargeback once teams understand their costs. Both require label-based cost allocation.

Q5
How does right-sizing reduce cloud costs? What tools help?
Show Answer
Answer

Right-sizing sets resource requests/limits based on actual usage, not over-provisioned guesses. Most teams request 2-5× more than they use. Tools: VPA recommendations (analyzes historical usage), Kubecost (shows cost of over-provisioned resources), and Prometheus dashboards showing actual vs requested resources.

Q6
What is pod bin packing? How does it save money?
Show Answer
Answer

Bin packing schedules pods densely on fewer nodes instead of spreading them out. If you have 10 pods each using 200m CPU, you can fit them on 2 nodes (5 pods each) instead of 10 nodes. Fewer nodes = lower cost. K8s scheduler does this automatically but you can tune with pod affinity/anti-affinity and resource requests.

Q7
How should you tag K8s resources for cost allocation?
Show Answer
Answer

Label all resources with: team (owning team), project (project name), environment (dev/staging/prod), cost-center (finance tracking code). Enforce these labels via Kyverno policies (require-labels). Kubecost then automatically aggregates costs by these labels, enabling per-team dashboards and chargeback.

Q8
What is unit economics in cloud cost management?
Show Answer
Answer

Unit economics measures cost per business metric: cost per deployment, cost per user, cost per API request. Instead of "our cloud bill is $50K," you say "we spend $0.003 per job posting" — this connects engineering decisions to business value. If cost per deployment goes from $2 to $0.50, you've improved efficiency 4×.

Q9
What is OpenCost? How does it differ from Kubecost?
Show Answer
Answer

OpenCost is the open-source, CNCF sandbox cost monitoring specification. Kubecost is a commercial product built on OpenCost with additional features (right-sizing recs, cloud billing integration, team dashboards). Both provide namespace-level cost allocation. OpenCost is free; Kubecost has free and paid tiers.

Q10
Your anihpj platform cloud bill is $15K/month. What's your cost optimization plan?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Inform: Deploy Kubecost to see per-namespace/per-team costs. Tag all resources. 2. Optimize: Move dev/staging to spot instances (save 60%), right-size production pods (VPA recommendations), enable Cluster Autoscaler, delete unused resources. 3. Operate: Set budget alerts in Kubecost, require cost-center labels via Kyverno, monthly FinOps review with teams. Target: $15K → $6-8K/month.

Chapter 29: Auto-Scaling Deep Dive

Auto-scaling is the secret to matching supply to demand — scaling up when traffic spikes, scaling down when it drops. K8s offers four scalers: HPA (horizontal, more pods), VPA (vertical, bigger pods), CA (cluster, more nodes), and KEDA (event-driven, scale to zero). Mastering them is essential for cost-efficient, resilient platforms.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~2 hours 📈 Scaling 🏗️ Domain 1 (15%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ HPA: metrics-based horizontal pod scaling
  • ✅ VPA: auto-adjust resource requests/limits
  • ✅ Cluster Autoscaler & Karpenter: node-level scaling
  • ✅ KEDA: event-driven scaling with 50+ scalers
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🏗️ Platform Architecture & Infrastructure 15%

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — Auto-Scaling

The CNPE exam tests your ability to configure and troubleshoot auto-scaling. Expect tasks like: "Configure HPA to scale anihpj-web based on CPU at 70%" or "Set up KEDA to scale workers based on Redis queue length." Know the HPA formula, the three VPA modes (Off/Auto/Initial), the difference between Cluster Autoscaler and Karpenter, and how KEDA achieves scale-to-zero (impossible with standard HPA). Stabilization windows and scaling policies are frequently tested.

29.1 Horizontal Pod Autoscaler (HPA)

HPA scales the number of pod replicas based on CPU, memory, or custom metrics. Formula: desiredReplicas = ceil(currentReplicas × currentMetricValue / desiredMetricValue).

# HPA: Scale anihpj-web from 3→10 pods when CPU > 70%
apiVersion: autoscaling/v2
kind: HorizontalPodAutoscaler
metadata:
  name: anihpj-web-hpa
spec:
  scaleTargetRef:
    apiVersion: apps/v1
    kind: Deployment
    name: anihpj-web
  minReplicas: 3
  maxReplicas: 10
  metrics:
    - type: Resource
      resource:
        name: cpu
        target:
          type: Utilization
          averageUtilization: 70
  behavior:
    scaleDown:
      stabilizationWindowSeconds: 300  # Wait 5 min before scaling down
    scaleUp:
      stabilizationWindowSeconds: 60   # React quickly to spikes

29.2 Vertical Pod Autoscaler (VPA)

VPA adjusts pod resource requests/limits based on historical usage. Three modes: Off (recommendations only), Auto (updates pod specs — causes restarts!), Initial (applies on pod creation). VPA + HPA can work together if VPA uses resource metrics and HPA uses custom metrics — but both using CPU/memory creates conflicts.

29.3 Cluster Autoscaler & Karpenter

Cluster Autoscaler adds nodes when pods are unschedulable and removes underutilized nodes. Karpenter (AWS-native) is the next-gen alternative — faster, more flexible, and selects the optimal instance type automatically. Both integrate with cloud auto-scaling groups (AWS ASG, GCP MIG).

29.4 KEDA — Event-Driven Autoscaling

KEDA scales pods based on external events — queue length, Kafka lag, Prometheus metrics, cron schedules. It supports 50+ scalers and can scale to zero when no events exist.

# KEDA: Scale anihpj workers based on Redis queue length
apiVersion: keda.sh/v1alpha1
kind: ScaledObject
metadata:
  name: anihpj-worker-scaler
spec:
  scaleTargetRef:
    name: anihpj-worker
  minReplicaCount: 0    # Scale to ZERO when no jobs in queue
  maxReplicaCount: 20
  triggers:
    - type: redis
      metadata:
        address: redis:6379
        listName: jobpost-queue
        listLength: "5"  # 1 pod per 5 pending jobs

Auto-Scaling — The Four Musketeers Compared

  SCALER    WHAT IT SCALES        TRIGGER              SPEED
  ───────── ────────────────────  ───────────────────  ───────
  HPA       PODS (replicas)      CPU/Memory/Custom    Fast (~30s)
            3→5→10 pods
  VPA       POD SIZE             Resource history     Slow (~min)
            (requests/limits)    
            256Mi→512Mi→1Gi
  CA        NODES                Pending pods         Medium (~1-2m)
            3→5→8 nodes          (unschedulable)
  KEDA      PODS (event-driven)  Queue length,        Fast (~sec)
            0→5→0 pods           Kafka lag, etc.

  🎯 anihpj: HPA for web, KEDA for workers, CA + Spot for cost savings

📝 Chapter 29 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
How does HPA calculate the desired number of replicas?
Show Answer
Answer

desiredReplicas = ceil(currentReplicas × currentMetricValue / desiredMetricValue). If you have 3 pods at 80% CPU and target is 70%: 3 × 80/70 = 3.43 → 4 pods. HPA checks every 15 seconds. Stabilization windows prevent rapid flapping.

Q2
Can HPA and VPA work together? What's the risk?
Show Answer
Answer

Yes, but carefully. If both use CPU/memory, they conflict — VPA changes pod size while HPA changes pod count, creating oscillation. Safe pattern: VPA uses resource metrics, HPA uses custom metrics (requests/second). Or use VPA in Off mode (recommendations only) and manually apply suggestions.

Q3
What's the difference between Cluster Autoscaler and Karpenter?
Show Answer
Answer

CA: Adds/removes nodes from ASG/MIG. Simple, mature. Karpenter: AWS-native, selects optimal instance type automatically, provisions nodes in seconds (vs minutes), and can mix spot/on-demand per workload. Karpenter is the future for AWS; CA is the cross-cloud standard.

Q4
How does KEDA differ from HPA? When use each?
Show Answer
Answer

HPA: Scales based on resource metrics (CPU/Memory). Good for steady-state services. KEDA: Scales based on external events (queue depth, Kafka lag). Good for event-driven workloads that should scale to zero. KEDA extends HPA — it creates HPA objects under the hood with richer triggers.

Q5
What is a stabilization window in HPA? Why is it important?
Show Answer
Answer

Stabilization windows prevent rapid flapping. scaleDown window (default 300s): HPA waits 5 minutes before reducing replicas — prevents thrashing from brief dips. scaleUp window (default 0s): HPA reacts immediately to spikes. Tune these per workload: shorter for stateless APIs, longer for stateful services.

Q6
What happens when KEDA scales anihpj workers to zero? How do new jobs trigger scaling?
Show Answer
Answer

When no jobs are in the Redis queue, KEDA scales worker pods to 0. New job → added to Redis queue → KEDA detects queue length > 0 → scales workers to 1 → worker processes job → queue empties → KEDA scales back to 0 after cooldown. Cost: zero compute when idle; you pay only for Redis queue storage.

Q7
What are the three VPA modes? Which is safest for production?
Show Answer
Answer

Off: Recommendations only (safest — no changes). Initial: Applies on pod creation (moderate risk). Auto: Updates running pods — causes restarts (highest risk). For production: use Off mode, review recommendations, manually apply during maintenance windows. Auto mode is safe for stateless dev workloads.

Q8
How does Cluster Autoscaler decide which node to remove?
Show Answer
Answer

CA checks: is the node underutilized (< 50% CPU/Memory request)? Can its pods be rescheduled elsewhere? Does the node have annotations preventing scale-down (scale-down-disabled)? Are there PDBs protecting pods? If yes to all, CA drains and removes the node. Pods are rescheduled onto remaining nodes.

Q9
What scalers does KEDA support? Give 5 examples.
Show Answer
Answer

KEDA supports 50+ scalers: Redis (list length), Kafka (consumer lag), Prometheus (any metric query), RabbitMQ (queue depth), Cron (time-based scaling), AWS SQS, Azure Service Bus, GCP Pub/Sub, and many more. Event-driven scaling from virtually any event source.

Q10
Design the auto-scaling strategy for anihpj: web API, job workers, and batch processing.
Show Answer
Answer

Web API: HPA on CPU (target 70%, min 3, max 20). Job Workers: KEDA on Redis queue length (scale 0→10 based on pending jobs). Batch Processing: KEDA Cron scaler (scale to 5 at 2AM for nightly reports, scale to 0 after). Nodes: Karpenter with mixed spot/on-demand, Cluster Autoscaler as backup. Result: elastic scaling matching demand with minimal cost.

Chapter 30: Capacity Planning and Performance

A platform that can't handle next month's traffic is a failed platform. Capacity planning ensures you have enough resources before you need them. Performance testing finds the breaking point — before your users do. Together, they answer: "Will anihpj survive Black Friday?"

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 📊 Performance 🏗️ Domain 1 (15%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Capacity planning: forecasting, headroom, growth projections
  • ✅ Performance testing: load testing, benchmarking, soak testing
  • ✅ Performance optimization: Django tuning, connection pooling, caching
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🏗️ Platform Architecture & Infrastructure 15%

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — Capacity & Performance

Expect questions about capacity planning ("How many pods do you need for 1000 req/s?") and performance optimization ("anihpj is slow — what do you fix first?"). Know the performance testing types (load, stress, soak, spike), the optimization hierarchy (connection pooling → caching → async → scaling), and k6 as the preferred load testing tool for K8s environments. The exam tests your ability to identify bottlenecks systematically — not guess at solutions.

30.1 Capacity Planning Fundamentals

Capacity planning answers: How many pods? How big? How many nodes? The formula: capacity = (expected RPS × average response time) + headroom. For anihpj: if you expect 1,000 requests/sec and each pod handles 100 req/s, you need 10 pods + 30% headroom = 13 pods.

ElementQuestionanihpj Example
ForecastExpected traffic next month?2× current users after campaign
HeadroomBuffer for spikes?30% extra (handle 1.3× peak)
GrowthMonthly growth rate?15% month-over-month
BudgetCost constraint?$8K/month for production

30.2 Performance Testing

Test TypeWhat It MeasuresTool
Load TestingHow many req/s can anihpj handle?k6, Locust, vegeta
Stress TestingWhere does it break? (find limits)k6 ramping to failure
Soak TestingMemory leaks over time?Run load test for 24+ hours
Spike TestingSudden traffic surge handling?k6 with sudden 10× ramp
 
# k6 load test: benchmark anihpj job listing API
import http from 'k6/http';
import { check, sleep } from 'k6';

export const options = {
  stages: [
    { duration: '2m', target: 50 },
    { duration: '5m', target: 200 },
    { duration: '2m', target: 0 },
  ],
  thresholds: {
    http_req_duration: ['p(95)<500'],
    http_req_failed: ['rate<0.01'],
  },
};

export default function () {
  const res = http.get('https://anihpj.example.com/api/jobs/');
  check(res, { 'status is 200': (r) => r.status === 200 });
  sleep(1);
}

30.3 Performance Optimization

TechniqueWhat It DoesImpact
Connection Poolingpgbouncer pools DB connections2-5× more req/s
Redis CachingCache frequent DB queries5-20× faster reads
CDN Static AssetsServe CSS/JS/images from edge10-50× faster static loads
Async ProcessingMove email/notifications to Celery3-10× faster API responses
Read ReplicasRead-heavy endpoints → replica2-3× more read capacity
Gunicorn TuningWorkers = (2 × CPU) + 1Optimal CPU utilization
💡 CNPE Performance Strategy

Cache at every layer: CDN (edge, 5ms) → Redis (in-cluster, 1ms) → Read Replica (DB, 50ms) → Primary DB. For anihpj job listings: cache popular searches in Redis (5 min TTL), serve static assets from CloudFront, use pgbouncer for connection pooling, offload email to Celery workers. Result: 50ms p99 latency for 95% of requests.

📝 Chapter 30 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
How do you calculate required pod count for expected traffic?
Show Answer
Answer

pods = ceil(expected_RPS / pod_capacity) + headroom%. If anihpj expects 1000 req/s, each pod handles 100 req/s, 30% headroom: ceil(1000/100) × 1.3 = 13 pods. Validate with load testing — real-world bottlenecks (DB, network) change actual capacity.

Q2
What's the difference between load, stress, and soak testing?
Show Answer
Answer

Load: Test expected traffic. Stress: Ramp until failure — find limits. Soak: Run for hours/days — find memory leaks. All three needed: load validates capacity, stress finds breaking points, soak catches slow failures.

Q3
What is connection pooling? Why critical for Django on K8s?
Show Answer
Answer

pgbouncer reuses DB connections instead of opening per request. Without it: 100 pods × 20 workers = 2000 idle connections → PostgreSQL chokes. With pgbouncer: 100 pods → pgbouncer (20 pooled) → PostgreSQL. 100× reduction in DB connections.

Q4
What caching layers should anihpj use? In what order?
Show Answer
Answer

Layer 1: CDN (5ms) — static assets + cacheable API. Layer 2: Redis (1ms) — frequent queries. Layer 3: Read Replica (50ms) — read-heavy endpoints. Cache at the edge first (biggest win), then progressively closer to the app.

Q5
What is headroom in capacity planning?
Show Answer
Answer

Headroom is extra capacity beyond expected peak — your safety buffer. Typical: 30% for production, 50% for critical services, 0% for dev. It's insurance against traffic spikes and node failures — not waste.

Q6
What tools would you use to load test anihpj?
Show Answer
Answer

k6 (JavaScript, developer-friendly), Locust (Python, matches Django), vegeta (Go, simple CLI), JMeter (GUI, legacy). k6 recommended: ramp to 200 users, check p95 < 500ms, error rate < 1%. Run from CI to catch regressions.

Q7
How does async processing (Celery) improve API performance?
Show Answer
Answer

Without async: user posts job → API sends email, updates index → responds 3s later. With Celery: API queues tasks → responds 50ms "Job posted! ✅" → workers process async. API response drops from 3s to 50ms.

Q8
What is the optimal Gunicorn worker count?
Show Answer
Answer

Formula: (2 × CPU cores) + 1. Pod with 2 CPUs: 5 workers. Too few = underutilized CPU. Too many = context switching + memory bloat. Test with k6 to find the sweet spot for your workload.

Q9
How do read replicas improve database performance?
Show Answer
Answer

Read replicas offload SELECT queries from the primary. Route reads (GET /api/jobs/ — 90% of traffic) to replicas; writes to primary. Django DATABASE_ROUTERS auto-route. This doubles or triples read capacity without scaling the primary DB.

Q10
anihpj is slow under load. Walk through your optimization process.
Show Answer
Answer

1. Profile: k6 reproduces load. Prometheus shows bottleneck. 2. DB: Add pgbouncer + missing indexes. 3. Cache: Redis for frequent queries, CDN for static assets. 4. Async: Celery for email/indexing. 5. Scale: HPA on CPU > 70%. 6. Re-test: k6 confirms p99 down from 2s to 200ms. Fix the bottleneck, measure again. Never guess.

Chapter 31: Event-Driven Architectures

Not everything needs to happen right now. Event-driven architecture decouples services through asynchronous messaging — the anihpj API responds instantly while Kafka fans out events to email, search, and analytics services. This pattern is essential for building scalable, resilient, and loosely-coupled platforms.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~2 hours 🔄 Events 🔄 Cross-Domain

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Sync vs async, event sourcing, CQRS
  • ✅ Kafka on K8s with Strimzi operator
  • ✅ CloudEvents standard and Knative Eventing
  • ✅ Workflow engines: Temporal, Argo Workflows, Dapr
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🔄 Cross-Domain Integrated

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — Event-Driven Architectures

Expect scenario questions: "How would you decouple the anihpj API from email and search services?" Know the sync vs async tradeoff (user waits vs instant response), the difference between event sourcing and CQRS, and which message broker to choose (Kafka for high throughput, Redis Streams for simplicity). CloudEvents as a CNCF standard and Dead Letter Queues for handling failed events are frequently tested.

31.1 Event-Driven Fundamentals

Synchronous vs Asynchronous — Why It Matters

  ❌ SYNCHRONOUS (Request-Response)
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  User → anihpj API → process job → send email → respond    │
  │                            ↑                              │
  │                     User WAITS 3 seconds 😤               │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ✅ ASYNCHRONOUS (Event-Driven)
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  User → anihpj API → 📬 KAFKA TOPIC "jobpost-events"      │
  │                         │                                   │
  │              ┌──────────┼──────────┐                       │
  │              ▼          ▼          ▼                       │
  │        ┌─────────┐┌─────────┐┌─────────┐                  │
  │        │📧 Email ││📊Analytics││🔍Search │                  │
  │        │ Service ││ Service ││ Indexer │                  │
  │        └─────────┘└─────────┘└─────────┘                  │
  │                                                             │
  │  User gets IMMEDIATE response: "Job posted! ✅"           │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Message Broker Comparison

BrokerBest ForK8s OperatorComplexity
KafkaHigh throughput, event sourcingStrimzi🔴 High
RabbitMQTraditional messaging, routingRabbitMQ Operator🟡 Medium
NATSLightweight, low latencyNATS Operator🟢 Low
Redis StreamsSimple pub/sub + cachingRedis Operator🟢 Low
Dapr Pub/SubSidecar abstraction layerN/A (sidecar)🟡 Medium

Event Sourcing & CQRS — Advanced Patterns

Event Sourcing stores the sequence of events (JobCreated → JobUpdated → JobClosed) rather than just current state. Benefits: complete audit trail, temporal queries, event replay for debugging. CQRS (Command Query Responsibility Segregation) separates write models (commands) from read models (queries) — each optimized independently. Together they enable powerful patterns: writes go to the event store; reads come from optimized materialized views in Redis or Elasticsearch.

31.2 Kafka on Kubernetes with Strimzi

Strimzi operator makes running Kafka on K8s as simple as deploying any other workload. It manages Kafka brokers, ZooKeeper (or KRaft), topics, users, Kafka Connect (integration with external systems like databases and S3), and Schema Registry (Avro/Protobuf schema validation for events) — all via K8s CRDs. This gives you a production-grade event streaming platform managed entirely through kubectl.

# Strimzi: Deploy a Kafka cluster for anihpj events
apiVersion: kafka.strimzi.io/v1beta2
kind: Kafka
metadata:
  name: anihpj-kafka
spec:
  kafka:
    version: 3.7.0
    replicas: 3
    listeners:
      - name: plain
        port: 9092
        type: internal
        tls: false
      - name: tls
        port: 9093
        type: internal
        tls: true
    storage:
      type: persistent-claim
      size: 100Gi
  entityOperator:
    topicOperator: {}
    userOperator: {}

31.3 CloudEvents & Event Mesh

CloudEvents is a CNCF standard for describing event data in a common format — making events portable across different brokers and platforms. Knative Eventing builds an event mesh on K8s, routing CloudEvents between sources and sinks. Dead Letter Queues (DLQs) catch events that fail processing — preventing data loss.

31.4 Workflow Engines

EngineStyleBest For
TemporalCode-based workflows (Go/Java/Python)Durable, long-running business workflows
Argo WorkflowsDAG-based, YAML-definedData pipelines, CI/CD, batch processing
TektonK8s-native CI/CD pipelinesBuilding and deploying from Git events
Dapr WorkflowsLightweight, sidecar-basedSimple orchestrations alongside microservices

📝 Chapter 31 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What is event-driven architecture? How does it differ from synchronous request-response?
Show Answer
Answer

Event-driven: services communicate via asynchronous events through a message broker. Producer emits events; consumers react independently. Synchronous: caller waits for response. Event-driven provides loose coupling, independent scaling, and fault isolation — if the email service is down, job posting still succeeds.

Q2
What is event sourcing? How does it differ from storing current state?
Show Answer
Answer

Event sourcing stores the sequence of events (JobCreated, JobUpdated, JobClosed) rather than current state (Job{title:"Engineer", status:"open"}). Current state is derived by replaying events. Benefits: complete audit trail, temporal queries ("what was this job's status last Tuesday?"), easy event replay for debugging.

Q3
What is CQRS? Why use it with event sourcing?
Show Answer
Answer

CQRS (Command Query Responsibility Segregation): separate write models (commands: createJob, updateJob) from read models (queries: listJobs, searchJobs). Each can be optimized independently — writes go through event store; reads come from materialized views (Redis, Elasticsearch). Perfect pairing with event sourcing.

Q4
How does Strimzi enable running Kafka on Kubernetes?
Show Answer
Answer

Strimzi is a K8s operator that manages Kafka clusters via CRDs: Kafka (broker config), KafkaTopic (topics), KafkaUser (auth), KafkaConnect (integrations). It handles rolling updates, scaling, and storage. Deploy Kafka with kubectl apply -f kafka.yaml — Strimzi handles the rest.

Q5
What is CloudEvents? Why standardize event formats?
Show Answer
Answer

CloudEvents is a CNCF standard for describing event data. It defines common attributes (id, source, type, time, subject, data) so events are portable across brokers (Kafka, NATS, HTTP) and platforms. Standardization means you can switch from Kafka to NATS without changing event consumers.

Q6
What is a Dead Letter Queue? Why is it essential for event-driven systems?
Show Answer
Answer

A DLQ stores events that failed processing after all retries. Without DLQs: failed events are lost (at-most-once) or block the queue (poison messages). With DLQs: failed events are stored for inspection and replay. This is critical for data integrity — no event is ever silently lost.

Q7
Compare Temporal and Argo Workflows. When would you choose each?
Show Answer
Answer

Temporal: Code-based workflows (Go/Python), supports very long-running workflows (days/months), built-in retry/compensation. Best for business processes (order fulfillment). Argo Workflows: YAML DAGs, K8s-native, great for data pipelines and CI/CD. Choose Temporal for business logic; Argo for infrastructure pipelines.

Q8
How would you design the anihpj job posting flow as event-driven?
Show Answer
Answer

User posts job → anihpj API validates and saves to DB → emits "JobCreated" event to Kafka. Consumers: Email Service sends confirmation, Search Indexer updates Elasticsearch, Analytics Service updates dashboards, Slack Notifier posts to #jobs channel. Each consumer scales independently. API responds in 50ms.

Q9
What are event delivery guarantees? Compare at-least-once and exactly-once.
Show Answer
Answer

At-least-once: Event is delivered ≥1 times (may duplicate). Simpler, higher throughput. Exactly-once: Event is delivered exactly once (no duplicates, no loss). Requires idempotent producers and transactional consumers. Kafka supports both. For anihpj: use at-least-once with idempotent consumers (email "already sent" check).

Q10
What message broker would you choose for anihpj? Justify your choice.
Show Answer
Answer

Start with Redis Streams (already used for caching — no new infrastructure). If event volume grows: migrate to Kafka via Strimzi for higher throughput, event replay, and long-term event storage. Kafka is the industry standard for event-driven platforms; Redis Streams is the pragmatic starting point for smaller workloads.

Chapter 32: API Management and Gateways

Every request to your platform flows through an API Gateway — the front door for authentication, rate limiting, routing, and observability. As a CNPE, you choose and configure the gateway that protects and manages all platform traffic. The Gateway API is the next-generation Ingress standard.

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — API Gateways

Expect questions about API Gateway selection and configuration: "Which gateway would you use for anihpj and why?" Know the Gateway API resource model (GatewayClass → Gateway → HTTPRoute), the difference between north-south and east-west traffic, and rate limiting strategies (token bucket vs sliding window). Also know where JWT validation should happen (at the gateway, not the app) and how WAF integration protects against common web attacks.

32.1 API Gateway Patterns

PatternDirectionResponsibilities
North-SouthExternal → Cluster (ingress)Routing, TLS, auth, rate limiting, WAF
East-WestService → Service (mesh)mTLS, load balancing, circuit breaking

32.2 Envoy Gateway & Kubernetes Gateway API

The Gateway API is the successor to Ingress — more expressive, role-oriented, and protocol-aware. Resources: GatewayClass (infra provider), Gateway (listener config), HTTPRoute (routing rules). Beyond basic routing, Gateway API supports: traffic splitting (90% v1, 10% v2 for canary), header-based routing (beta users → v2), request mirroring (duplicate traffic to test environment), and cross-namespace routing. Envoy Gateway is the CNCF reference implementation.

# Gateway API: Route traffic to anihpj with path-based routing
apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: HTTPRoute
metadata:
  name: anihpj-routes
spec:
  parentRefs:
    - name: anihpj-gateway
  rules:
    - matches:
        - path: { type: PathPrefix, value: /api/v2 }
      backendRefs:
        - name: anihpj-v2
          port: 8000
    - matches:
        - path: { type: PathPrefix, value: /api/ }
      backendRefs:
        - name: anihpj-v1
          port: 8000

32.3 API Management Platforms

GatewayStrengthsBest For
Kong200+ plugins, full API managementAPI management (auth, rate limit, analytics)
Envoy GatewayCNCF standard, Gateway API nativeGateway API-first platforms
TraefikAuto-discovery, Let's Encrypt, simpleSimple K8s-native ingress
NGINX IngressMature, massive communityExisting NGINX expertise
APISIXApache, high performance, pluginsHigh-throughput gateways

32.4 Rate Limiting and API Protection

StrategyHow It WorksBest For
Token BucketTokens refill at fixed rate; 1 token per requestSmooth traffic, allows bursts
Sliding WindowCount requests in last N secondsPrecise enforcement
Fixed WindowCount per fixed interval (per minute)Simple (burst at boundary risk)

For anihpj: use Kong rate limiting — 100 req/s per API key. Combine JWT auth at gateway + WAF rules to block SQL injection/XSS at the edge.

📝 Chapter 32 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What is an API Gateway? What responsibilities does it handle?
Show Answer
Answer

An API Gateway is the front door for all platform traffic: routing, authentication (JWT/OAuth2), rate limiting, request transformation, TLS termination, and analytics. It centralizes cross-cutting concerns so services don't handle them individually.

Q2
What is the Kubernetes Gateway API? How does it improve on Ingress?
Show Answer
Answer

Next-gen Ingress: more expressive (header routing, traffic splitting), role-oriented (GatewayClass → Gateway → HTTPRoute), protocol-aware (HTTP/TCP/gRPC). Separates concerns: platform team creates Gateways; devs create Routes without cluster-admin.

Q3
Compare Kong, Traefik, and NGINX. Which for anihpj?
Show Answer
Answer

Kong: Full API management (plugins). Traefik: Auto-discovery, simple. NGINX: Mature, performant. For anihpj: Kong for API management features (rate limiting, JWT, analytics); Traefik for simplicity.

Q4
Where should JWT validation happen — gateway or application?
Show Answer
Answer

At the gateway — validate JWT before reaching Django. Reduces app load, centralizes auth, blocks invalid requests early. Django receives pre-authenticated requests with user context in headers.

Q5
Compare rate limiting strategies: token bucket vs sliding window.
Show Answer
Answer

Token Bucket: Tokens refill at steady rate, allows bursts. Best for most APIs. Sliding Window: Counts requests in last N seconds. More precise. For anihpj: 100 req/s per API key with token bucket.

Q6
What is Envoy Gateway? How does it relate to Gateway API?
Show Answer
Answer

Envoy Gateway is the CNCF reference implementation of Gateway API — translates Gateway API resources into Envoy config. "Batteries included" Envoy gateway: install, create resources, it handles the rest.

Q7
What is a WAF? Why integrate with your API gateway?
Show Answer
Answer

A WAF blocks attacks at HTTP layer: SQL injection, XSS, CSRF. Integration at gateway means malicious requests blocked at edge — before reaching any service. Cloud WAFs: AWS WAF, Cloudflare WAF.

Q8
What is north-south vs east-west traffic?
Show Answer
Answer

North-South: External → cluster (ingress/API gateway). East-West: Service → service (mesh, mTLS). Different patterns, different tools. Gateway handles external; service mesh handles internal.

Q9
What is the GatewayClass → Gateway → HTTPRoute model?
Show Answer
Answer

GatewayClass: Infra implementation (Envoy). Gateway: Listener config (ports, TLS). HTTPRoute: Routing rules (devs). Enables self-service routing — devs create Routes without cluster-admin access.

Q10
Design the API gateway for anihpj: routing, auth, rate limiting.
Show Answer
Answer

1. Kong API Gateway as front door. 2. Routing: /api/v1→v1, /api/v2→v2. 3. JWT auth at gateway. 4. Rate limit: 100 req/s per key. 5. TLS via cert-manager. 6. Prometheus metrics from Kong. All GitOps-managed via Kong declarative config in Git.

Chapter 33: Database and Data Management on Kubernetes

Running databases on K8s was once considered risky — not anymore. Operators automate DBA tasks (backups, failover, scaling), StatefulSets provide stable identity and persistent storage, and Velero handles disaster recovery. This chapter covers running PostgreSQL, Redis, and other stateful workloads on your platform.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~2 hours 🗄️ Data 🗄️ Cross-Domain

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ StatefulSets: stable identity, persistent volumes, headless services
  • ✅ K8s Operators: CloudNativePG, Percona, Redis Operator
  • ✅ Backup & DR: Velero, pg_dump, etcd backup, RPO/RTO
  • ✅ Data migration: blue/green, expand-contract, schema versioning
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
🗄️ Cross-Domain Integrated

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — Databases on K8s

Expect questions about stateful workloads on K8s: "How would you deploy PostgreSQL for anihpj?" Know StatefulSets (stable identity, VolumeClaimTemplates), operators (CloudNativePG for PostgreSQL), Velero for backup/DR, and RPO/RTO definitions. Also know migration strategies: blue/green (minimal downtime) and expand-contract (zero downtime). The exam tests your ability to run databases reliably on K8s — not fear them.

33.1 Stateful Workloads on K8s — StatefulSets

StatefulSets provide: stable pod identity (pod-0, pod-1 — not random), persistent storage per pod (VolumeClaimTemplates), ordered deployment (0, then 1, then 2), and stable network identity via headless services. A headless service (clusterIP: None) doesn't load-balance — it returns individual pod IPs via DNS, giving each pod a stable address like postgresql-0.anihpj.svc.cluster.local. This is how PostgreSQL replicas find the primary — by its stable DNS name, not a random pod IP.

# PostgreSQL StatefulSet for anihpj
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: StatefulSet
metadata:
  name: postgresql
spec:
  serviceName: postgresql  # Headless service
  replicas: 3
  volumeClaimTemplates:
    - metadata:
        name: data
      spec:
        accessModes: ["ReadWriteOnce"]
        resources:
          requests:
            storage: 100Gi
  template:
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: postgresql
          image: postgres:16
          volumeMounts:
            - name: data
              mountPath: /var/lib/postgresql/data

33.2 Kubernetes Operators for Databases

OperatorDatabaseKey Features
CloudNativePGPostgreSQLAuto-failover, backups, scaling, K8s-native
Percona OperatorMySQL, MongoDB, PostgreSQLMulti-DB, enterprise features
Redis OperatorRedisCluster mgmt, sentinel, persistence
StackGresPostgreSQL150+ extensions, advanced configs
💡 Why Operators Matter

Operators encode DBA knowledge into software. CloudNativePG handles: automated failover, continuous WAL archiving to S3, scaling (add replicas), and rolling upgrades. Deploy the operator; it manages the database lifecycle automatically.

33.3 Backup and Disaster Recovery

ToolWhat It Backs UpUse Case
VeleroK8s resources + PV snapshotsFull cluster DR, namespace restore
pg_dump / WALPostgreSQL logical/physical backupPoint-in-time recovery for DB
etcd snapshotCluster state (all K8s objects)Control plane DR

RPO: Max data loss acceptable (5 min for anihpj). RTO: Max recovery time (30 min — automated failover). Balance business needs vs infrastructure cost.

33.4 Data Migration Strategies

StrategyHow It WorksDowntime
Blue/GreenNew DB syncs from old; cut over when readyMinimal (seconds)
Expand-ContractAdd columns, dual-write, migrate, remove oldZero downtime
Schema VersioningDjango migrations: versioned, reversible, CI testedControlled rollout
⚠️ Migration Safety Checklist

Every DB migration must be: (1) backward-compatible, (2) tested in CI (migrate + rollback), (3) staged (staging first, then production), (4) reversible (have a rollback plan). For Django: python manage.py migrate — always test in staging first.

📝 Chapter 33 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What is a StatefulSet? How does it differ from a Deployment?
Show Answer
Answer

StatefulSet: Stable pod identity (pod-0), persistent storage per pod (VolumeClaimTemplates), ordered scaling, stable DNS. Deployment: Random names, shared storage, parallel scaling. StatefulSet for databases; Deployment for stateless apps.

Q2
What is a K8s Operator? Why use CloudNativePG for PostgreSQL?
Show Answer
Answer

An operator encodes DBA knowledge into software. CloudNativePG handles: automated failover, continuous WAL backups, scaling replicas, rolling upgrades. Deploy once; it manages the DB lifecycle. No manual DBA intervention needed for routine operations.

Q3
What is Velero? How does it enable disaster recovery?
Show Answer
Answer

Velero backs up K8s resources (JSON to S3) and PV snapshots. If cluster is destroyed, Velero restores all objects + data. Key for DR: namespace-level or full-cluster restore. Test restore procedures quarterly.

Q4
What are RPO and RTO? What values for anihpj?
Show Answer
Answer

RPO: Max data loss (5 min — continuous WAL). RTO: Max recovery time (30 min — auto-failover). Lower = higher cost. Balance business needs vs investment.

Q5
What is blue/green database migration?
Show Answer
Answer

Create new DB (green) synced from old (blue). When caught up, switch apps to green. Rollback: switch back. Downtime: seconds. Requires replication or dual-write capability.

Q6
What is expand-contract migration? Why zero-downtime?
Show Answer
Answer

Expand: add column. Dual-write to both. Backfill. Contract: remove old column. Each step backward-compatible — old code ignores new; new code writes to both. Zero downtime achieved.

Q7
How do you backup and restore etcd?
Show Answer
Answer

Backup: etcdctl snapshot save. Restore: etcdctl snapshot restore. etcd stores ALL K8s cluster state. Automate backup via cron, store in S3, test restore quarterly.

Q8
What safety checks should every DB migration pass?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Backward compatible. 2. CI tested (migrate+rollback). 3. Staging first. 4. Reversible (rollback plan). 5. No locking (use CONCURRENTLY for indexes).

Q9
What is a VolumeClaimTemplate? How does it differ from a regular PVC?
Show Answer
Answer

A VolumeClaimTemplate auto-creates a PVC for each pod in a StatefulSet. pod-0 gets pvc-data-postgresql-0, pod-1 gets pvc-data-postgresql-1, etc. Regular PVCs are shared. VCT ensures each StatefulSet pod has its own persistent volume — essential for databases.

Q10
Design the DB architecture for anihpj: PostgreSQL, backups, DR.
Show Answer
Answer

1. CloudNativePG operator. 2. 3-node StatefulSet (1 primary + 2 replicas). 3. Continuous WAL archiving to S3 (RPO: 5min). 4. Velero nightly (K8s resources + PV snapshots). 5. Auto-failover via operator (RTO: 30min). 6. Weekly pg_dump. 7. Quarterly DR test: Velero restore to fresh cluster.

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — Exam Strategy

The exam is performance-based, not multiple choice. You must actually configure systems in a live terminal. Practice with real clusters — reading about kubectl isn't enough. Key strategies: scan all tasks first, complete easy tasks first for confidence and points, flag and skip hard tasks (6 min max per task), and verify your work before moving on. The 12-week study plan aligns with domain weights — spend 25% of your time on GitOps and APIs (50% of the exam) and 15% on Architecture and Security.

💡 Time Management is Everything

2 hours ÷ ~15-20 tasks = 6-8 minutes per task. If you're stuck after 5 minutes, FLAG IT and move on. The exam is not linear — some tasks are prerequisites for others, but most are independent. Completing 12 tasks perfectly is better than attempting 18 and rushing. Aim for quality over quantity.

Chapter 35: Troubleshooting and Incident Response

When the platform breaks at 3 AM, you don't panic — you follow a systematic process. Troubleshooting is the most tested skill on the CNPE exam: diagnosing pod failures, network issues, certificate problems, and performance degradation. This chapter gives you the methodology and the playbook.

🔴 Exam Focus ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 🔧 Troubleshooting 📋 ALL DOMAINS

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Systematic troubleshooting methodology
  • ✅ Common failure scenarios and their fixes
  • ✅ Incident response: severity levels, communication, postmortems
  • ✅ Platform resilience patterns
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
📋 ALL 5 DOMAINS 100%

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — Troubleshooting

Troubleshooting is heavily tested — expect scenarios like: "Pod is CrashLoopBackOff — diagnose and fix" or "Service has no endpoints — why and how do you resolve it?" Master these commands: kubectl describe, kubectl logs --previous, kubectl get events. Know the 5-step methodology (Observe→Isolate→Diagnose→Fix→Learn) and the 3 incident severity levels (SEV1/2/3). The exam tests your ability to quickly identify root causes — not just restart things and hope.

35.1 Troubleshooting Methodology

👀OBSERVE
→
🎯ISOLATE
→
🔬DIAGNOSE
→
🔧FIX
→
📝LEARN
StepActionCommands
ObserveWhat's broken? Gather symptomskubectl get pods, events, describe
IsolateNarrow the scope — which component?kubectl logs, top, exec
DiagnoseFind root cause — don't fix symptomsCheck logs, metrics, traces, configs
FixApply the minimal fix to restore servicekubectl edit, apply, rollout restart

35.2 Common Failure Scenarios

SymptomLikely CauseFirst Command
Pod CrashLoopBackOffApp crash, OOMKilled, bad configkubectl logs <pod> --previous
Pod PendingNo nodes available, resource shortagekubectl describe pod <pod>
Service unreachableWrong selector, port mismatchkubectl get endpoints <svc>
ImagePullBackOffWrong image name/tag, registry authkubectl describe pod

35.3 Incident Response

SeverityDefinitionResponse
SEV1 — CriticalComplete platform outage, users can't access anihpjWar room, all hands, incident commander
SEV2 — MajorDegraded service, significant user impactOn-call engineer + escalation
SEV3 — MinorPartial issue, workaround existsOn-call during business hours

Postmortems are blameless — focus on "what happened, why, how do we prevent it?" Not "who caused it?" Every SEV1/SEV2 gets a written postmortem with action items tracked to completion.

35.4 Platform Resilience Patterns

PatternHow It WorksExample
Circuit BreakerStop calling failing service, fail fastIstio DestinationRule outlier detection
Retry with BackoffRetry failed requests with increasing delaysIstio VirtualService retry policy
BulkheadIsolate resources per service (no shared fate)Separate DB connection pools per service
Health ChecksAuto-detect and remove unhealthy instancesK8s liveness + readiness probes
Graceful DegradationServe degraded but functional experienceCache stale data when DB is down

📝 Chapter 35 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
What is the 5-step troubleshooting methodology?
Show Answer
Answer

Observe → Isolate → Diagnose → Fix → Learn. Observe symptoms without jumping to conclusions. Isolate to the specific component. Diagnose root cause (not symptom). Apply minimal fix. Learn by writing postmortems and updating monitoring to prevent recurrence.

Q2
Pod is in CrashLoopBackOff. What are your first 3 commands?
Show Answer
Answer

1. kubectl describe pod — check events (OOMKilled? Image pull error?). 2. kubectl logs <pod> --previous — see crash logs from previous instance. 3. kubectl get events --sort-by='.lastTimestamp' — cluster-level events. These three diagnose 90% of CrashLoopBackOff cases.

Q3
What's the difference between SEV1, SEV2, and SEV3 incidents?
Show Answer
Answer

SEV1: Complete outage, all hands on deck. SEV2: Degraded but functional, on-call + escalation. SEV3: Minor issue, workaround exists, business hours. Clear severity definitions prevent both over-reaction and under-reaction.

Q4
What is a blameless postmortem? Why is it important?
Show Answer
Answer

A postmortem analyzes what happened and how to prevent it — without blaming individuals. Key: "the system failed, not the person." This creates psychological safety, encourages honest incident reporting, and leads to real systemic fixes rather than scapegoating. Every SEV1/SEV2 gets one.

Q5
What are 5 platform resilience patterns?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Circuit Breaker: Stop calling failing services. 2. Retry with Backoff: Retry with increasing delays. 3. Bulkhead: Isolate resources per service. 4. Health Checks: Auto-remove unhealthy instances. 5. Graceful Degradation: Serve stale/cached data when backend is down.

Q6
How do you debug "Service has no endpoints"?
Show Answer
Answer

1. kubectl get endpoints <svc> — empty list confirms no endpoints. 2. Check Service selector matches pod labels. 3. Check pods are Running and Ready (readiness probe passes). 4. Check port names match between Service and pod containerPort. Most common cause: label selector mismatch.

Q7
What is the circuit breaker pattern? How does Istio implement it?
Show Answer
Answer

Circuit breaker stops sending traffic to failing backends to prevent cascading failures. Istio implements via DestinationRule outlierDetection: if PostgreSQL returns 5 consecutive 5xx errors, Istio ejects it for 60s. During ejection, traffic goes to healthy instances or fails fast.

Q8
Pod stuck in Pending state. What do you check?
Show Answer
Answer

1. kubectl describe pod — check Events for scheduler messages. 2. Is it "0/nodes available"? → Check node resources (CPU/memory), taints/tolerations, nodeSelectors. 3. PVCs pending? → Check storage class and PV availability. 4. Resource quota exceeded? → Check namespace ResourceQuota.

Q9
What's in a good incident postmortem? 5 essential sections.
Show Answer
Answer

1. Timeline: What happened, when. 2. Impact: Users affected, duration, data loss. 3. Root Cause: The "why" behind the incident. 4. Resolution: How it was fixed. 5. Action Items: Specific, assigned, time-bound tasks to prevent recurrence.

Q10
anihpj is down at 3AM. Walk through your incident response from alert to resolution.
Show Answer
Answer

1. Acknowledge PagerDuty alert (SEV1). 2. Open war room (Slack #incidents). 3. Observe: Grafana shows 0 requests → kubectl shows pods CrashLoopBackOff. 4. Isolate: kubectl logs --previous → "table 'jobs' doesn't exist". 5. Diagnose: Django migration failed. 6. Fix: Rollback to last known good deployment via ArgoCD. 7. Verify: RED dashboard returns green. 8. Learn: Postmortem — add migration pre-check to CI.

Chapter 36: Real-World Platform Engineering Case Studies

Theory meets practice. This chapter walks through three complete platform engineering scenarios — building the anihpj IDP from scratch, migrating a legacy platform, and scaling to handle 10× traffic. These case studies integrate everything you've learned across all 5 CNPE domains.

🔴 Exam Focus ⏱️ ~1 hour 📖 Case Studies 📋 ALL DOMAINS

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Building an IDP from scratch: architecture decisions, tool selection, implementation
  • ✅ Platform migration: moving from legacy to modern platform
  • ✅ Scaling: handling 10× traffic growth without architectural collapse
🎓 CNPE Domain Relevance
📋 ALL 5 DOMAINS 100%

🎓 CNPE Exam Tip — Case Studies

The CNPE exam may include integrated scenario tasks that span multiple domains: "Deploy anihpj with GitOps, monitoring, and security policies." These case studies prepare you for those multi-domain tasks. Know how to select tools for specific scenarios (ArgoCD for GitOps, Kyverno for policy), how to plan migrations (replication → verify → cutover → monitor), and how to scale platforms (HPA + CA + caching + CDN). Think holistically — the exam tests your ability to architect complete solutions, not isolated commands.

36.1 Building the anihpj IDP — From Zero to Platform

Scenario: anihpj is a Django job board with 5 developers. Deployments are manual (git pull + restart on a single VPS). The CTO wants a proper platform. Your task: build an Internal Developer Platform in 3 months.

MonthFocusKey Deliverables
Month 1FoundationEKS cluster (Tofu), CI/CD (GitHub Actions), ArgoCD, containerize anihpj
Month 2Platform ServicesMonitoring (Prometheus+Grafana), logging (Loki), cert-manager, Kyverno policies
Month 3Developer ExperienceBackstage portal, service catalog, software templates, self-service DB (Crossplane)
📖 Key Architecture Decisions

ArgoCD over Flux: Rich UI, ApplicationSets for future multi-cluster. Kyverno over OPA: YAML-native, simpler for 5-person team. Crossplane: Self-service databases without manual tickets. Backstage: Single pane of glass — catalog, docs, templates. Developers now deploy with git push — no SSH, no manual steps. Deployment time: from 2 days to 15 minutes.

36.2 Platform Migration — Legacy to Modern

Scenario: anihpj moves from single VPS running Docker Compose to the K8s IDP. PostgreSQL data must migrate with zero data loss and minimal downtime.

StepActionRisk
1Deploy CloudNativePG operator on K8s🟢 Low
2Create PostgreSQL cluster (3 nodes, streaming replication)🟢 Low
3Set up logical replication from VPS → K8s Postgres🟡 Medium
4Verify data consistency (row counts, checksums, sample queries)🟢 Low
5Cutover: stop VPS app → promote K8s Postgres → deploy anihpj on K8s (5 min downtime)🔴 High
6Monitor 24h, snapshot VPS, decommission after 48h green🟢 Low

36.3 Scaling anihpj — Handling 10× Traffic Growth

Scenario: anihpj goes viral. Traffic: 100 req/s → 1,000 req/s. Platform must scale without manual intervention.

ActionToolImpact
Scale pods horizontallyHPA (CPU > 70% → 3→15 pods)Handles spike immediately
Add read replicasCloudNativePG (add 2 replicas)Offloads 90% of DB reads
Enable Redis cachingCache job listings (5 min TTL)Reduces DB queries by 70%
Scale nodesCluster Autoscaler (3→10 nodes)Provides compute capacity
CDN static assetsCloudFront + S3Offloads 100% of static traffic

Within 5 minutes: HPA scaled pods, CA added nodes, Redis was already caching. Users experienced no slowdown. The platform handled 10× traffic without manual intervention — because auto-scaling was configured before it was needed.

📝 Chapter 36 — CNPE Practice Questions

Q1
You're building anihpj IDP from scratch. What's your tool selection?
Show Answer
Answer

EKS via Tofu, GitHub Actions CI, ArgoCD GitOps, Prometheus+Grafana monitoring, Loki+Tempo logs+traces, Kyverno policy, cert-manager TLS, Crossplane self-service DB, Backstage portal. Each chosen for K8s-native integration and CNCF ecosystem alignment.

Q2
How would you migrate PostgreSQL from VPS to K8s with minimal downtime?
Show Answer
Answer

Deploy CloudNativePG. Set up logical replication VPS→K8s. Verify data consistency. Scheduled cutover: stop VPS app, promote K8s PG, deploy on K8s (5 min downtime). Monitor 48h, keep VPS snapshot for 30 days as safety net. Zero data loss; minimal downtime.

Q3
Traffic spikes 10×. What's your first 5 actions?
Show Answer
Answer

1. HPA scales pods (auto). 2. CA adds nodes (auto). 3. Add read replicas. 4. Enable Redis caching. 5. CDN static assets. If HPA+CA pre-configured, steps 1-2 are automatic — zero manual intervention. This is why proactive auto-scaling matters.

Q4
What are the 3 phases of building the anihpj IDP?
Show Answer
Answer

Month 1 (Foundation): Cluster, CI/CD, ArgoCD. Month 2 (Platform Services): Monitoring, logging, security. Month 3 (Developer Experience): Backstage, catalog, templates, self-service DB. Build infrastructure first; developer experience comes last.

Q5
How do you verify data consistency during migration?
Show Answer
Answer

Compare row counts per table. Run checksum queries. Sample random rows. Verify replication lag near zero pre-cutover. Run app-level smoke tests against new DB before cutting over traffic.

Q6
What's most critical to configure BEFORE a traffic spike?
Show Answer
Answer

HPA (Horizontal Pod Autoscaler) with proper min/max and targets. Without it: pods overload → slow → users leave. With it: auto-scale 3→15 pods → users don't notice. Configure day 1, not during the incident.

Q7
Why Backstage for the developer portal?
Show Answer
Answer

Service catalog (discover all services), software templates (scaffold new services), TechDocs (docs alongside code), plugin ecosystem (K8s, ArgoCD, Grafana). Single pane of glass — no jumping between 10 tools.

Q8
How do you decommission old VPS safely after migration?
Show Answer
Answer

Keep VPS 48h post-migration (rollback target). Monitor K8s: errors, latency, DB. All green after 48h: snapshot VPS, terminate. Keep snapshot 30 days. Never decommission immediately — always have a rollback window.

Q9
What DORA metrics improved after deploying the IDP?
Show Answer
Answer

Deployment Frequency: weekly→multiple/day. Lead Time: 2 days→15 min. Change Failure Rate: 20%→5%. MTTR: 2 hours→10 min. These metrics prove the platform's value quantitatively.

Q10
How do you measure the success of the anihpj IDP?
Show Answer
Answer

Adoption (all 5 devs using it?), DORA velocity metrics, SLO compliance, Developer NPS (would they recommend?), cost vs previous VPS, security posture. A successful platform is measured by developer outcomes — not just infrastructure uptime.

A kubectl Command Reference

Why this matters for CNPE: The CNPE exam expects fluency with kubectl — you'll need to debug, inspect, and modify resources under time pressure. Master these commands so they become muscle memory. Pro tip: The exam terminal has kubectl with bash completion enabled — USE IT!

Essential Aliases & Setup (Do This First!)

# Add to ~/.bashrc on exam day — saves 30% of your typing time
alias k=kubectl
alias kd="kubectl describe"
alias kg="kubectl get"
alias ka="kubectl apply -f"
alias kn="kubectl config set-context --current --namespace"
source <(kubectl completion bash)  # Tab completion is a LIFESAVER
export do="--dry-run=client -o yaml"  # k create deploy nginx --image=nginx $do

JSONPath & Output Formatting (Exam Essential!)

# Get all container images across all pods
kubectl get pods -A -o jsonpath='{.items[*].spec.containers[*].image}'

# Get node IPs sorted
kubectl get nodes -o jsonpath='{.items[*].status.addresses[?(@.type=="InternalIP")].address}'

# Custom columns (most useful exam trick!)
kubectl get pods -o custom-columns=NAME:.metadata.name,NODE:.spec.nodeName,STATUS:.status.phase

# Sort by restart count
kubectl get pods --sort-by='.status.containerStatuses[0].restartCount'

DRY-RUN PATTERN (Your Best Friend in the Exam):
k create deploy nginx --image=nginx --dry-run=client -o yaml > deploy.yaml
→ Generates valid YAML instantly — NEVER write YAML from scratch!

CNPE Exam Strategy: 90% of exam YAML can be generated with kubectl create ... --dry-run=client -o yaml. Practice this until it's automatic. For complex resources like Ingress or NetworkPolicy, memorize the kubectl explain RESOURCE command — it shows you the full spec with descriptions.

kubectl explain — Your Built-in Documentation

# Show full resource spec with field descriptions
kubectl explain deployment.spec.template.spec.containers

# Show recursively (every nested field!)
kubectl explain pod.spec --recursive

# Quick check: what fields does Ingress have?
kubectl explain ingress.spec.rules.http.paths

# EXAM TIP: If you forget a field name, just kubectl explain it!

B Helm Command Reference

What is Helm? Helm is the Kubernetes package manager. Think of it as "apt/yum for K8s" — it packages YAML into reusable, versioned Charts with templating via Go templates. CNPE exam expects you to know Helm for platform component deployment.

Helm Chart Structure (anihpj Example)

anihpj-chart/
├── Chart.yaml          # Chart metadata (name: anihpj, version: 1.0.0, appVersion: "2.1")
├── values.yaml         # Default configuration values
├── values-dev.yaml     # Dev overrides: replicas=1, debug=true
├── values-prod.yaml    # Production: replicas=5, debug=false, TLS on
├── templates/          # Kubernetes YAML templates (Go template syntax)
│   ├── deployment.yaml
│   ├── service.yaml
│   ├── hpa.yaml
│   ├── ingress.yaml
│   ├── configmap.yaml
│   ├── _helpers.tpl    # Reusable template functions (labels, names)
│   └── NOTES.txt       # Post-install instructions shown to user

values.yaml Example

# anihpj default values
replicaCount: 3
image:
  repository: registry.io/anihpj
  tag: "v2.1.0"
  pullPolicy: IfNotPresent
service:
  type: ClusterIP
  port: 8000
ingress:
  enabled: true
  host: anihpj.example.com
  tls: true
resources:
  requests:
    cpu: 250m
    memory: 256Mi
  limits:
    cpu: 500m
    memory: 512Mi
autoscaling:
  enabled: true
  minReplicas: 3
  maxReplicas: 15
  targetCPU: 70
